An example of\n\nkinetic adsorptive separation is the separation of a CO2/CH4 mixture using a carbon\n\n19\n\nPage 19 of 95\n\nmolecular sieve, in which the separation is achieved by the large difference in\n\ndiffusion rates between the two components (Diagne et al., 1996). In fact, because no accumulation of\n\nadsorbates is allowed, the film transfer and macropore diffusion can be treated as\n\n21\n\nPage 21 of 95\n\nsequential steps, and mass conservation assumption is applicable. It can be\n\nexpressed as the following equation (Jin et al., 2006; LeVan et al., 1999):\n\n∂ q i ∂ t = 3 R k p fi ( c i - c pi ( t , R p ) Ac) = 3 R p ( , ) cepted Manuscript\n\n22 ε p D pi ∂ c pi ∂\n\nR t R\n\np\n\n(10)\n\nwhere kfi is the external film mass transfer coefficient, Rp is the macroparticle\n\nradius, cpi is the adsorbate concentration in the macropore, which is a function of\n\nradial position in the particle, εp is the adsorbent porosity, D pi is the macropore\n\ndiffusivity, and R is the distance along the macroparticle radius.\n\nThe external film mass transfer coefficient, kfi, around the particles can be\n\nestimated from the following correlation, which is applicable over a wide range of\n\nconditions (Wakao and Funazkri, 1978):\n\nSh = 2 k fi R p\n\nD mi\n\n\\= 2 + 1.1 Sc 1/3 Re 0.6 (11) In most gas adsorption studies, the intraparticle diffusional resistance is normally\n\nmuch greater than the external fluid film resistance (intraparticle transport of the\n\nadsorbate is the slower step). Therefore, it is reasonable to assume negligible gas-\n\nside resistance and simulate adsorption systems based on a diffusion model (Carta\n\nand Cincotti, 1998; Farooq et al., 2001; Raghavan et al., 1985). An accurate kinetic\n\nPage 22 of 95\n\nmodel that accounts for the intraparticle diffusional resistances can provide reliable\n\nsimulations of kinetically controlled PSA processes. Indeed, neglecting\n\nintraparticle mass transfer kinetics leads to significant deviations from the exact\n\nsolution (Chahbani and Tondeur, 2000).\n\n3.2.2.2. Macropore diffusional resistance\n\nDiffusion in sufficiently large pores (macro- and mesopores) such that the\n\ndiffusing molecules escape from the force field of the adsorbent surface is often\n\nreferred to as macropore diffusion (or pore diffusion). Depending on the relative\n\nmagnitude of the pore diameter and the mean free path of the adsorbate molecules,\n\ntransport in a macropore can occur by different mechanisms (Karger and Ruthven,\n\n1992). For gas phase diffusion in small pores at low pressure, when the molecular\n\nmean free path is much greater than the pore diameter, Knudsen diffusion\n\ndominates the transport mechanism. In this case, the resistance to mass transfer\n\nmainly arises from collisions between the diffusing molecules and the pore wall.\n\nThe Knudsen diffusivity (Dki) is independent of pressure and varies only weakly\n\nwith temperature as follows (Karger and Ruthven, 1992; Ruthven, 1984; Suzuki,\n\n1990; Yang, 1987): ki 9700 p Accepted Manuscript D = r T M (12)\n\n23\n\nPage 23 of 95\n\nwhere rp is the mean macropore radius in cm, T is the temperature, and M is the\n\nmolecular weight of the adsorbate.\n\nBy diameter, and Ribeiro the case main comparable molecular macropore 2008; where D 1 pi contrast, Stefan–Maxwell τ= can of diffusional Yang, τ ( molecular D et be is 1 ki the al., diffusion the + diffusivity estimated significance, when D 1987): 1 bulk mi 2008b; Apore ) resistance. diffusion, the molecular ctortuosity constitute equation from Ruthven, (Dmolecular cp) and ethe is For the diffusion obtained factor.\n\nthus pfor the Chapman-Enskog 1984; collisions the mean multi-component trate-controlling the eintermediate Sherwood combined from will free d between be path the Mthe Bosanquet is effects et case, equation dominant mechanism. small al., diffusing systems aboth 1975) nof relative (Bird the equation umechanisms (Suzuki, transport molecules for Knudsen The set to binary cal., the effective (Grande 1990). mechanism r2002; pore are and systems ipare the In the of et t the\n\nal.,\n\n(13) or\n\nAs discussed above, in macropore diffusion, transport occurs within the fluid-filled\n\npores inside the particle (LeVan et al., 1999; Ruthven, 1984). The amplitude of the temperature variation depends primarily on the heat\n\nof adsorption, the throughput, and the heat transfer characteristics of the packed\n\nadsorbed column (Farooq et al., 1988; Ruthven, 1984). The generated heat is\n\n43\n\nPage 43 of 95\n\nconducted to the surface of the particles and then is transferred to the gas phase by\n\na convection mechanism. To take into account the energy transfer, three different\n\ncontrol volumes may be considered: gas, solid, and column wall.\n\n3.3. 1. Gas phase energy balance\n\nThe variation of gas temperature with respect to time arises from the transfer of\n\nenergy due the axial conductive solid-phase heat flux to the gas phase along the z\n\ndirection as well as the energy transfer by convection through the bed due to the\n\nbulk movement of gas. Therefore, the energy balance for the gas phase, which\n\nincludes the heat transfer between the flowing fluid and the surface of the solid\n\nadsorbent through a laminar film and the energy transferred to the wall of the\n\ncolumn, may be written as the following (Rezaei and Grahn, 2012; Ruthven,\n\n1984): - λ L 2 2\n\nAint ∂ T g ∂ z + ρ g C g ( ccepted Manuscript\n\n44 ∂ uT g ) ∂ z + ρ g C g ∂ T g ∂\n\nt + ( 1 - ε b ε b ) ha f s ( T g - T s ) + ε 4 b\n\nd h w\n\n( T g - T w ) = 0 (35) The above equation is used to find the distribution of gas temperature along the\n\nbed, where Tg represents the bulk gas temperature; Cg is the heat capacity of the\n\ngas; ρg is the bulk density of the gas; hf denotes the film heat transfer coefficient\n\nbetween the gas and the adsorbent; as expresses the ratio of the particle external\n\nPage 44 of 95\n\nsurface area to volume; Ts denotes the solid temperature; hw is the internal\n\nconvective heat transfer coefficient between the gas and the column wall; dint is the\n\ncolumn internal diameter; Tw denotes the wall temperature; and λL is the effective\n\naxial heat dispersion, which can be estimated using the following correlation\n\n(Grande and Rodrigues, 2005; Wakao and Funazkri, 1978; Yang, 1987):\n\nλLkg = 7 + 0.5 (36) where number. Conventional gas separation processes using\n\npacked beds of beads or granules suffer predominantly from high pressure drop\n\nand mass transfer resistance when higher throughputs are required, leading to\n\nlower productivity and recovery and higher power consumption (Rezaei and\n\nWebley, 2010). In order to overcome these drawbacks, novel adsorbent structures\n\nsuch as monoliths, laminates, and foams structures are considered as alternate\n\ncandidates for the replacement of conventional adsorbent configurations as they\n\noffer the advantage of allowing increased throughput and reduced pressure drops.\n\nIn recent years, monolithic adsorbents have garnered considerable attention for\n\nCO2 capture from the gas streams (Brandani et al., 2004; Grande and Rodrigues,\n\n2008; Mosca et al., 2008; Ribeiro et al., 2008b). A detailed review of these\n\nalternate structures and their merits was recently conducted by Rezaei and Webley\n\n(2010).\n\nThe pressure drop of the monolithic and laminate structures can be expressed by\n\nthe following equation (Rezaei and Webley, 2009):\n\n32 2(48)\n\nb ∆ L P =\n\nd\n\nε μu Accepted Manuscript\n\nwhere d is the channel dimension.\n\n51\n\nPage 51 of 95\n\nIn the case of foam adsorbents, the pressure drop can be estimated using the\n\nequation presented by Richardson et al.   "fileContent": "Mathematical modeling and numerical simulation of sulfamethoxazole adsorption onto sugarcane bagasse in a fixed-bed column \n\n|  |\n| :-: |\n| [**Page 1**]() |\n\nMathematical modeling and numerical simulation of\n\nsulfamethoxazole adsorption onto sugarcane bagasse in a fixed-bed\n\ncolumn\n\nDiego Juela [a](#1), Mayra Vera [b](#1), Christian Cruzat [b](#1), Ximena Alvarez [b](#1), Eulalia Vanegas [b](#1), [\\*](#1)\n\na *Chemical Engineering, Faculty of Chemical Sciences, University of Cuenca, 010203, Cuenca, Ecuador*\n\nb *Center for Environmental Studies, Department of Applied Chemistry and Production Systems, Faculty of Chemical Sciences, University of Cuenca, 010203,*\n\n*Cuenca, Ecuador*\n\nh i g h l i g h t s\n\ng r a p h i c a l a b s t r a c t\n\nAdsorption of sulfamethoxazole with\n\nsugarcane bagasse was investigated.\n\nA new mechanistic model was\n\ndeveloped and solved numerically.\n\nEight analytical models were used to\n\nfit the breakthrough data.\n\nThe mechanistic model predicted\n\nbreakthrough\n\ncurves\n\nwith\n\ngreat\n\naccuracy.\n\nThe Log-Gompertz model correlated\n\nwell with asymmetric breakthrough\n\ncurves.\n\na r t i c l e i n f o\n\n*Article history:*\n\nReceived 22 January 2021\n\nReceived in revised form\n\n19 April 2021\n\nAccepted 20 April 2021\n\nAvailable online 27 April 2021\n\nHandling Editor: Yongmei Li\n\n*Keywords:*\n\nAnalytical models\n\nFixed-bed column\n\nDynamic simulation\n\nWastewater treatment\n\nBiosorption\n\nBreakthrough curve\n\na b s t r a c t\n\nHaving rigorous mathematical models is essential for the design and scaling of adsorption columns. In\n\nthis study, the dynamic behavior of the sulfamethoxazole adsorption on sugarcane bagasse was studied\n\nand compared using analytical models and a theoretical mechanistic model. Initially, fixed-bed column\n\ntests were carried out at different flow rates and bed heights. Then, the experimental data were fitted\n\nwith the most widely used analytical kinetic models, and their fit and fixed-bed parameters were\n\ncompared with the mechanistic model. Of all analytical models analyzed, the Log-Gompertz model was\n\nthe one that had the best agreed with experimental data. Although some analytical models fitted the\n\nexperimental data accurately, their usefulness was questionable. Their parameters did not show a clear\n\nrelationship with the change in operating conditions, and in certain cases had different behavior from\n\nthat observed in experimentation. Conversely, the mechanistic model not only predicted the break-\n\nthrough curves with great accuracy in the initial and transition stage (R2 \\\u003e 0.92; SSE \\\u003c 0.06), but it also\n\nestimated relevant parameters. Additionally, the effects of the global mass transfer coefficient (*Ki*) and\n\nthe axial dispersion coefficient (*Dz*) on breakthrough curves were studied using the mechanistic model.\n\nIncreasing *Ki* increased the slope of the breakthrough curves with a faster adsorption rate. Similarly, high\n\nvalues of *Dz* produced lower adsorption capacities of the adsorbent; and it was established that the axial\n\ndispersion is relevant in SMX adsorption on SB. The theoretical model presented can be used for the\n\ndesign, scaling, and optimization of adsorption columns.\n\n© 2021 Elsevier Ltd. All rights reserved.\n\n\\* Corresponding author. University of Cuenca, Av. 12 de Abril, 010203, Cuenca,\n\nEcuador.\n\n*E-mail address:* [eulalia.vanegas@ucuenca.edu.ec ](mailto:eulalia.vanegas@ucuenca.edu.ec)(E. Vanegas).\n\nContents lists available at [ScienceDirect](http://www.sciencedirect.com/science/journal/00456535)\n\nChemosphere\n\njournal homepage: [www.elsevier.com/locate/chemosphere](http://www.elsevier.com/locate/chemosphere)\n\n\u003chttps://doi.org/10.1016/j.chemosphere.2021.130687\u003e\n\n0045-6535/© 2021 Elsevier Ltd. All rights reserved.\n\n[Chemosphere 280 (2021) 130687](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.chemosphere.2021.130687)\n\n-----\n\n|  |\n| :-: |\n| [**Page 2**]() |\n\n**1. Introduction**\n\nSulfamethoxazole (SMX) is a bacteriostatic antibiotic, and it is\n\none of the most common sulfonamides consumed in human and\n\nveterinary medicine, especially in high-income countries ([Klein](#12)\n\n[et al., 2018](#12)). A conventional wastewater treatment plant (WWTP)\n\nis not designed to completely remove SMX residues present in\n\nwastewaters ([Sun et al., 2016](#12); [Zhang et al., 2015](#13)). In WWTPs, SMX is\n\npartially removed by microbiological degradation and adsorption\n\nin sludge ([da Silva Rodrigues et al., 2020](#11); [Ekpeghere et al., 2017](#12)). As\n\na consequence of incomplete removal of SMX, this antibiotic is one\n\nof the most commonly detected in wastewaters, groundwaters,\n\nsurface and drinking waters in concentrations ranged from ng/L to\n\nmg/L ([Wang and Wang, 2018](#12); [Wilkinson et al., 2017](#12)). The concern\n\nregarding SMX in aquatic environments is due to this antibiotic has\n\nthe ability to accumulate in aquatic organisms. SMX has a bio-\n\nconcentration factor of 2.80, which is much higher than other an-\n\ntibiotics ([Liu et al., 2018b](#12)); SMX also has a hazard ratio of 34.81 ([Liu](#12)\n\n[et al., 2018a](#12)), which allows its accumulation in fish, shrimp, and\n\nother aquatic organisms. This makes it a high-risk antibiotic for the\n\naquatic environment as well as for the food chain. Besides, many\n\nstudies have reported some toxicity effects of SMX on aquatic or-\n\nganisms. For instance, in microalgae, it has been shown that SMX\n\ndestroys cell wall synthesis and disrupts photosynthesis pathways\n\n([V€](#12)alitalo et al., 2017); it leads to a general decrease in the utilization\n\nof the carbon source in the periphyton ([Johansson et al., 2014](#12)). In\n\nbioluminescent bacteria such as *Vibrio* fi*scheri*, SMX affects their\n\nenzymatic system and therefore reduces their bioluminescence by\n\n50% ([Prasannamedha and Kumar, 2020](#12)). All of these effects occur\n\nwhen aquatic organisms are exposed to concentrations in the mg/L\n\nrange, so SMX levels found in surface and drinking waters are not\n\nhigh enough to affect human and animal health. However, due to\n\nthe frequent, extensive, and uninterrupted use of these types of\n\ndrugs, these very low concentrations can pose a threat to the\n\ndevelopment of antibiotic-resistant bacteria.\n\nAdsorption is a low-cost and efficient technology that can be\n\nused to remove SMX and other antibiotics from wastewaters. Ma-\n\nterials like activated carbon ([Serna-Carrizales et al., 2020](#12)), biochar\n\n([Zeng et al., 2021](#13)), carbon nanotubes ([Chen et al., 2017](#11)), graphene\n\nand its derivates ([Masri et al., 2020](#12)), metal-organic frameworks ([Yu](#13)\n\n[et al., 2020](#13)), clays and minerals ([Wu et al., 2019](#12)), and agricultural\n\nresidues ([Peñ](#12)afiel et al., 2020) has been used as adsorbents for SMX\n\nadsorption. In agro-industrial countries like Ecuador, where a large\n\nnumber of residues is produced annually, natural adsorbents are\n\nmore attractive. Sugarcane bagasse (SB) is one of these residues\n\nthat has shown high potential to remove pollutants from\n\n**Nomenclature**\n\n*Notation*\n\n*a*\n\nparameter of the Logistic model\n\n*a*0\n\nmodel (1/s)\n\n*kBA*\n\nBohart-Adams’ rate coefficient (L/mg min)\n\n*kf*\n\nexternal film mass transfer coefficient (m/s)\n\n*kL*\n\nenergy constant related to the heat of adsorption equilibrium with C0 from\n\nBatch studies (mg/g)\n\n*q*max\n\nmaximum adsorption capacity of the Langmuir\n\nisotherm (mg/g)\n\n*Q*\n\nflow rate of the solution (mL/min)\n\n*r*\n\nmodel parameter of solution (K)\n\n*tb*\n\nbreakthrough time (min)\n\n*ts*\n\nsaturation time (min)\n\nv*i*\n\ninterstitial velocity of the solution (m/s)\n\nv*s*\n\nsuperficial velocity of the solution (m/s)\n\n*Vm*\n\nmolar volume of SMX at its normal boiling point\n\n(cm3/mol)\n\n*Z*\n\nbed height (m)\n\n*z*\n\ndistance along the bed (m)\n\n*Greek Letters*\n\na\n\nvelocity of constant pattern Juela, M. Vera, C. Cruzat et al.*\n\n*Chemosphere 280 (2021) 130687*\n\n2\n\n-----\n\n|  |\n| :-: |\n| [**Page 3**]() |\n\nwastewaters ([Ahmad et al., 2018](#11); [Juela, 2020](#12); [Nardy Ribeiro et al.,](#12)\n\n[2011](#12); [Vera et al., 2018](#12)). Adsorption can be carried out in a batch\n\nprocess or in fixed-bed columns. However, the latter has greater\n\nadvantages due to its simple mode of operation, suitable for large\n\nvolumes, easy to scale up from laboratory to pilot or industrial\n\noperation, and it is applicable to the operation mode of WWTPs\n\n([Ahmed and Hameed, 2018](#11)). In order to design efficient adsorption\n\ncolumns, it is necessary to understand the main mechanisms\n\ninvolved in the adsorption of a given adsorbate-adsorbent system.\n\nMany mathematical models have been developed to elucidate\n\nthese mechanisms and predict the breakthrough curve of a given\n\nadsorption system.\n\nAnalytical kinetic models such as the Yoon-Nelson, Thomas,\n\nBohart-Adams, Modified dose-response, and Bed Depth Service\n\nTime model have been used to estimated kinetic parameters for\n\ncontinuous adsorption of SMX ([Jaria et al., 2019](#12); [Li et al., 2020](#12); [Tian](#12)\n\n[et al., 2013](#12); [Zeng et al., 2021](#13)). The Thomas model correlated well\n\nwith experimental data on the SMX adsorption with lignite acti-\n\nvated carbon, and this revealed that the SMX adsorption could be\n\ndominated by film diffusion ([Li et al., 2020](#12)). Similarly, [Jaria et al.](#12)\n\n[(2019) ](#12)used the Thomas, Yoon-Nelson, and Modified dose-\n\nresponse models to analyze the dynamic behavior of the SMX\n\nadsorption into granular activated carbon, and the modified dose-\n\nresponse model was the one that best fit the experimental data\n\nin all tests. Although these models fit with some precision to a real\n\nadsorption process, these models are not based on a mechanistic\n\napproach and ignore intraparticle diffusion resistance and axial\n\ndispersion, and all of these models require experimental break-\n\nthrough\n\ncurves to\n\nestimate\n\ntheir respective\n\nparameters\n\n([Unuabonah et al., 2019](#12)). Besides, several studies have questioned\n\nthe practical utility of classic analytical models due to their inac-\n\ncuracy in predicted fixed-bed parameters ([Dorado et al., 2014](#12); [Jaria](#12)\n\n[et al., 2019](#12); [Li et al., 2020](#12)). For instance, [Dorado et al. (2014) ](#12)found\n\nthat the adsorption capacity predicted by the modified-dose\n\nresponse model and Wolborska model were 100 times lower\n\nthan those measured, or had an unexpected behavior with oper-\n\nating conditions. Due to these disadvantages of these models, in the\n\nlast decades, several theoretical mathematical models have been\n\ndeveloped to describe the dynamic behavior of a fixed-bed\n\nadsorption column. Most of these models are composed of a\n\nmacroscopic mass conservation equation, a kinetic equation, and\n\nan equilibrium relationship or isotherm, which can be solved\n\nnumerically using computational software. These theoretical\n\nmodels take into account both film and intraparticle diffusion,\n\nhence these are more closely approximate a real adsorption process\n\nwith a higher degree of accuracy ([Unuabonah et al., 2019](#12)). Addi-\n\ntionally, a mechanistic model can estimate valuable parameters\n\nsuch as mass transfers and axial dispersion coefficients.\n\nIn this work, six most widely used analytical kinetic models (the\n\nThomas, Yoon-Nelson, Bohart-Adams, Clark, Modified dose-\n\nresponse, Wolborska models) and two recently developed models\n\n(the Gompertz and Log-Gompertz models) were applied to fit the\n\nbreakthrough experimental data under different operation condi-\n\ntions for the SMX adsorption into SB. Then, the best-fitted analyt-\n\nical model was compared with a theoretical mechanistic model,\n\nwhich considered film diffusion, intraparticle diffusion, and axial\n\ndispersion. The mechanistic model was also used to evaluate the\n\neffects of global mass transfer coefficient and axial dispersion co-\n\nefficient on the breakthrough curves. Finally, the relative impor-\n\ntance of the axial dispersion phenomenon was assessed by\n\ncomparison with other rate mechanisms.\n\n**2. Theory**\n\n*2.1. Analytical kinetic models of* fi*xed-bed column adsorption*\n\nThe biosorption process in continuous systems is a dynamic\n\nprocess since the adsorbate concentration varies with time, as well\n\nas throughout the column. This particular dynamic behavior of a\n\ncolumn packed with a biosorbent is described by its breakdown\n\ncurve. This curve can be obtained through experimentation or the\n\napplication of mathematical models. In the available literature,\n\nthere are many analytical models that have been used to predict the\n\nbreakthrough curve in liquid phase. In this study, eigth models\n\nwere used to study the performance and predict the fixed bed\n\ncolumn parameters: Yoon-Nelson, Thomas, Bohart-Adams, Wol-\n\nborska, Modified dose-response, Clark, Gompertz, and Log-\n\nGompertz models. The Bohart-Adams, Yoon-Nelson, and Thomas\n\nmodels were unified as a single model called “Logistic model” due\n\nto they are mathematically equivalent according to various studies\n\n([Bakka et al., 2020](#11); [Chu, 2020a](#11); [Dima et al., 2020](#12)). After applying\n\nthe Logistic model, the parameters of the Bohart-Adams, Yoon-\n\nNelson, and Thomas models were estimated using simple correla-\n\ntions as shown in [Fig. 1](#3). The breakthrough and linear equations of\n\nall these models are shown in [Table 1](#4).\n\n**Fig. 1.** Scheme to obtain the Bohart-Adams, Thomas, and Yoon-Nelson parameters using the Logistic model.\n\n*D. Juela, M. Vera, C. Cruzat et al.*\n\n*Chemosphere 280 (2021) 130687*\n\n3\n\n-----\n\n|  |\n| :-: |\n| [**Page 4**]() |\n\n*2.2. Approach to a theoretical mechanistic model*\n\nThe analytical models named above were compared with a\n\ntheoretical mechanistic model. This model was defined as a set of\n\nequations for the biosorbent bed layer, and it is based on the mass\n\nbalance of SMX in the liquid phase, a kinetic model, and an equi-\n\nlibrium model.\n\nThe general mass balance equation for the transport and\n\nadsorption of diluted species in porous media when the pore space\n\nis primarily filled with liquid but also contains pockets or immobile\n\ngas is described as shown in Eq. [(1)](#4).\n\nε*b*\n\nvð*C*Þ\n\nv*t*\n\nþ r*b*\n\nvð*q*Þ\n\nv*t*\n\nVð*Dz*  *De*ÞV*C* þ\n\nvðv*sC*Þ\n\nv*z*\n\n¼ *R*\n\n(1)\n\nwhere, *C* (mg/L) and *q* (mg/g) are the SMX concentration in the\n\nliquid and solid phase, respectively, ε*b* and r*b* (kg/m3) are the bed\n\nvoid fraction and bed bulk density, respectively. *Dz* (m2/s) and *De*\n\nare (m2/s) are the axial dispersion coefficient and volatilization\n\ncoefficient, respectively, and *vs* (m/s) is the superficial velocity. On\n\nthe left side of Eq. [(1)](#4), the first two terms correspond to the SMX\n\nconcentration within the liquid and solid phase, the following term\n\nintroduces the dispersion (*Dz*) and volatilization (*De*) phenomenon\n\nin the x, y, and z directions. While the last term describes mass\n\ntransfer by convection. On the right-hand side, R describes the rate\n\nof the adsorption reaction. The volatilization and reaction adsorp-\n\ntion were neglected (*De* ¼ *0, R* ¼ *0*) for being a little influential in\n\naqueous phase adsorption ([Xu et al., 2013](#12)). Besides, it was\n\nconsidered that there is an SMX concentration gradient only in the\n\nlongitudinal direction (z-axis) of the column. The variation of su-\n\nperficial velocity along the longitudinal direction of the bed was\n\nestimated using Eq. [(2) ](#4)([Babu and Gupta, 2005](#11)).\n\nr\n\nvðv*s*Þ\n\nv*z*\n\n¼ &#3;ð1 &#3; ε*b*Þr*b*\n\nv*q*\n\nv*t*\n\n(2)\n\nwhere r (kg/m3) is the density of the SMX solution. The adsorption\n\nkinetic model used in this study was the Linear driving force (LDF)\n\nmodel. In this model, the intrapellet diffusion rate is represented by\n\na lumped mass transfer coefficient and is shown in Eq. [(3) ](#4)([Naidu](#12)\n\n[and Mathews, 2021](#12)).\n\nv*q*\n\nv*t*\n\n¼ *Ki*ð*qe*  *q*Þ\n\n(3)\n\nwhere, *Ki* (1/s) is the global mass transfer coefficient and *qe* (mg/g)\n\nis the SMX concentration within the adsorbent particle at equilib-\n\nrium. In Eq. [(3)](#4), *Ki* combined both the film diffusion and intra-\n\nparticle diffusion. Besides, within the intraparticle mass transfer,\n\nboth surface diffusion and pore diffusion were considered, since\n\nboth are simultaneously involved according to several studies\n\n([Kavand et al., 2018](#12); [Xu et al., 2013](#12)). Finally, the adsorption equi-\n\nlibrium model used was the Langmuir isotherm, Eq. [(4)](#4), according\n\nto our previous batch studies carried out in our department ([Zeas](#13)\n\n[Sol](#13)orzano and Zhunio Campoverde, 2019).\n\n*qe* ¼\n\n*qmaxkLCe*\n\n1 þ *kLCe*\n\n(4)\n\nwhere, *q*max (mg/g) is the maximum adsorption capacity, *kL* (L/mg)\n\nis the energy constant related to the heat of adsorption, and *Ce* (mg/\n\nL) is the SMX concentration in the liquid phase at equilibrium.\n\nThese equilibrium data were estimated at a temperature of 20 C\n\nand are presented in [Table 3](#6).\n\n*2.2.1. Boundary and initial conditions*\n\nThe boundary and initial conditions to solve Eqs. Parameters*’ *estimation*\n\nSeveral additional parameters are necessary to solve the\n\nmechanistic model. Empirical correlations were used to estimate\n\nthe axial dispersion coefficient *Dz*, the global mass transfer coeffi-\n\ncient *Ki*, the film diffusion coefficient *kf*, the SMX molecular diffu-\n\nsion coefficient *Dm*, the effective pore diffusion coefficient *Dep*, the\n\npore diffusion coefficient *Dp*, surface diffusion coefficient *Ds*, the\n\ntortuosity factor t*p*, the Reynolds number *Re*, the Schmidt number\n\n*Sc*, and the Peclet number *Pe*. All these parameters were estimated\n\nusing Eqs. (10)-(18) and are shown in [Table 2](#5).\n\nWhere, ε*p* is the particle porosity, *dp* (m) and *rp* (m) are the\n\naverage diameter and average radius of the adsorbent particle, a*A* is\n\na solvent association parameter, *Ms* (g/mol) is the molecular weight\n\nof the solvent, *T* (K) is the temperature of the SMX solution, *Vm*\n\n(cm3/mol) is the molar volume of SMX at its normal boiling point, r\n\n(kg/m3) and m (kg/m.s) are the density and dynamic viscosity of the\n\nSMX solution, and *vs* (m/s) is the superficial velocity through the\n\nbed. The properties of SB and SMX used to estimate the parameters\n\nare described in [Table 3](#6). The methodology used to measure equi-\n\nlibrium ([Zeas Sol](#13)orzano and Zhunio Campoverde, 2019), fixed-bed\n\nparameters ([Vera et al., 2016](#12)), and sugarcane bagasse properties\n\n([Juela, 2020](#12); [Peñ](#12)afiel et al., 2021) is detailed in our previous studies.\n\n*2.3. Numerical solution*\n\nThe model was implemented and solved numerically in Comsol\n\nMultiphysics V5.4. The module of partial differential equations\n\n(PDE) was used in this study; three PDE modules were used to\n\nrepresent the Eqs. [(1)](#4)e(3), according to the methodology described\n\nby [Aguilera and Ortiz (2016)](#11). The time-dependent and one-\n\ndimensional model was solved using the finite element method.\n\n**3. Materials and methods**\n\n*3.1. Adsorbent and adsorbate*\n\nRaw SB was obtained from a local sugar mill in Azuay, Ecuador.\n\nBefore their use in adsorption tests, SB was washed repeatedly with\n\ndistilled water in order to remove impurities and residual sugars.\n\nThen, sugarcane marrow was dried in an oven at 60 C for 8 h.\n\nFinally, the size of the fibers was reduced using a hammer mill and\n\nsubsequent. Particles between 0.841 and 0.42 mm were used in\n\nadsorption experiments.\n\nSMX with a purity of 99.00% supplied by Sigma-Aldrich was\n\nused in this study. Methanol of analytical grade Merck with a purity\n\nof 99.00% was used to dissolve SMX. The SMX solutions were\n\nprepared with distilled water and with 1% V/V methanol. All syn-\n\nthetic solutions had a concentration of 5 mg/L of SMX. The pH of the\n\nsolution was adjusted to 6 with the addition of HCl (0.1 N) solution.\n\n*3.2. Packed column tests*\n\nA glass column with a size of 35 cm height and 2.2 cm internal\n\ndiameter was used for the adsorption tests. The system was oper-\n\nated with downward flow, and at a temperature of 18 C. For the\n\nexperimental setup of the column, initially, 1 cm of gravel of 2 mm\n\ndiameter was placed at the bottom of the column to prevent the\n\nadsorbent from being entrained by the solution, then the adsorbent\n\npreviously weighed was introduced into the inside the column,\n\nthen 1 cm of gravel of 2 mm diameter was placed on the top of the\n\nfixed bed in order to avoid the floating of the bed and provide a\n\nbetter distribution to the SMX solution. A peristaltic pump was\n\nused to pump the SMX solution up to the column inlet. SMX\n\nsamples were periodically collected at the column outlet. The initial\n\nand residual concentrations of SMX were determined by the Visible\n\nGenesys 10S UV spectrophotometry technique from Thermo SCI-\n\nENTIFIC, at a wavelength of 261 nm. Finally, the\n\nbreakthrough time (*tb*/*C/C0* ¼ *0.05*), saturation time (*ts*/*C/C0*\n\n¼ *0.75*), the adsorption capacity *qe* (mg/g), and the fraction of the\n\nsaturated bed was estimated *Hb* (%).\n\n*qe* ¼\n\n*Q*\n\n1000 *m*\n\nð*t*¼*ts*\n\n*t*¼0\n\n*CRdt*\n\n(19)\n\n*Hb* ¼\n\n*Ztb*/*ts*\n\n*Z*\n\n\\*100\n\n(20)\n\n**4. Results and discussion**\n\nThe SMX adsorption onto sugarcane bagasse was carried at\n\nthree flow rates (2, 5, and 7 mL/min) and two values of bed height\n\n(15 and 25 cm). [Fig. 2](#6)a, b, and 2c show the experimental break-\n\nthrough curves at bed height of 15 cm and flow rates of 2, 5, and\n\n7 mL/min, respectively. Whereas [Fig. 2](#6)d, e, and 2f show the\n\nexperimental breakthrough curves at bed height of 25 cm and flow\n\nrates of 2, 5, and 7 mL/min, respectively. The breakthrough and\n\nsaturation times increase as the flow rate decreases ([Table 4](#7)), hence\n\nthe breakthrough and saturation times are greater at lower flow\n\nrates, it is because the interstitial velocity increases and the resi-\n\ndence time of the SMX solution decreases as the flow rate increases\n\n([Babu and Gupta, 2005](#11)). Similarly, an increase in bed height\n\nsignificantly prolongs the breakthrough and saturation time of the\n\ncolumn because there is a greater amount of adsorbent material\n\navailable for SMX removal ([Juela, 2020](#12)). Besides, the experimental\n\nadsorption capacity (*qe*) decreased with flow rate, but no particular\n\ntrend was observed with the change of bed height. The maximum\n\nadsorption capacity was obtained under conditions of a flow rate of\n\n2 mL/min and a bed height of 25 cm; similarly, at the same\n\n**Table 3**\n\nProperties of fixed-bed and SMX solution.\n\nParameter\n\nSymbol\n\nValue\n\nUnit\n\nEquilibrium data\n\nMaximum adsorption capacity\n\n*q*max\n\n1.52\n\nmg/g\n\nEnergy constant\n\n*kL*\n\n0.02\n\nL/mg\n\nAdsorption capacity at equilibrium\n\n*q0*\n\n0.155\n\nmg/g\n\nFixed-bed\n\nBulk density\n\nr*b*\n\n70.2\n\nkg/m3\n\nBed diameter\n\n*D*\n\n0.022\n\nm\n\nBed porosity\n\ne*b*\n\n0.63\n\ne\n\nSugarcane bagasse\n\nSB particle porosity\n\ne*p*\n\n0.21\n\ne\n\nSB particle density\n\nr*b*\n\n1088.4\n\nkg/m3\n\nAverage particle diameter\n\n*dp*\n\n5.9 &#7; 104\n\nm\n\nSpecific surface area\n\n*S*\n\n2.55\n\nm2/g\n\nSMX solution\n\nMolar volume of SMX\n\n*Vm*\n\n173.1\n\ncm3/mol\n\nDensity solution\n\nР\n\n998.6\n\nkg/m3\n\nSolvent association parameter\n\na*A*\n\n2.6\n\ne\n\nDynamic viscosity solution\n\nm\n\n0.001\n\nkg/m.s\n\n**Fig. 2.** Experimental and predicted breakthrough curves at flow rates of 2, 5, and 7 mL/min, 15 cm (a, b, c) and 25 cm (d, e, f) of bed height.\n\n*D. Juela, M. Vera, C. Cruzat et al.*\n\n*Chemosphere 280 (2021) 130687*\n\n6\n\n-----\n\n|  |\n| :-: |\n| [**Page 7**]() |\n\nconditions, the highest fraction of the saturated bed was obtained.\n\nBesides, *Hb* increased with bed height in most cases, but no clear\n\neffect was observed with the change of flow rate. Considering these\n\nparameters, the optimal operative conditions, where the best per-\n\nformance of the sugarcane bagasse to remove SMX is achieved,\n\nwere 2 mL/min of flow rate and 25 cm of bed height.\n\n*4.1. Fit with analytical models*\n\nThe six experimental tests were fitted to seven analytical\n\nmodels; the predicted breakthrough curves by the models are\n\nshown in [Fig. 2](#6). The correlation coefficient (R2) and the kinetic\n\nparameters of each model are shown in [Table 4](#7). As can be seen, high\n\ncorrelation coefficients R2 \\\u003e 0.90 were obtained with the Log-\n\nGompertz and Modified dose-response models for all the adsorp-\n\ntion tests, being the Log-Gompertz model the one that best\n\nreproduced the experimental data. The Clark and Gompertz models\n\nhad also a good fit with the experimental data, their R2 values are\n\nbetween 0.87 and 0.98. For the Logistic model, which groups the\n\nYoon Nelson, Thomas, and Bohart-Adam models, R2 values were\n\nobtained in the range of 0.77e0.95, while the worst fit was ob-\n\ntained with the Wolborska model with R2 \\\u003c 0.69. In other words,\n\nthe R2 values obtained with kinetic models which provide asym-\n\nmetric breakthrough curves (the Log-Gompertz and Modified dose-\n\nresponse, Clark, and Gompertz models) were higher than the\n\nmodels wich represents symmetric sigmoidal curves (the Yoon\n\nNelson, Thomas, and Bohart-Adam models) ([Hu et al., 2020](#12)).\n\nIn [Fig. 2](#6), the predicted breakthrough curves by the Modified\n\ndose-response model were very close to the experimental curve.\n\nThis model also had a good fit in the SMX adsorption with other\n\nadsorbents ([Jaria et al., 2019](#12)). At conditions of 2 mL/min of flow rate\n\nand 15 cm of bed height, this model provides a high fit with the\n\nexperimental curve, especially in the transitory and saturation\n\nzone; when the conditions change, the breakthrough curves of this\n\nmodel only coincide with the experimental data in the initial re-\n\ngion, and for this reason, the correlation coefficient values are\n\nlower. The breakthrough curves predicted by the Clark and Gom-\n\npertz models were very similar for all experimental tests. According\n\nto [Hu et al. (2020) ](#12)the Clark model will generate asymmetric\n\nbreakthrough curves when the parameter n s2; in this study,\n\nn ¼ 1.2 ([Zeas Sol](#13)orzano and Zhunio Campoverde, 2019) indicates\n\nthat the breakthrough curves are asymmetric and therefore have an\n\nacceptable fit with the experimental data. While the Wolborska\n\nmodel had not a good agreement with the experimental data in any\n\nregion. Generally, this model is suitable to reproduce the initial\n\nregion of the breakthrough curve (C/C0 \\\u003c 0.5), but in this case, its\n\nadjustment was low even in this region; this means that the SMX\n\nadsorption is not controlled by film diffusion ([Das et al., 2020](#11)).\n\nOf all analytical models analyzed in this study, the Log-\n\nGompertz model was the one that best reproduced the experi-\n\nmental data; the predicted breakthrough curves coincide with the\n\nexperimental data in most of the trajectory, but it was more accu-\n\nrate in the initial region of the curve where the rest of models failed.\n\nThis model recently adopted for adsorption processes has also\n\nshown a good correlation with the breakthrough data in the\n\nadsorption of metals and dyes ([Chu, 2020b](#11)). [Chu (2020b) ](#11)also\n\nproved that this model is better than the Logistic (the Thomas,\n\nBohart-Adams, and Yoon-Nelson models) and Gompertz models,\n\nand this research work shows that this model can even have a\n\nbetter fit than the popular Modified dose-response model. The\n\nmain justification for these results is that the Log-Gompertz model\n\nwas developed to fit asymmetric breakthrough curves, such as\n\nthose obtained in this study, whereas the Bohart-Adams, Thomas,\n\nand Yoon-Nelson models only predict symmetric sigmoidal curves,\n\nwhich is a great drawback for adsorption studies with asymmetric\n\ndata ([Darweesh and Ahmed, 2017](#11); [Reynel-Avila et al., 2015](#12)).\n\n**Table 4**\n\nKinetic parameters of the seven analytical models.\n\nExperimental\n\nQ (mL/min)\n\n2\n\n5\n\n7\n\n2\n\n5\n\n7\n\nZ Cruzat et al.*\n\n*Chemosphere 280 (2021) 130687*\n\n7\n\n-----\n\n|  |\n| :-: |\n| [**Page 8**]() |\n\nAlthough the equations of the Gompertz and modified dose-\n\nresponse models also are essentially asymmetric, their fit is not\n\nenough especially in the initial and final region of the breakthrough\n\ncurve; this happens when the experimental curve exhibits a pro-\n\nnounced degree of tailing ([Chu, 2020b](#11)). On the other hand, the Log-\n\nGompertz model, which is a modified version of the Gompertz\n\nmodel, is able to effectively model a strong asymmetry because it\n\ntransforms the independent variable *t* (from the Gompertz model)\n\nto the term ln *(t)*, which improves the fit with the breakthrough\n\ndata in the initial and final region.\n\nA drawback of the Log-Gompertz and Gompertz models is that\n\ntheir parameters do not provide valuable information regarding the\n\nadsorption process; that is because there is not a mechanistic un-\n\ndertone behind these models. The equation of the Gompertz model\n\nequation was initially developed to explain the human mortality\n\ncurve, and therefore, it is not based on any assumptions regarding\n\nadsorption mechanisms ([Gompertz, 1825](#12)). Accordingly, its param-\n\neters do not elucidate useful information on the dominant mech-\n\nanisms in the adsorption of SMX onto SB. However, it was observed\n\nthat these parameters change as the operating conditions of the\n\nfixed-bed column change; Both b*G* and a*G* of the Log-Gompertz\n\nmodel decrease with increasing flow rate, but increasing with\n\nbed height. Although the same parameters with the Gompertz\n\nmodel did not show a clear trend with changes in flow rate and bed\n\nheight.\n\nIn most cases, the kinetic constant of the Bohart-Adams and\n\nThomas models increased with flow rate, hence the bed saturation\n\nis rapid at high flow rates ([Jaria et al., 2019](#12)). Similarly, the time in\n\nwhich SMX in the effluent reaches 50% of the initial concentration\n\n(t), in the Yoon-Nelson model, decreases with the increase in the\n\nflow rate but increases with the increase in the bed height. This is\n\nlogical as the experimental breakthrough curves shift to the right\n\nwith decreasing flow rate and increasing bed depth. A similar effect\n\nwas observed in the adsorption of tetracycline with active carbon\n\n([Marzbali and Esmaieli, 2017](#12)).\n\nBoth Modified dose-response and Thomas models assume no\n\naxial dispersion, external and internal diffusion resistances\n\nextremely small, and the second-order reversible reaction kinetics.\n\nAlthough both of these models are based on the same assumptions,\n\ntheir adjustment to the experimental data is different; this is due to\n\nthe modified dose-response model was proposed to minimize the\n\nmathematical errors of the Thomas model equation, for this reason,\n\nthis model gives a better adjustment to the experimental data ([Yan](#12)\n\n[et al., 2001](#12)). Despite the best fitting of the Modified dose-response\n\nmodel, the adsorption capacity determined by this model in some\n\ncases is higher and in other cases is lower than experimentally ones\n\n([Table 4](#7)). Besides, a decrease of *qF* values of the Modified dose-\n\nresponse model when increasing the flow rate was expected, just\n\nas it happened in experimentation, but it was not observed. The\n\nsame happened with the adsorption capacity predicted by the\n\nThomas and Wolborska models; this behavior has been also\n\nobserved in other studies ([Dorado et al., 2014](#12); [Jaria et al., 2019](#12)).\n\nRegarding the amount of adsorbate retained per bed volume (*N0*) of\n\nthe Bohart-Adams model, it also should decrease as the flow rate\n\nincreases, but it had a similar effect to *qF* in the Thomas and\n\nModified dose-response models. In all cases, no direct effect on *qF*\n\nand *N0* was observed with bed height. Similar to other models, the\n\nparameters *r* and *B* of the Clark model have no clear relationship\n\nwith the change of flow rate and bed height.\n\n*4.2. Comparison of the best analytical model with the mechanistic*\n\n*model*\n\nThe dynamic behavior of the SMX in the column packed with SB\n\nwas also studied with a theoretical mechanistic model, which is\n\nbased on the film and intraparticle diffusions, and on the axial\n\ndispersion phenomenon. This mechanistic model was compared\n\nwith the best-fit analytical model (Log-Gompertz model), and the\n\nsum squared errors (SSE) and the correlation coefficient (R2) were\n\nused as error functions to analyze the fit between experimental and\n\npredicted breakthrough curves. The experimental and predicted\n\nbreakthrough curves by the Log-Gompertz and mechanistic models\n\nare shown in [Fig. 3](#8), while the values of SSE and R2 for both models\n\nare shown in [Table 5](#9).\n\n*SSE* ¼\n\nX*n*\n\n*i*¼1\n\n*xi*,*cal*  *xi*,*meas*\n\n2\n\n(21)\n\n*R*2 ¼ 1\n\nP*n*\n\n*i*¼1\n\n*xi*,*cal*  *xi*,*meas*\n\n2\n\nP*n*\n\n*i*¼1\n\n*xi*,*meas*  *x*\n\n2\n\n(22)\n\nThe C/C0 values predicted by the theoretical model agree well\n\nwith the experimental data, as good as the Log-Gompertz model.\n\nHowever, the predicted curves with the analytical model are more\n\nconsistent, especially in the initial and final region of the\n\n**Fig. 3.** Experimental and predicted breakthrough curves by the Log-Gompertz and mechanistic models at 15 cm (a) and 25 cm (b) of bed height.\n\n*D. Juela, M. Vera, C. Cruzat et al.*\n\n*Chemosphere 280 (2021) 130687*\n\n8\n\n-----\n\n|  |\n| :-: |\n| [**Page 9**]() |\n\nbreakthrough curve; whereas the mechanistic model does not\n\naccurately correlate with experimental data in the final region of\n\nthe curve. While in the transitional stage both models show a good\n\nfit. The breakthrough and saturation times of simulated curves with\n\nthe theoretical model are less than the experimental ones, which\n\ntheoretically represents a fast adsorption rate of SMX. Conversely,\n\nthese operating times were very similar to the curves predicted by\n\nthe analytical model. The R2 and SSE values are given in [Table 5](#9). The\n\npredicted breakthrough curve by the Log-Gompertz model has\n\nhigher R2 values (R2 \\\u003e 0.98) and lower SSE values (SSE\\\u003c0.016) than\n\nthe mechanistic model in all tests; this confirms that the analytical\n\nmodel fits better with experimental breakthrough data. Even\n\nthough the R2 and SSE values were lower than the analytical model,\n\nthe theoretical model proposed for the SMX adsorption onto SB\n\npredicts the breakthrough curves with acceptable precision; cor-\n\nrelation coefficients higher than 0.87 and SSE values lower than\n\n0.13 were obtained, and according to the values of these statistical\n\nindicators, predicted results are not highly consistent with exper-\n\nimental data ([William Kajjumba et al., 2019](#12)). A noticeable deviation\n\nbetween breakthrough data and the mechanistic breakthrough\n\ncurve was obtained in the final region. The main reason may be the\n\ntail behavior of the experimental breakthrough curves; this phe-\n\nnomenon has been observed in other mechanistic models where\n\nthe simulated curve does not converge with the final region of the\n\nexperimental data ([Chu, 2020b](#11); [Izquierdo et al., 2010](#12); [Vera et al.,](#12)\n\n[2021](#12)). Many authors suggest that the factors responsible for the\n\ntailing behavior should be included in the mechanistic model to get\n\nbetter breakthrough curves ([Chu, 2020b](#11)). Although the mecha-\n\nnistic model does not provide great precision in the final region, it\n\nshowed a good correlation in the initial and transition stage of the\n\ncurves, which could be useful because in the practice the adsorbent\n\nis replaced in the breakthrough time. Correlation coefficients\n\nhigher than 0.92, and sum squared errors less than 0.06 were\n\nobtained up to C/C0 of 0.6.\n\nAlthough the breakthrough curves predicted by the Log-\n\nGumpertz model had a good correlation with the experimental\n\nones, the good similarity between the results of the mechanistic\n\nmodel and the experimental results is encouraging and supports\n\nthe precision of the assumptions made, empirical correlations used,\n\nand model parameters calculated. Furthermore, when comparing\n\nboth models, it should be considered that the mechanistic model is\n\na theoretical model that for its application only requires basic in-\n\nformation, i.e. adsorbent and adsorbate properties, and operating\n\nconditions. Hence, a breakthrough curve can be simulated without\n\nprior experimentation. Whereas the analytical model requires\n\nbreakthrough data, that is, it is necessarily necessary to carry out\n\nexperimental adsorption studies before its application. Hence, this\n\nmodel is only a simple tool for experimental data fitting, without\n\nproviding useful information regarding the main mechanisms\n\ninvolved in the adsorption process. Furthermore, as we discussed\n\npreviously, their parameters do not show a clear relationship with\n\nthe change in operating conditions, and these in certain cases have\n\ndifferent behavior from those observed in experimentation; hence\n\ntheir practical usefulness is questionable. Undoubtedly, to under-\n\nstand better the complex mechanisms of adsorption, it is necessary\n\nto develop more rigorous mechanics models. The mechanistic\n\nmodel proposed in this study was validated with lab-scale column\n\nexperiments and showed a good agreement with the experimental\n\ndata; therefore, the same model can be used with confidence to\n\npredict the breakthrough curves for the same adsorbent/adsorbate\n\nsystem on large scale columns, such as pilot or industrial adsorp-\n\ntion columns. In this way, it would be possible to estimate break-\n\nthrough and saturations times on these columns and compare\n\nthem with conventional activated carbon columns. For these rea-\n\nsons, for the purposes of design, scaling, and optimization of\n\nadsorption columns the mechanistic models have shown to be\n\nmore useful ([Burkert et al., 2011](#11); [Unuabonah et al., 2019](#12)).\n\n*4.3. Mechanistic model parameters and the relative importance of*\n\n*axial dispersion*\n\nIn [Fig. 3](#8)a and b, the breakthrough curves were simulated with\n\nvalues of global mass transfer coefficient (*Ki*) and axial dispersion\n\ncoefficient (*Dz*) determined using the empirical correlations pre-\n\nsented in the Eqs. (10) and (11), without using the experimental\n\ndata shown. Both *Ki* and *Dz* vary with feed flow rate but remain\n\ninvariant with bed height as shown in [Fig. 4](#9)a and [Table 6](#10). The\n\njustification for this is because the *Ki* and *Dz* values are estimated by\n\n**Table 5**\n\nError functions for Log-Gompertz and mechanistic models.\n\nTest\n\nQ (mL/min)\n\nZ (cm)\n\nLog-Gompertz\n\nmodel\n\nMechanistic\n\nmodel\n\nSSE\n\nR2\n\nSSE\n\nR2\n\n1\n\n2\n\n15\n\n0.007\n\n0.996\n\n0.026\n\n0.987\n\n2\n\n5\n\n15\n\n0.006\n\n0.993\n\n0.127\n\n0.887\n\n3\n\n7\n\n15\n\n0.016\n\n0.987\n\n0.099\n\n0.922\n\n4\n\n2\n\n25\n\n0.007\n\n0.992\n\n0.043\n\n0.957\n\n5\n\n5\n\n25\n\n0.008\n\n0.993\n\n0.118\n\n0.892\n\n6\n\n7\n\n25\n\n0.011\n\n0.989\n\n0.113\n\n0.895\n\n**Fig. 4.** Variation of the global mass transfer and axial dispersion coefficients (a), and the Peclet number (b) with flow rate and bed height.\n\n*D. Juela, M. Vera, C. Cruzat et al.*\n\n*Chemosphere 280 (2021) 130687*\n\n9\n\n-----\n\n|  |\n| :-: |\n| [**Page 10**]() |\n\nempirical correlations that only consider the hydrodynamic con-\n\nditions, depending mainly on the fluid velocity for any adsorbate-\n\nadsorbent system ([Lin et al., 2017](#12); [Saadi et al., 2019](#12)). The param-\n\neter *Ki* combined both the film diffusion and intraparticle diffusion;\n\nthe film diffusion coefficient lowered with increasing flow rates\n\nindicating that the external mass transfer resistance is less influ-\n\nential ([Quek and Al-Duri, 2007](#12)). On the other hand, the surface\n\ndiffusion and pore diffusion coefficients remained constant for the\n\nvariation in flow rate and bed height ([Table 6](#10)). The Peclet number\n\n(*Pe*) was also affected by the change in flow rate and bed height\n\n([Fig. 4](#9)a and b), *Pe* increases proportionally with both flow rate and\n\nbed height, although the change was more significant with the bed\n\nheight (from 51.8 to 86.3 when it goes from 15 to 25 cm) than the\n\nflow rate (from 51.8 to 52.44 when it goes from 2 to 7 mL/min); this\n\nhappens because the Peclet number is based on the length of the\n\nfixed-bed (Eq. (18)).\n\nAdditionally, the relative importance of axial dispersion was\n\nassessed by comparison with the other rate mechanisms (i.e.\n\nintraparticle diffusion and external film mass transfer); the pa-\n\nrameters gp and gf were used, represented in Eq. [(23) ](#10)and showed\n\nin [Table 6 ](#10)([Cooney, 1991](#11)).\n\ng*p* ¼\n\na2ð1 &#3; ε*b*Þ\n\n15ε*b*\n\n*r*2\n\n*p K*\n\n*DepDz*\n\n\\!\n\ng*f* ¼\n\na2ð1 &#3; ε*b*Þ\n\n3ε*b*\n\n*rpK*2\n\n*kf Dz*\n\n\\!\n\n(23)\n\nwhere K is the distribution ratio equal to q0/C0, a is the velocity of\n\nconstant pattern front, and it was estimated with the correlation of\n\n[Cooney (1991)](#11). As [Cooney (1991) ](#11)has shown, when g values are\n\nhigher than 1, axial dispersion can safely be ignored. However, for\n\nthis study, the parameters gp and gf were lower than 1 for all\n\nstudies; so, in this case, the axial dispersion would be significant.\n\nThe axial dispersion phenomenon usually is neglected for liquid-\n\nphase systems, but many studies have shown that when the par-\n\nticle sizes and/or the flow rates are much smaller than usual, the\n\nparameters gp and gf could very easily be on the order of 1 or less, in\n\nwhich case axial dispersion would be significant as it happens in\n\nthis study ([Davila-Guzman et al., 2016](#11); [Díaz-Blancas et al., 2020](#11);\n\n[Franco et al., 2020](#12); [Saadi et al., 2015](#12)). Additionally, in all tests\n\ngf \\\u003c gp, which means that the interparticle diffusion is more rele-\n\nvant than film diffusion, and the difference between gp and gf is\n\nhigher as flow rate increases ([Cooney, 1991](#11)).\n\nFinally, a sensitivity analysis was developed in order to evaluate\n\nthe relative importance of the global mass transfer coefficient and\n\naxial dispersion coefficient in the prediction of the breakthrough\n\ncurves. The effect of *Ki* on the breakthrough curves was studied by\n\nvarying *Ki* from its base value (0.0011 s\n\n1\n\n, estimated for the simu-\n\nlated breakthrough curves at 2 mL/min), while the value of the axial\n\ndispersion coefficient remained constant at 2.96 &#7; 10\n\n7\n\nm2/s. Three\n\n*Ki* values were used, which were calculated using Eq. (10). The in-\n\nfluence of *Ki* on the breakthrough curves is shown in [Fig. 5](#10)a. A\n\nsimilar analysis was performed for *Dz*. Three *Dz* values were studied,\n\nstarting from its base value (2.96 &#7; 10\n\n7\n\nm2/s, used in the simulated\n\ncurves at 2 mL/min) while the *Ki* value was kept constant at 0.0011\n\ns\n\n1\n\n. [Fig. 5](#10)b shows the results obtained.\n\nThe variation of *Ki* from 0.0011 to 0.011 s\n\n1\n\nproduces an increase\n\nin the breakthrough times and a decrease in the saturation times;\n\nthis is mainly due to the increase in the slope of the breakthrough\n\ncurve as *Ki* increases. According to the LDF kinetic model, the\n\nadsorption rate of SMX is directly proportional to the mass transfer\n\ncoefficient, hence high *Ki* values increase the adsorption rate;\n\nincreasing *Ki* also means that the external film resistance, and\n\ndiffusion and intraparticle resistances decrease; so the saturation\n\nrate increases rapidly ([Xu et al., 2013](#12)). Something that is also\n\n**Table 6**\n\nFilm diffusion and intraparticle diffusion parameters.\n\nQ (mL/min)\n\nH (cm)\n\nkf x106 (m/s)\n\nDs x1012 (m2/s)\n\nDp x1012 (m2/s)\n\nDep x1012 (m2/s)\n\nKi x103 (1/s)\n\nDz x107 (m2/s)\n\ngp\n\ngf\n\n2\n\n15\n\n6.61\n\n3.86\n\n9.89\n\n6.68\n\n1.10\n\n2.96\n\n0.26\n\n0.15\n\n5\n\n15\n\n8.46\n\n3.86\n\n9.89\n\n6.68\n\n2.11\n\n7.30\n\n0.66\n\n0.29\n\n7\n\n15\n\n9.33\n\n3.86\n\n9.89\n\n6.68\n\n2.81\n\n10.2\n\n0.92\n\n0.38\n\n**Fig. 5.** Effect of global mass transfer coefficient (a) and axial dispersion coefficient (b) on the predicted breakthrough curves.\n\n*D. Juela, M. Vera, C. Cruzat et al.*\n\n*Chemosphere 280 (2021) 130687*\n\n10\n\n-----\n\n|  |\n| :-: |\n| [**Page 11**]() |\n\nevident is that the rise in *Ki* causes the curves to start to converge\n\nafter a certain value. In [Fig. 5](#10)a, the curve of *Ki* ¼ 0.011 s1\n\nis very\n\nclose and practically coincident to the curve of *Ki* ¼ 0.031 s1\n\n,\n\ndespite the notable difference in the values of the global mass\n\ntransfer coefficient. Which could mean that for higher values of *Ki*\n\nthe curves will overlap, and the variation in the SMX adsorption\n\nrate would become smaller and smaller. As a result, for a higher\n\nvalue of *Ki*, the adsorption rate could be roughly constant. Similar\n\nresults were obtained in other fixed-bed column adsorption sim-\n\nulations ([Aguilera and Guti](#11)errez Ortiz, 2016; [Shekhar, 2014](#12)).\n\nThe axial dispersion coefficient groups the effects of turbulence,\n\nflow division, and reincorporation around particles, Taylor disper-\n\nsion, channeling, and wall effects suffered by the fluid when\n\ncirculating through the porous bed ([Lin et al., 2017](#12)). In [Fig. 5](#10)b, the\n\nincrease in *Dz* from 1.46 &#7; 10\n\n7\n\nto 4.96 &#7; 10\n\n7\n\nm2/s causes the\n\nbreakthrough and saturation times to shift to the left, causing\n\nsaturation of the bed in a shorter time and lower adsorption ca-\n\npacities. This behavior of the breakthrough curve is reasonable\n\nsince at high values of *Dz* the effects of axial mixing will be\n\nconsiderable, reducing the efficiency in the adsorption process and\n\nproducing a rapid saturation of the bed ([Aguilera and Guti](#11)errez\n\n[Ortiz, 2016](#11)). Another important effect on the variation of *Dz* is re-\n\nflected in *Pe*. From [Fig. 5](#10)b, it is observed that when *Dz* decreases\n\nfrom 4.96 &#7; 10\n\n7\n\nto 1.46 &#7; 10\n\n7\n\nm2/s, *Pe* increases from 30.81 to\n\n104.7. Furthermore, with *Dz* values less than 1.46 &#7; 10\n\n7\n\nm2/s, *Pe*\n\nwill be sufficiently greater than 100 and the axial dispersion phe-\n\nnomenon will not have any influence on the breakthrough curves. If\n\nthe axial dispersion were neglected in the simulation (*Dz* ¼ 0 m2/s),\n\nthe breakthrough curve obtained would be very similar to that\n\nobtained with *Dz* ¼ 1.46 &#7; 107\n\nm2/s, and it would differ signifi-\n\ncantly\n\nfrom\n\nthe\n\nsimulated\n\ncurve\n\nwith\n\naxial dispersion\n\n(*Dz* ¼ 2.96 &#7; 107\n\nm2/s). Therefore, it can be inferred againthat the\n\naxial dispersion phenomenon is significant in the SMX adsorption\n\nwith SB. This may be the main reason why the breakthrough curves\n\npredicted by analytical models are not accurate and differ from the\n\nexperimental data, since most of these models do not take this\n\nphenomenon into account ([Unuabonah et al., 2019](#12); [Xu et al., 2013](#12)).\n\n**5. Conclusions**\n\nExperimental breakthrough curves at different flow rates and\n\nbed heights were used to fit the classical analytical models. The\n\nModified dose-response and Log-Gompertz models were the two\n\nbest that showed good capabilities for breakthrough data fitting.\n\nHowever, their kinetic parameters did not provide relevant infor-\n\nmation about the adsorption process of SMX on SB, and in certain\n\ncases, these parameters had a different behavior from that\n\nobserved in the experimentation. The Log-Gompertz model, which\n\nwas the one that best reproduced the experimental asymmetric\n\nbreakthrough curves, was compared with a mechanistic model that\n\naccounts for external and intraparticle diffusion, and axial disper-\n\nsion. Both models showed good agreement with experimental data,\n\nbeing the Log-Gompertz model that showed a slightly better fit.\n\nUseful parameters such as the global mass transfer coefficient, axial\n\ndispersion coefficient, pore diffusion coefficient, and surface\n\ndiffusion coefficient were estimated with the mechanistic model.\n\nFinally, the model showed the importance of the axial dispersion in\n\nthe SMX adsorption on a lab scale. This model can be used with\n\nconfidence for purposes of design, scaling, and optimization of\n\nadsorption columns.\n\n**Credit author statement**\n\nDiego Juela: Investigation, Formal analysis, Writing e original\n\ndraft, Writing e review & editing.
