  "fileContent": "Estimation of fixed-bed column parameters and mathematical modeling of breakthrough behaviors for adsorption of levulinic acid from aqueous solution using SY-01 resin \n\nAccepted Manuscript\n\nEstimation of fixed-bed column parameters and mathematical modeling of breakthrough behaviors for adsorption of levulinic acid from aqueous solution using SY-01 resin\n\nXiaoqing Lin, Gaoxiang Qi, Silan Shi, Lian Xiong, Chao Huang, Xuefang Chen, Hailong Li, Xinde Chen\n\nPII: S1383-5866(16)30379-3 DOI: http://dx.doi.org/10.1016/j.seppur.2016.10.016 Reference: SEPPUR 13286\n\nTo appear in: Separation and Purification Technology\n\nReceived Date: 14 May 2016 Revised Date: 11 October 2016 Accepted Date: 14 October 2016\n\nPlease cite this article as: X. Lin, G. Qi, S. Shi, L. Xiong, C. Huang, X. Chen, H. Li, X. Chen, Estimation of fixed- bed column parameters and mathematical modeling of breakthrough behaviors for adsorption of levulinic acid from aqueous solution using SY-01 resin, Separation and Purification Technology (2016), doi: http://dx.doi.org/10.1016/ j.seppur.2016.10.016\n\nThis is a PDF file of an unedited manuscript that has been accepted for publication. As a service to our customers we are providing this early version of the manuscript. The manuscript will undergo copyediting, typesetting, and review of the resulting proof before it is published in its final form. Please note that during the production process errors may be discovered which could affect the content, and all legal disclaimers that apply to the journal pertain.\n\nEstimation of fixed-bed column parameters and\n\nmathematical modeling of breakthrough behaviors for\n\nadsorption of levulinic acid from aqueous solution using\n\nSY-01 resin\n\nXiaoqing Lina,b,c, Gaoxiang Qib,d, Silan Shib,d, Lian Xionga,b,c, Chao Huanga,b,c,\n\nXuefang Chena,b,c, Hailong Lia,b,c, Xinde Chena,b,c,\\*\n\na Key Laboratory of Renewable Energy, Chinese Academy of Sciences, No. 2\n\nNengyuan Road, Tianhe District, Guangzhou 510640, People’s Republic of China\n\nb Guangzhou Institute of Energy Conversion, Chinese Academy of Sciences, No. 2\n\nNengyuan Road, Tianhe District, Guangzhou 510640, People’s Republic of China\n\nc R\\&D Center of Xuyi Attapulgite Applied Technology, Guangzhou Institute of\n\nEnergy Conversion, Chinese Academy of Sciences, No. 3 Dongfeng Road, Xuyi\n\n211700, People’s Republic of China\n\nd University of Chinese Academy of Sciences, No. 19A Yuquan Road, Beijing\n\n100049, People’s Republic of China\n\n∗ Corresponding author: Prof. Xinde Chen\n\nTel./Fax: +86 20 37213916\n\nE-mail: cxd\\_cxd@hotmail.com\n\n1\n\nAbstract\n\nLevulinic acid (LA) adsorption on a microporous hyper-cross-linked adsorption\n\nresin, SY-01, from aqueous solution was performed in a fixed-bed column. Batch\n\nequilibrium isotherm was analyzed using Langmuir and Freundlich isotherm models,\n\nand the Langmuir isotherm model was found to fit the adsorption isotherm data well.\n\nThe effects of feed flow rate (Qf=1.0-5.0 mL/min), initial LA concentration\n\n(cf=1.0-10.0 g/L), fixed-bed column length (Lc=5.65-16.96 cm) and fixed-bed column\n\ndiameter (Dc=1.6-5.5 cm) on the adsorption characteristics of LA were investigated\n\nsystematically. The results show that an increase in fixed-bed column length,\n\nfixed-bed column diameter, initial LA concentration and a decrease in feed flow rate\n\nimprove the adsorption performance. Furthermore, a general rate model (GRM) was\n\ndeveloped for predicting adsorption breakthrough curves of LA. The mathematical\n\nmodel results were in good agreement with the experimental data under various\n\noperating conditions, which confirmed that the GRM can successfully simulate this\n\nprocess. In addition, the values of Biot number (Bi) at different operating conditions\n\nwere all more than 10, demonstrating intraparticle diffusion is the rate controlling step\n\nfor the adsorption of LA on the resin. In conclusion, our results provide an important\n\nbasis for future scale-up of the product recovery of LA from biomass hydrolysate.\n\nKeywords: Levulinic acid; Fixed-bed column adsorption; Breakthrough curve;\n\nMathematical model; Resin adsorbent\n\n2\n\n1\\. Introduction\n\nDepleting fossil resources and growing concerns over environmental pollution\n\nand ecological deterioration have led to global interest in searching for alternative\n\nsustainable energy sources to produce fuels and value-added chemicals \\[1\\].\n\nLignocellulosic biomass, because of its properties of abundance, widely available,\n\nrenewable, and cheap, has attracted extensive worldwide attention of Academia and\n\nindustry in the fields of energy, chemical and biological engineering \\[2\\].\n\nConsequently, how to economically, safely and efficiently convert renewable biomass\n\nresource into high added value of industrial products or platform chemicals is key\n\ncentral to achieve a sustainable development of human society. Among the large\n\nnumber of fossil resource alternatives, levulinic acid (LA) has been recognized as one\n\nof the “Top 10” value-added platform chemicals from biomass by the U.S.\n\nDepartment of Energy, and has drawn worldwide concerns due to its easy production\n\nfrom lignocellulosic materials and can be converted to a broad range of desirable\n\nchemicals \\[3, 4\\].\n\nTraditionally, LA is mainly produced from cellulosic feedstocks using 1-5 wt%\n\nsulfuric acids as the catalyst \\[5\\]. During the dehydration process, hexose is dehydrated\n\ninto 5-hydroxymethylfurfural (5-HMF) firstly, and then 5-HMF will undergo a\n\nring-opening step to form LA together with formic acid (FA) under aqueous, acidic\n\nconditions. However, approximately 50-70% of the total cost for LA production is\n\ngenerated by downstream processing. Nevertheless, to the best of our knowledge,\n\ndownstream processing of LA is far less than that on selection of catalyst system as\n\nwell as preparation and optimization of catalyst. Therefore, downstream processing\n\nhas grown up to be an obstacle for large scale production of LA through hydrolysis\n\ntechnology.\n\n3\n\nTo date, several treatment methods and technologies have been proposed for LA\n\nrecovery from aqueous solution or actual biomass hydrolysate, which include\n\ndistillation \\[6\\], extraction \\[7\\], esterification \\[8\\], electrodialysis \\[9\\], and adsorption\n\n\\[10-16\\]. Among these methods, adsorption has been considered to be one of the most\n\nefficient and simple methods and has received major concern due to its wide\n\napplication scope, low-cost, energy-saving, environmentally friendly, easy handing,\n\nand high efficiency \\[17, 18\\]. Accordingly, various adsorbent, including active\n\ncarbon\\[15\\], ion-exchange resin\\[10-13\\], and hyper-cross-linked adsorption resin \\[16\\],\n\nhave been extensively studied for recovering LA for aqueous solutions or actual\n\nbiomass hydrolysate. However, the major drawback of large scale application of\n\nactive carbon is the low selectivity, higher production cost, and difficult regeneration\n\nand reuse. Although the ion exchange resin is widely applied in the filed of separation\n\nand purification of carboxylic acid, the most prominent shortcoming is that the\n\npretreatment and regeneration process of ion exchange resin requires large amounts of\n\nacid and alkali, which produces significant amounts of acid and alkali wastewaters,\n\nincreasing the cost of late treatment. Hyper-cross-linked adsorption resins have\n\nattracted wide attention for potential application in separation owing to their stable\n\nperformance, controllable pore structure and surface polarity, as well as high specific\n\nsurface area \\[19, 20\\]. In our previous work, a microporous hyper-cross-linked\n\nadsorption resin, SY-01, possessed amide groups \\[21\\], was used to adsorb LA\n\neffectively and had a high selectivity and affinity for LA \\[16\\]. This is mainly because\n\nof the LA can be absorbed onto SY-01 resin through π-π stacking formed between\n\ncross-linked benzene ring of resin and the alkyl chain of LA, as well as hydrogen\n\nbonding between the amide groups and LA, which played an important role in the\n\nadsorption. The details of adsorption equilibrium, thermodynamic, kinetic analysis\n\n4\n\nwere further determined experimentally and simulated. Therefore, the previous results\n\nsuggested that that the SY-01 resin has potential application in product recovery of LA\n\nfrom biomass hydrolysate.\n\nIn general, the application of resin adsorption process in industry was adopted by\n\nfixed-bed column adsorption. In order to design and scale up fixed-bed column\n\nadsorption, it is essential to investigate systematically the dynamics and transport in\n\nthe fixed-bed column \\[12\\]. However, the main disadvantages of the industrial design\n\nand optimization of adsorption operating conditions demanded considerable\n\nmanpower, material and financial resources \\[22\\]. Mathematical modeling is a useful\n\ntool for facilitating the industrial design and validation of fixed-bed column.\n\nMoreover, the mathematical modeling, once verified, can also estimate the\n\nconcentration profile for specific component in the liquid phase at all locations within\n\nthe fixed-bed column \\[22\\]. Consequently, the development, evaluation and\n\noptimization of a suitable adsorption process in fixed-bed columns can be easily\n\naccomplished by a mathematical model describing the effects of the integrated\n\nequilibrium and kinetics. Liu et al. have established a general rate model (GRM)\n\nconsidering external-film mass transfer followed by intraparticle pore diffusion or\n\nparallel pore/surface diffusion to describe the breakthrough curves of LA in a\n\nfixed-bed column with 335 and D315 ion exchange resin \\[12\\]. Sulaymon and Ahmed\n\nalso used GRM to prediction the multicomponent competitive adsorption of furfural\n\nand phenolic in a packed column with activated carbon \\[23\\].\n\nAs a continuation of our previous work, the main objective of this present work\n\nis devoted to the kinetic study of the adsorption of LA onto SY-01 resin in a fixed-bed\n\ncolumn experiment as well as by mathematic modeling. First of all, the adsorption\n\nequilibrium was described using Langmuir and Freundlich isotherm model. Secondly,\n\n5\n\nthe effects of feed flow rate (Qf=1.0-5.0 mL/min), initial LA concentration\n\n(cf=1.0-10.0 g/L), fixed-bed column length (Lc=5.65-16.96 cm) and fixed-bed column\n\ndiameter (Dc=1.6-5.5 cm) on the adsorption characteristics of LA were investigated\n\nsystematically. And lastly, the GRM was developed to describe the dynamic\n\nbreakthrough behaviors of LA adsorption in a fixed-bed column with SY-01 resin.\n\nMeanwhile, the model was verified by comparing the experimental and the\n\npredictions results. The results of this present study will be of great value to design\n\nand optimization of a chromatographic separation process for recovering LA from\n\naqueous solution or actual biomass hydrolysate.\n\n2\\. Theory\n\n2.1 Adsorption isotherm\n\nAccording to different adsorption mechanisms, there are currently several\n\ndifferent adsorption isotherms used for fitting experimental adsorption results \\[24\\].\n\nAmong these, two classic isotherm models, Langmuir \\[25\\] and Freundlich \\[26\\], are\n\nmost generally used to illustrate the equilibrium characteristics and therefore were\n\napplied in this work. These isotherms are mathematically described as follows:\n\nLangmuir isotherm model q = q 1\n\nmax + k k c c (1)\n\nFreundlich isotherm model q e = k F c 1/e n\n\nF(2)\n\nAll the isotherm model parameters were evaluated by non-linear regression using\n\nMatlab software. In all regression cases, the residual root mean square error (RMSE)\n\ntogether with the correlation coefficient (R2) was used to evaluate the fitness of the\n\nmodel calculations to the experimental data.\n\nThe RMSE can be defined as:\n\nL e e\n\nL e\n\n6\n\n1 RMSE = N − 2\n\n∑ N i = ( ) q exp − q cal\n\n(3)\n\n2.2 Modeling dynamic fixed-bed column breakthrough experiments\n\nThe chromatographic model is useful for the design and optimization of\n\nadsorption process in fixed-bed columns under different operating conditions. In this\n\nwork, a non-equilibrium and isothermal general rate model (GRM) is used to simulate\n\nthe adsorption behaviors of LA in the single fixed-bed column. GRM is one of the\n\nmost detailed chromatographic models, considering axial dispersion (Dax), external\n\nmass transfer through the liquid film around the resin particle (kfilm), intraparticle\n\ndiffusion resistance (Dp), and nonlinear isotherms , which has been used widely and\n\nsuccessfully in many domains to describe the adsorption and mass transfer processes\n\n\\[23, 27-29\\]. The following basic assumptions are made to formulate the GRM \\[27,\n\n30\\]: (1) the resin bed is homogeneous and the resin particles are spherical in shape\n\nand uniform in size; (2) the column void fraction, fluid flow rate and the liquid phase\n\nvelocity are constant during a run; (3) the operating adsorption process is isothermal;\n\n(4) the liquid phase inside pores is stationary and is not affected by movement of the\n\nmobile phase; (5) the compressibility of the liquid phase is negligible; and (6) the\n\nliquid phase has an axial dispersion flow.\n\nBased on these assumptions, the mass balance in a volume element for LA in the\n\nliquid phase can be expressed by the following equation \\[31\\]:\n\n2\n\n2\n\n2\n\n1\n\n∂ c ∂ t = D ax ∂ ∂ x c - ν ∂ ∂ x c - 1 − ε b\n\nε b\n\n  − =   (4)\n\n\\= f\n\nc b\n\n3 r p\n\nk film\n\nc c p | r r p ν QA ε (5)\n\nε b\n\n\\= V VBv\n\n(6)\n\n7\n\nThe relevant initial and boundary conditions are described as follows:\n\nInitial condition:\n\nt = 0,0 \\\u003c x \\\u003c L c ; c = 0 (7)\n\nBoundary condition: x = 0, t \\\u003e 0 ; D ax ∂ ∂c x = - ν ( c 0 − c| x = 0 ) (8)\n\nx = L c , t \\\u003e 0 ; ∂∂\n\ncx| x=L c =0 (9)\n\nStructurally, SY-01 resin is a rigid cross-linked polymer with pores that are\n\nsufficiently wide to permit diffusion \\[21\\]. The adsorbate needs to diffuse inside the\n\nresin pores for adsorption. In this study, the LA is a strong component adsorbed onto\n\nSY-01 resin. The adsorbed LA can not diffuse independently, due to the strong\n\nhydrophobic interaction formed between LA and SY-01. Therefore, authors regard\n\nthat the rate of surface diffusion was zero. In this case, the surface diffusion of\n\nstrongly retained compounds can be negligible in practice. The continuity equation in\n\nspherical coordinates of solid phase is given by:\n\nε p ∂ c p ∂ t + ρ p 2\n\n∂ q p ∂ t\n\n\\= r 1\n\n∂ ∂ r 2\n\n   r    p p ε D ∂ c p\n\n∂ r      \n\n(10)\n\nInitial conditions: 0 \\\u003c r \\\u003c r p , t = 0 ; c p = 0 ; q p = 0 (11)\n\nBoundary condition: r = 0, t \\\u003e 0 ; ∂c p∂ r\n\n| r=0\n\n\\=0 ; ∂q p∂ r\n\n| r=0\n\n\\=0 (12)\n\nr = r p , t \\\u003e 0 ; p ε p D ∂c p\n\n∂ r | r = r p = k film\n\n( c − c p | r =\n\nr p ) (13)\n\nThe adsorption equilibrium isotherm of solute at the liquid-solid phase interface\n\ncan be described by the Langmuir isotherm since it was the most suitable as discussed\n\n8\n\nin Section 3.1.\n\nq p\n\n\\= k 1L +\n\nq max\n\nk L c c p p\n\n(14)\n\n2.3 Numerical simulation methodology\n\nIn solving equation (4) and (8) together with the corresponding initial and\n\nboundary conditions, the method of orthogonal collocation on finite elements (OCFE)\n\nis used \\[32\\]. Equation (4) and (8) can be discretized into a set of ordinary differential\n\nequations (ODEs) by the finite element method and the orthogonal collocation method,\n\nrespectively. The number of elements in axial (x in coordination) direction and the\n\nnumber of points inparticular (r in coordination) directions were 50 and 50,\n\nrespectively. Then the resulting ODE system (with initial values) is solved with an\n\nODE solver (integrator) in MATLAB (The MathWorks, Natick, MA, USA). Absolute\n\nand relative tolerances of 10-5 were used.\n\n2.4 Estimation of fixed-bed column parameters\n\nThe GRM parameters include geometrical parameters (rp, εb, εp), fluid dynamics\n\nparameters (Dax), adsorption isotherms, and adsorption kinetics parameters (kfilm and\n\nDp).\n\nThe axial dispersion coefficient (Dax) was determined with the following\n\nempirical correlation suggested by Suzuki and Smith \\[33\\]:\n\nD ax = 0.44 D m + 0.83 Ud p (15)\n\nThe molecular diffusivity of levulinic acid (Dm) was calculated using the\n\nWilke-Chang equation \\[34\\]:\n\nD m\n\n7.4 10 8\n\n( A M s\n\n)0.5 T V m0.6 = ×\n\n− α\n\nµ\n\n(16)\n\nThe external mass transfer coefficient (kfilm) can be calculated using Wilson and\n\n9\n\nGeankoplis correlation \\[35\\]:\n\nSh = 1.09ε b\n\nRe 1/3 Sc 1/3 ; 0.0015 \\\u003c Re \\\u003c 55 (17)\n\nSh = d p D k m\n\nfilm\n\n; Re = υ d µ pρ\n\n;\n\nSc = ρ µDm\n\n(18)\n\nThe effective pore diffusion coefficient (Dp) was obtained from the simulation of\n\nadsorption kinetics, which was reported in our previous work.\n\nIn the calculations, the error between experimental and predicted values (ct,out/c0)\n\nwas calculated using the absolute average deviation (AAD):\n\n c , -\n\nc\n\n,\n\nAAD = N\n\n1 ∑ N| i =\n\n1 0 exp ,0\n\npred | 0exp c c   c c t out       t out       t out   \n\n(19)\n\nIn a laboratory scale, if AAD ≤ 0.05, the predicted results could be highly\n\nconsistent to the experimental data; if 0.05 \\\u003c AAD \\\u003c 0.1, the model provides a general\n\nagreement with the experimental data, and if AAD ≥ 0.1, the model may not\n\naccurately correlate with the experimental data \\[36\\].\n\n3\\. Materials and Methods\n\n3.1 Materials\n\nAll the chemicals used in the present study were of high-purity analytical grade,\n\nwhich were purchased from Sinopharm Chemical Reagent and used without further\n\nmodification. The hyper-cross-linked resin adsorbent SY-01 was kindly supplied by\n\nGuangzhou Institute of Energy Conversion, Chinese Academy of Sciences\n\n(Guangzhou, China). The detail characterization of the SY-01 resin were described\n\nand summarized in our previous work \\[21\\]. The characterization results show that the\n\nSY-01 resin contained well-developed pore structure in both the region of micropores\n\n10\n\nand mesopores, which helpful for mass transfer the LA to the internal adsorption sites.\n\n3.2 Equilibrium experiments\n\nAll experiments of adsorption isotherm were performed with 1.0 g resin in 100\n\nmL conical flasks with 50 mL of LA aqueous solution in a concentration ranging from\n\n0 g/L to 10.0 g/L. Then, the flasks were shaken under 160 rpm for 360 min at 298 K\n\nto reach adsorption equilibrium. After adsorption, the resin was filtered and the\n\nremaining LA concentration in aqueous solution was analyzed by high performance\n\nliquid chromatography (HPLC). The quantity of LA adsorbed per gram of the resin qe\n\n(mg/g) was calculated by the concentration difference method using Eq. (20).\n\n0 ( ) e e c c V q m− = (20)\n\n3.3 Dynamic column adsorption experiments\n\nThe fixed-bed column adsorption experiments were carried out in water-jacketed\n\nglass columns. Table 1 lists some of the physical properties of the SY-01 resin and\n\ncharacteristics of the fixed-bed along with the operating conditions used for the\n\ndynamic column adsorption experiments. All experiments in the fixed-bed operation\n\nwere performed isothermally at 298 K. A constant flow peristaltic pump (BT00-300M,\n\nHebei, China) transported LA from the top of the column at a prescribed flow rate. All\n\neffluent samples were collected at predetermined time intervals by a fraction collector\n\n(BS-100A, Shanghai, China). Afterwards, breakthrough curves were determined by\n\nanalyzing effluent samples by HPLC for varying the feed flow rate, initial LA\n\nconcentration, fixed-bed column length and fixed-bed column diameter. In this work,\n\nthe breakthrough time (tb), the stoichiometric breakthrough time (t\\*), the dynamic\n\nbreakthrough and saturation adsorption capacity (qb and qs), empty bed resistance time\n\n(EBRT), the length of the mass transfer zone, LMTZ , and the efficiency of the fixed-bed\n\ncolumn (ψ), are used to describe the breakthrough behavior of the LA adsorption. The\n\n11\n\nbreakthrough point and saturated point were set to be ct,out/c0=0.05 and ct,out/c0=0.95,\n\nrespectively. The capacities of LA adsorbed onto the SY-01 resin at breakthrough and\n\nsaturation point were calculated by numerical integration of the area above the\n\nbreakthrough curve using the following equation \\[37, 38\\]:\n\nq t\n\n\\= Q f c f V B ∫ 0t (1 − c t ,\n\nout c f\n\n)\n\ndt (21)\n\nThe empty bed resistance time (EBRT) was determined by the equation:\n\ncf E BR T = Q V = A c L c\n\nQ f\n\n(22)\n\nThe LMTZ was calculated using the following equation:\n\nL MTZ =\\[ t s − ( t t s s −\n\n− t t b\n\nb\n\n)/2\n\n\\] L c\n\n(23)\n\nThe efficiency of the fixed-bed column (ψ) was defined by:\n\nψ = t tb\\* (24)\n\nt b =\n\n∫ 0 t b    1- c t ,\n\nout c\n\n0\n\n   dt (25)\n\nt \\*\n\n\\= ∫ 0 ∞   1- c t , out c\n\n0\n\n   dt (26)\n\n3.4 Analytical methods\n\nDetermination of the concentrations of LA in the aqueous solution was carried\n\nout by HPLC (Waters 2685 systems, Waters Corp., USA) with a refractive index\n\ndetector (Waters 2414) on an Aminex HPX-87H anion exchange column (300 mm ×\n\n7.8 mm, Bio-Rad Corp., USA ) using 5 mmol/L sulfuric acid as mobile phase at a\n\nflow rate of 0.5 mL/min. The column temperature was maintained at 55oC. External\n\nstandards were established for calibration. Samples (10 µL) were injected by an\n\n12\n\nauto-sampler \\[39\\].\n\nIn order to ensure reliability and improve accuracy of the experimental data in\n\nthis study, equilibrium and fixed-bed column studies on LA adsorption were\n\nconducted in triplicate and averaged values of three data sets were reported. Relative\n\nstandard deviations were found to be less than ±10%.\n\n4\\. Results and Discussion\n\n4.1 Adsorption isotherm\n\nFig. 1 presents the adsorption isotherm data (points) of LA onto SY-01 resin at\n\n298 K along with the Langmuir, and Freundlich isotherm model. The corresponding\n\nisotherm parameters obtained from non-linear regression of experimental data are\n\nshown in Table 2. Compared with correlation coefficients (R2) and RMSE, Langmuir\n\nisotherm fits the experimental data well, indicating that homogenous adsorption of the\n\nLA onto the SY-01 resin occurred. Furthermore, the maximum equilibrium adsorption\n\namount was observed to be 103.74 mg/g.\n\n4.2 Effect of feed flow rate on the LA breakthrough curves\n\nThe effect of feed flow rate on the breakthrough curves of LA adsorption on the\n\nSY-01 resin at constant initial LA concentration of 5.0 g/L is illustrated in Fig. 2. The\n\noperating temperature was held constant at 298 K. The diameter and length of the\n\nfixed-bed column was 2.6 cm and 9.42 cm, respectively. The obtained characteristic\n\nparameters for each experimental breakthrough curve were summarized in Table 3. As\n\nshown in Fig. 2 (and in Table 3), the breakthrough and saturation time (tb and ts)\n\ndecreased significantly as the feed flow rate increased. This phenomenon may be due\n\nto the fact that a higher feed flow rate results in a higher Reynolds number and may\n\nprolong the mass transfer zone, leading to a shorter breakthrough time and thereby\n\ndecreasing the diffusion coefficient. Similarly, it can be observed from Table 3 that the\n\n13\n\ndynamic adsorption capacity of LA at the breakthrough point (qb) was reduced from\n\n50.41 mg/g to 31.51 mg/g when the feed flow rate was increased from 1.0 mL/min to\n\n5.0 mL/min. One possible reason for this phenomenon is that the LA molecules will\n\nnot have sufficient time to diffuse in the pores of SY-01 resin and leave the fixed-bed\n\nbefore reaching the equilibrium at higher feed flow rate, leading to low efficiency of\n\nthe fixed-bed column (see Table 3). These observations are consistent with those\n\nreported elsewhere \\[22, 40, 41\\]. Notably, the slopes of LA breakthrough curves\n\nbecame steeper as the feed flow rate varied from 1.0 mL/min to 5.0 mL/min. A\n\nprobable explanation for this phenomenon is that the degree of turbulence and mixing\n\nincreases and breakthrough occurs faster with an increasing feed flow rate \\[22, 42\\]. In\n\naddition, the “tailing” of LA breakthrough curves (i.e., a slow approach of ct,out/c0\n\ntoward 1) at lower feed flow rate was also observed in Fig. 2, which most likely\n\nbecause of slow intraparticle diffusion within the pores of resin particle \\[41, 43\\].\n\nTherefore, it is advisable to select relatively lower feed flow rate for the separation of\n\nLA because the contact time and the treated LA volume will increase. However, much\n\nlower feed flow rates, resulting in the long operating time, are not accepted when the\n\nproduction efficiency and cost need to be taken into account. In next studies, an\n\nappropriate feed flow rate (2.0 mL/min) was required to satisfy the separation of LA.\n\n4.3 Effect of initial LA concentration on the LA breakthrough curves\n\nFig. 3 displays the effect of initial LA concentration ranging from 1.0 g/L to 10.0\n\ng/L on the breakthrough curves of LA adsorption on SY-01 resin at feed flow rate of\n\n2.0 mL/min at 298 K. The diameter and length of the fixed-bed column was 2.6 cm\n\nand 9.42 cm, respectively. The related characteristic parameters for each experimental\n\nbreakthrough curve were also shown in Table 3. The Fig. 3 and Table 3 showed that,\n\nthe breakthrough curves of LA were dispersed and breakthrough appeared slowly at\n\n14\n\nlower initial LA concentration. The breakthrough times, tb, for initial LA\n\nconcentration values of 1.0, 5.0, and 10.0 g/L were found to be 248, 166, and 20 min,\n\nrespectively. Furthermore, the slope of breakthrough curves became steeper and the\n\nsaturation of the bed was faster as initial LA concentration increased. These\n\nphenomenon can be ascribed to the change of concentration gradient, which affects\n\nthe saturation rate and breakthrough time \\[44\\]. As the initial LA concentration\n\nincreased, not only did LA adsorption rate increased, but also did the driving force for\n\nmass transfer enhanced, resulting in a decrease of the mass transfer zone (see Table 3).\n\n4.4 Effect of fixed-bed column length on the LA breakthrough curves\n\nThe effect of fixed-bed column length on the LA breakthrough curves were\n\ninvestigated at various fixed-bed column lengths (Lc) of 5.65, 9.42 and 16.96 cm. The\n\ndiameter the fixed-bed columns were 2.6 cm. The feed solution contained an initial\n\nLA concentration of 5.0 g/L in all cases and was permitted to pass through the\n\nfixed-bed column with a constant inlet flow rate of 2.0 mL/min. The experimental\n\nbreakthrough curves at various Lc were shown in Fig. 4 and the related characteristic\n\nparameters for each experimental breakthrough curve were listed in Table 3. It can be\n\nobserved from Fig. 4 and Table 3 that the breakthrough time (tb) and the dynamic\n\nadsorption capacity of LA at the breakthrough point (qb) increased with increasing Lc\n\nas expected. The reason for this behavior can be explained that a higher fixed-bed\n\ncolumn length corresponds to a larger amount of resin in the column, and more active\n\nresin adsorbent sites are available for adsorption of the adsorbate with the increase of\n\nLc \\[45\\]. Apart from this, LA has more time to be in contact with the resin particle at a\n\nhigh fixed-bed column length, resulting in a higher efficiency (ψ) of LA molecules in\n\nthe fixed-bed column (see Table 3). Notably, it also can be observed from Fig. 4 that\n\nthe shape of the breakthrough curves was changed slightly from steep concave to flat\n\n15\n\nconcave as the fixed-bed column length increased from 5.65 to 16.96 cm, leading to a\n\nbroadened mass transfer zone (see Table 3). However, too high fixed-bed column\n\nlength is not advised for a single fixed-bed column. This is because the flow rate at\n\nthe outlet of column was unstable when the fixed-bed column length was too high due\n\nto a higher flow resistance \\[46\\].\n\n4.5 Effect of fixed-bed column diameter on the LA breakthrough curves\n\nThe effect of the fixed-bed column diameter on the LA breakthrough curves was\n\nstudied with various fixed-bed column diameters of 1.6, 2.6 and 5.5 cm, which was\n\nshown in Fig. 5. The related characteristic parameters for each experimental\n\nbreakthrough curve were given in Table 3. In all tests, the fixed-bed column length\n\nwas maintained at 9.42 cm. The inlet feed flow rate was remained at 2.0 mL/min and\n\nthe initial LA concentration at 5.0 g/L for all three cases. From Fig. 5 and Table 3, it is\n\nclear that the breakthrough time (tb) and the dynamic adsorption capacity of LA at the\n\nbreakthrough point (qb) increased with increasing Dc. Furthermore, the adsorption\n\nefficiency of LA molecules in the fixed-bed column (ψ) increased with increasing\n\ncolumn diameter (see Table 3). This may be due to the fact that a larger number of\n\nSY-01 resin existed along the cross sectional area of the column at a wider diameter\n\ncolumn which was beneficial to the contact of LA molecules to the SY-01 resin,\n\nresulting in a decrease of the mass transfer zone \\[45\\].\n\n4.6 Mathematical modeling for adsorption behaviors of LA on SY-01 resin\n\nThe fixed-bed column chromatographic model was established on the basis of\n\nthe obtained isotherm equation as well as the kinetic model. The chromatographic\n\nmodel (GRM) would be verified by comparison of the model predictions to the\n\nexperimental data at various operating conditions in terms of feed flow rate, initial LA\n\nconcentration, fixed-bed column length, and fixed-bed column diameter in this section.\n\n16\n\nComparison of experimental and theoretical breakthrough curves for LA adsorption\n\non SY-01 resin packed column using GRM at different operating conditions were\n\nsimultaneously illustrated in Fig. 6A-D. The related model parameters in terms of the\n\naxial dispersion coefficient (Dax), the molecular diffusivity of levulinic acid (Dm), the\n\nexternal mass transfer coefficient (kfilm), the pore diffusion coefficient (Dp), Peclet\n\nnumber (Pe), Stanton number (St), Reynodls number (Re), and Biot number (Bi) were\n\nlisted in Table 4. Clearly, the GRM-simulated breakthrough profiles matched well\n\nwith the experimental data at various operating conditions according to the values of\n\nAAD in the range of 0 to 1.0 (see Table 4 and Fig. 6A-D) \\[27\\]. As can be also seen\n\nfrom Table 4 that the axial dispersion coefficient (Dax), the external mass transfer\n\ncoefficient (kfilm) and Reynolds number increased with increasing feed flow rate and\n\ndecreasing fixed-bed column diameter but kept constant for various initial LA\n\nconcentrations and fixed-bed column lengths. This is because the values of Dax, kfilm,\n\nand Re are determined by hydrodynamic conditions and primarily depend on fluid\n\nvelocity for specific adsorbate-adsorbent system \\[27, 47\\]. The fluid velocity increase\n\nwith increasing feed flow rate and decreasing fixed-bed column diameter but remain\n\nconstant for different initial LA concentrations and fixed-bed column lengths.\n\nHowever, the effective pore diffusion coefficient (Dp) obtaining from the simulation\n\nof adsorption kinetics were nearly the same at various operating conditions when the\n\nsame adsorbent was used \\[16\\]. This phenomenon was consistent with reports from\n\nmany other researchers \\[27, 48-50\\]. It is worth noting that the Bi (see Table 4),\n\nreflecting the significance of the external mass transfer versus intraparticle mass\n\ntransfer resistance \\[51\\], are more than 10 at various different operating conditions,\n\nindicating the pore diffusion resistance is dominant phenomenon \\[27, 52\\]. This could\n\nbe attributed to the specific pore structure of the resin. According to the IUPAC\n\n17\n\nclassification of pore diameters, the SY-01 resin contains well-developed pore\n\nstructure in both the region of micropores (dp ≤ 2 nm) and mesopores (2 nm \\\u003c dp \\\u003c50\n\nnm) (see Fig. 7) \\[21\\]. For a better intuitively understanding of the LA adsorption\n\nprocess in packed column, Fig. 8 schematically displays the concentration profile\n\npredicted by the verified GRM for LA in the liquid phase at all locations within the\n\nfixed-bed column (T=298 K, cf=5.0 g/L, Qf =2.0 mL/min, Lc=9.42 cm, Dc=2.6 cm).\n\nWhern the fixed-bed column adsorption reaction was performed for 10, 30, 50, 100,\n\n150, and 200 min, the LA were diffused to the fixed-bed column length of 1.88, 3.01,\n\n3.96, 5.09, 5.84, 7.72 and 9.42 cm, respectively. Furthermore, the time required to\n\nreach the saturation at the outlet of fixed-bed was within 350 min.\n\n5\\. Conclusions\n\nThe adsorption of LA onto SY-01 resin from aqueous solutions was\n\nsystematically investigated under various operating conditions. Adsorption parameters\n\nfor Langmuir and Freundlich isotherms were determined and the equilibrium data\n\nwere found to be best described by the Langmuir isotherm model. The effects of feed\n\nflow rate, initial LA concentration, fixed-bed column length and fixed-bed column\n\ndiameter on the adsorption breakthrough behavior of LA in the fixed-bed column\n\nwere investigated. It was found that an increase in fixed-bed column length, fixed-bed\n\ncolumn diameter, initial LA concentration and a decrease in feed flow rate improved\n\nthe adsorption performance. GRM was used successfully to describe and simulate the\n\nadsorption behaviors of LA in a fixed-bed column under various operating conditions.\n\nIn addition, the values of Bi at different operating conditions are all more than 10,\n\nsuggesting that intraparticle diffusion is the rate controlling step for the adsorption of\n\nLA on the resin. Based on the above results, the GRM model can facilitate the design\n\nof fixed-bed column simply by reducing the number of experiments required to\n\n18\n\nevaluate various operating conditions and design parameters, meanwhile saving the\n\nexperimental expense and improving work efficiency.\n\nAcknowledgements\n\nThe authors acknowledge the financial support of the Science and Technology\n\nPlanning Project of Guangdong Province, China (2016A010104009,\n\n2016A010105016), the Project of National Natural Science Foundation of China\n\n(51508547, 21606229), the Youth   "fileContent": "A critical review of breakthrough models with analytical solutions in a fixed-bed column \n\nA critical review of breakthrough models with analytical solutions in a fixed-bed column\n\nQili Hu a,\\*, Xingyue Yang a, Leyi Huang a, Yixi Li a, Liting Hao b, Qiuming Pei c, Xiangjun Pei a,\\*\n\na State Environmental Protection Key Laboratory of Synergetic Control and Joint Remediation for Soil & Water Pollution, College of Ecology and Environment, Chengdu University of Technology, Chengdu 610059, China b Key Laboratory of Urban Stormwater System and Water Environment, Ministry of Education/Sino-Dutch R\\&D Centre for Future Wastewater Treatment Technologies, Beijing University of Civil Engineering and Architecture, Beijing 100044, PR China c Faculty of Geosciences and Environmental Engineering, Southwest Jiaotong University, Chengdu 611756, China\n\nA R T I C L E I N F O\n\nEditor: Xiaohong Guan\n\nKeywords: Adsorption Fixed bed Breakthrough curve Fractal-like kinetics Controversy\n\nA B S T R A C T\n\nThe modeling and simulation of the breakthrough curves in a fixed-bed column play a vital role in evaluation of the adsorption capability and prediction of the dynamic adsorption behaviors. With the popular use of the breakthrough models, the relevant mistakes and inconsistencies also rise and are repeated in subsequent pub- lications. This work conducts a profound review of the fundamental principles of continuous adsorption and the underlying assumptions, curve characteristics, intrinsic relationships and application scope of the widely used breakthrough models with analytical solutions. Besides, the physical meanings of the model parameters are clarified, the relevant mistakes and controversies in the fixed-bed studies are addressed and the reasons for the asymmetric breakthrough curves are discussed. Error statistics and residual plot are a good measure of the goodness of fit. The F-test and Akaike’s information criterion (AIC) can be used to compare the fitting results from two models for the same dataset. The fractal-like kinetics provides new insights into the diffusion-limited adsorption process in a heterogeneous system. Some empirical breakthrough models are also considered to be an alternative method to description of the dynamic adsorption behaviors. This review aims to help beginners better understand and use the breakthrough models.\n\n1\\. Introduction\n\nAdsorption is a phase transfer process that means the enrichment of chemical species on the surface of the porous adsorbents \\[1\\]. It has become an indispensable unit operation to eliminate impurities or un- desirable substances and recover compounds with high added value from municipal and industrial wastewaters \\[2\\]. The engineered adsorption processes more adopt the fixed bed rather than a batch reactor since the former has greater advantages such as simple mode of operation, efficient utilization of the adsorbent capacity, suitable treatment of large volumes, and ease of scaling up \\[3–5\\]. Compared with the traditional design-build-test approach, the design and optimization of an adsorption system increasingly rely on the mathematical modeling and simulation to save cost and time \\[6\\]. It is desirable to carry out the mathematical modeling of mass transfer, chemical reaction and ther- modynamic equilibrium during adsorption process \\[7\\]. The rigorous prediction of the breakthrough curve contributes to understanding the\n\ndynamic adsorption behaviors and transport properties and avoiding the extensive experiments that tend to be expensive and time-consuming \\[8\\]. The accuracy of prediction is related to the quality and availabil- ity of the experimental data and to the underlying assumptions and rational approximations of the mathematical models.\n\nIt is inherently difficult to develop a mathematical model to accu- rately describe the dynamic adsorption behaviors in a fixed-bed column because the concentration profiles are a function of both space and time in the liquid and solid phases \\[9\\]. The phenomenological model estab- lished by the mass balance has the potential to identify mechanisms related to the mass transfer and the ability to make predictions outside of the scope of evaluation for obtaining the model parameters \\[10\\]. Although more general and mathematically rigid, it requires compli- cated numerical solutions \\[11\\]. Under initial and boundary conditions, the complete analytical solution of partial differential equations involved in phenomenological model is not available. If the goal is to accurately predict the breakthrough behaviors in a fixed-bed column, the use of simpler and more tractable models that avoid the need for\n\n\\* Corresponding authors.\n\nE-mail addresses: huqili@cdut.edu.cn (Q. Hu), peixj0119@tom.com (X. Pei).\n\nhttps://doi.org/10.1016/j.jwpe.2024.105065 Received 16 November 2023; Received in revised form 10 February 2024; Accepted 23 February 2024\n\nAvailable online 29 February 2024 2214-7144/© 2024 Elsevier Ltd. All rights reserved.\n\nContents lists available at ScienceDirect\n\nJournal of Water Process Engineering\n\njournal homepage: www.elsevier.com/locate/jwpe\n\nJournal of Water Process Engineering 59 (2024) 105065\n\nnumerical solutions appears more suitable and logical \\[12\\]. Analytical solutions can clearly present the dependence of the process on the operating parameters in a way not possible with numerical solutions \\[13\\]. To this end, it is desirable to develop simple mathematical models to satisfactorily predict the dynamic adsorption behaviors. The Bohart–Adams \\[14\\], Thomas \\[15\\], Yoon–Nelson \\[16\\] and Clark \\[17\\] models are frequently applied for modeling of the breakthrough curves since they have the simple analytical solutions and can be easily line- arized, allowing their free parameters to be estimated by the linear fitting \\[18\\]. Despite different underlying assumptions or empirical ap- proximations, these mathematical models have achieved great success in the modeling of the experimental data from adsorption of heavy metals \\[19\\] and emerging contaminants such as antibiotics \\[20\\] and microplastics \\[21\\].\n\nHowever, with the popular propagation of these models, the relevant problems also come along. The breakthrough models are blindly selected to analyze the experimental data probably due to a lack of the understanding of the fundamental principles of continuous adsorption as well as the underlying assumptions, curve characteristics, intrinsic relationships and application scope of the breakthrough models, resulting in a poor fit or even incorrect conclusions \\[22,23\\]. The main limitation of these models is the assumption of the time-independent rate constant or transport properties, not interpreting the heteroge- neous porous structures of the adsorbent and the changes in diffusive microenvironments caused by the progressive occupation of the adsorption sites by the adsorbate molecules \\[24\\]. Moreover, a major difficult for the use of the breakthrough models is how to accurately estimate the model parameters. A common way to evaluate the goodness of fit is to adopt various error statistics such as the coefficient of deter- mination (R2) \\[25\\], chi-squared value (χ2) \\[26\\] and root of mean squared error (RMSE) \\[27\\]. But, these error statistics are not adequate criteria but the residual plot \\[28\\]. The selection of an appropriate model\n\nQ. q0 equilibrium loading of the bed or adsorption capacity at\n\nsaturation (mg g− 1) qe amount of the solute uptake at equilibrium (mg g− 1) qmax maximum adsorption capacity (mg g− 1) qt amount of the solute uptake at time t (mg g− 1) t1/2 operating time at c/c0 = 0.5 (min) tb breakthrough time (min) ts saturation time (min) Vbed volume of bed (L) β0 external mass-transfer coefficient (min− 1) βa effective kinetic coefficient (min− 1)\n\nJournal of Water Process Engineering 59 (2024) 105065\n\n2\n\na mass-transfer area per unit volume of the bed (cm− 1) A0 fractal-like Clark rate constant (dimensionless) c concentration of the solute at the outlet of column (mg L− 1) Dm molecular diffusivity calculated by the Stokes–Einstein\n\nequation (cm2 min− 1) h fractal-like exponent (dimensionless) k0 fractal-like rate constant kn n-order Bohart–Adams rate constant (Ln mg− n min− 1) kBA,0 fractal-like Bohart–Adams rate constant (L mg− 1 minh− 1) kT,0 fractal-like Thomas rate constant (mL mg− 1 minh− 1) kYN,0 fractal-like Yoon–Nelson rate constant (minh− 1) m weight of the adsorbent in the bed (g) n Freundlich constant (dimensionless) p number of the model parameters t operating time (min) u flow rate per unit cross-sectional area of column (cm\n\nmin− 1) v flow rate (mL min− 1) V volume of the effluent at the outlet (mL) vd linear velocity of movement of the adsorption zone (cm\n\nmin− 1) x bed height (cm) or relative concentration (x = c/c0) z axial position of bed (cm) ε void fraction of bed (dimensionless) μ migration velocity of the concentration front in the bed\n\n(cm min− 1) ρ bulk density of the bed (g L− 1) σa standard deviation of the position of the adsorption zone\n\nfront (cm) τ operating time required to reach 50 % breakthrough (min)\n\nbased on both error statistics and residual plot is relatively rare in fixed- bed studies \\[29\\]. The fitting results from different models for the same dataset have not been quantitatively compared yet. This work attempts to address these problems and controversies to help beginners to better understand the breakthrough models.\n\n2\\. General description of fixed bed\n\nContinuous adsorption is a non-stationary rate-controlled process in a fixed-bed column, i.e. a time- and distance-dependent process \\[30\\]. The fixed bed is one of the basic forms of the dynamic operations in the adsorption field. Compared with the batch operation, the main advan- tage of the fixed-bed adsorption is to more conveniently scale up the adsorption process by the dimensional similarity approach (i.e. geo- metric and kinematic similitude). The fixed-bed adsorber through scaling up can be applied to the treatment of municipal and industrial wastewaters. The fixed-bed adsorption can provide reliable information on the breakthrough time, loss of adsorption capacity during subsequent cycles and acceptable flow rate \\[31\\]. However, it also faces some chal- lenges such as high pressure drop, channeling effects, non-ideal flow behaviors and mass-transfer resistances, limiting its application in wastewater treatment \\[32\\].\n\nAs shown in Fig. 1, the adsorption process takes place when the influent moves through the bed. The bed is divided into three regions: saturation zone, mass-transfer zone (MTZ) and adsorption zone. In the saturation zone, the adsorption of the contaminant reaches the dynamic equilibrium at the solid/solution interface, where the amount adsorbed q0 is in equilibrium with the influent concentration c0. The available capacity of the adsorbent is exhausted and no mass transfer occurs from solution to the adsorbent particles. In the adsorption zone, the adsorbent particles are not loaded by the adsorbate molecules, and the concen- tration of the contaminant is equal to zero. Adsorption occurs only in the\n\nMTZ, in which the adsorbent particles accumulate the adsorbate mole- cules continuously from the feed and the amount adsorbed in the bed increases from zero to q0. The shape and length of the MTZ largely depend on the rate of adsorption and the shape of the isotherm curve \\[33\\]. During the adsorption process, the MTZ travels through the bed with a velocity that is much slower than the influent velocity. The breakthrough occurs for the first time when the MTZ reaches the end of the bed. The operating time required is called the breakthrough time (tb). For a given flow rate, the better adsorbable the contaminant is, the later the breakthrough occurs. In practice, the breakthrough time is often defined as the operating time required to reach the minimum detectable or maximum allowable concentration of the contaminant to be removed \\[34\\]. As time goes on, the MTZ gradually moves forward, while the saturation zone expands. Also, the concentration of the contaminant rapidly rises at the outlet. The saturation is reached when the entire MTZ leaves the column. At this moment, no net adsorption takes place in the bed and the related time is known as the saturation time (ts).\n\nThe profile of the relative concentration of a contaminant at the outlet over time is called the breakthrough curve. The S-shaped break- through curve is a consequence of the decrease in the driving force for mass transfer from the fluid to the solid phase \\[35\\]. The breakthrough curve is a mirror of the MTZ and also affected by the rate of adsorption and the shape of the isotherm curve. To be specific, it is primarily influenced by the operating parameters such as flow rate, influent concentration, bed height, pH and particle size \\[36,37\\]. When the shape of the breakthrough curve is as sharp as possible, the most effective adsorption performance can be obtained \\[38\\]. The position of the breakthrough curve at the t-axis depends on the moving velocity of the MTZ, which in turn depends on the flow velocity and the strength of adsorption. Given that the asymptotic properties of the breakthrough models, they cannot exactly predict the breakthrough time and the saturation time. The operating times at c/c0 = 0.05 and 0.95 are popularly defined as the breakthrough time and the saturation time, respectively \\[39\\].\n\nA phenomenological model for adsorption of water contaminants is developed according to the following assumptions \\[40–42\\]: (i) isothermal condition; (ii) plug flow in the axial direction; (iii) negligible radial dispersion; (iv) linear driving force for the solid adsorption; (v) uniformly spherical adsorbent particles; and (vi) constant geometric dimensions, interstitial velocity and void fraction. The mass balance equation in the liquid phase is expressed as \\[43\\].\n\nQ. Hu et al.\n\nJournal of Water Process Engineering 59 (2024) 105065\n\nFig. 1. DL = udpε\n\n(20ε\n\nDm udp +12)\n\n(2)\n\nThe molecular diffusivity Dm can be calculated by the Sto- kes–Einstein eq. \\[45\\]. The equilibrium loading can be obtained by integration of the measured breakthrough curve.
