Comparison and novelty*\n\nAs mentioned above, the empirical breakthrough models pro-\n\nposed in this work have the ability to describe various multi-\n\ncomponent adsorption systems, showing smaller *R*2 values\n\nand randomly distributed residuals. Compared with the fitting\n\nresults in the original literature, this work provides either smal-\n\nler *R*2 values or better fitting curves. That is, Eqs. [(7) ](#3)and [(8)](#3)\n\nprovide comparable or better fitting performance for the mul-\n\nticomponent adsorption. The model parameters (*k*, s, *k*\\*, s\\*\n\nand *c*) may be regarded as the lumped parameters that embed\n\nsome physical processes and operating features. In principle,\n\nthe coupled partial differential equations require the use of\n\nthe numerical simulation to solve the breakthrough curves\n\nunder different initial and boundary conditions. Many experi-\n\nmental parameters need to be collected such as the column\n\nparameters, initial operating conditions, bed voidage and\n\nadsorbent density. The experimental workload is large and cal-\n\nculation process is very complicated. This simulation method\n\nis greatly limited in the practical application. The two empiri-\n\ncal breakthrough models have the simple mathematical forms,\n\nlimited number of the model parameters and good fitting qual-\n\nity. Eqs. [(7) ](#3)and [(8) ](#3)are established by the superposition of the\n\nlogistic and Gompertz functions and their first-order deriva-\n\ntives respectively. The primitive function and its first-order\n\nderivative reveal the adsorption and displacement processes\n\nrespectively. The model parameters *ki*, s*i*, *ki*\\*, s*i*\\* and *ci* are\n\ndirectly obtained by the nonlinear regression rather than the\n\ncomplicated numerical solution. The equilibrium loading is\n\neasily calculated when the five model parameters are known.\n\nCompared with the coupled partial differential equations, the\n\nexperimental and calculated workload will decrease largely.\n\nThe two empirical models are successfully applied to the\n\ngas–solid and liquid–solid adsorption by adjusting the model\n\nparameters.\n\n**4. Conclusions**\n\nThis work establishes the two empirical multicomponent breakthrough\n\nmodels based on the logistic and Gompertz functions. They have the\n\nsimple mathematical forms, limited number of the model parameters\n\nand good fitting performance. The two empirical models provide smal-\n\nler *R*2 values and randomly distributed residuals for description of\n\nthree binary and four ternary adsorption systems, including the gas–\n\nsolid and liquid–solid adsorption. The model parameters *ki*, s*i*, *ki*\\*,\n\ns*i*\\* and *ci* are regarded as the lumped parameters that embed some\n\nphysical processes and operating features. Eqs. [(12) ](#4)and [(13) ](#4)give the\n\nquantitative calculation of the equilibrium loading for each component\n\nin a multicomponent system. The model parameters and equilibrium\n\nloading are easily obtained by Origin and MATLAB software. This\n\nwork may be expected to provide an alternative method for prediction\n\nof the multicomponent breakthrough curves.\n\n**Declaration of Competing Interest**\n\nThe authors declare that they have no known competing\n\nfinancial interests or personal relationships that could have\n\nappeared to influence the work reported in this paper.\n\n**Acknowledgements**\n\nThe authors gratefully acknowledge the financial support from\n\nthe State Environmental Protection Key Laboratory of Syner-\n\ngetic Control and Joint Remediation for Soil & Water Pollu-\n\ntion Open Funding (No. GHBK-2021-008).\n\n**Appendix A.   "fileContent": "Prediction of breakthrough curves in a fixed-bed column based on normalized Gudermannian and error functions \n\n|  |\n| :-: |\n| [**Page 1**]() |\n\nPrediction of breakthrough curves in a fixed-bed column based on\n\nnormalized Gudermannian and error functions\n\nQili Hu[a](#1), Qi Huang[a](#1), Danni Yang[b](#1),⁎, Hengyuan Liu[c](#1)\n\na *College of Ecology and Environment, Chengdu University of Technology, Chengdu 610059, China*\n\nb *College of Architecture and Environment, Sichuan University, Chengdu 610065, Sichuan, China*\n\nc *College of Chemistry and Life Sciences, Chifeng University, Chifeng 024000, China*\n\na b s t r a c t\n\na r t i c l e i n f o\n\n*Article history:*\n\nReceived 21 July 2020\n\nReceived in revised form 9 November 2020\n\nAccepted 11 December 2020\n\nAvailable online xxxx\n\n*Keywords:*\n\nAdsorption\n\nFixed bed\n\nGudermannian function\n\nFractal-like kinetics\n\nResidual plot\n\nThe curve characteristics of the breakthrough models were constrained by the mathematical laws that the\n\nmodels themselves followed. The existing breakthrough models such as Bohart–Adams, Thomas, Yoon–Nelson,\n\nClark and modified dose-response models may be not enough to describe all adsorption systems. This work\n\nestablished the Gudermannian and error models only containing the two parameters. The fractal-like\n\nGudermannian and fractal-like error models could describe the diffusion-limited process on the heterogeneous\n\nsurfaces. The applicability of these models was evaluated by the adjusted coefficient of determination (Adj.\n\n*R*2), reduced chi-square value (χ2) and residual plot. The introduction of the fractal-like kinetics made the\n\nGudermannian and error models have the ability to describe the asymmetric curves. Among these models, the\n\nfractal-like Gudermannian model provided the best fit for norfloxacin adsorption on granular activated carbon\n\n(Adj. *R*2 = 0.9991 and χ2 = 1.26 × 10−4) and methylene blue adsorption on porous silica microsphere (Adj.\n\n*R*2 = 0.9997 and χ2 = 6.35 × 10−5). The breakthrough models proposed in this work were an important supple-\n\nment of adsorption model studies.\n\n© 2020 Elsevier B.V. All rights reserved.\n\n**1. Introduction**\n\nFor engineered adsorption processes, a fixed-bed adsorber is fre-\n\nquently used due to its two important advantages: A high driving\n\nforce remains over the whole process and the adsorbate is totally re-\n\nmoved until the breakthrough occurs \\[[1](#6)\\]. The dynamic adsorption be-\n\nhaviors of the adsorbate in a fixed-bed column can be described by\n\nthe effluent concentration-time profile, i.e. the breakthrough curve.\n\nThe design of an adsorption process usually requires the development\n\nof a model that can accurately predict the breakthrough curves in a\n\ngiven fixed-bed system \\[[2](#6)\\]. This experimentally verified model is then\n\nemployed to explain the effects of the process parameters on the ad-\n\nsorption performance, which is responsible for the preference of a\n\nbreakthrough model by many researchers.\n\nIn the last two decades, the Bohart–Adams \\[[3](#6)\\], Thomas \\[[4](#6)\\], Yoon–\n\nNelson \\[[5](#6)\\], Clark \\[[6](#6)\\] and modified dose-response \\[[7](#6)\\] models have\n\nbeen widely employed to predict the breakthrough curves without\n\ncomplex numerical solution. They only contain two or three undeter-\n\nmined coefficients, which can be easily obtained by the nonlinear re-\n\ngression method. In some cases, the Bohart–Adams, Thomas and\n\nYoon–Nelson models have a distinct imperfection: A poor fit occurs\n\nwhen correlated with the experimental data with the asymmetric distri-\n\nbution \\[[8–11](#6)\\]. The three models are the logistic function in mathematical\n\nnature, representing a symmetric sigmoidal curve. In general, a good fit\n\nrequires that the model curves must approach the experimental data\n\n\\[[12](#6)\\]. In other words, the logistic function may be invalid for an asymmet-\n\nric breakthrough curve. To address this issue, it is of prime necessity to\n\ndevelop new breakthrough models using other sigmoidal functions.\n\nIn this work, the Gudermannian and error functions are selected to\n\nestablish new breakthrough models. The fractal-like kinetics is used to\n\nfurther modify the Gudermannian and error models, leading to the\n\nfractal-like Gudermannian and fractal-like error models. Effects of the\n\nfractal-like exponent on the breakthrough curves and rate profiles are\n\ninvestigated. The fitting quality of these models is evaluated by the ad-\n\njusted coefficient of determination, reduced chi-square value (χ2) and\n\nresidual plot.\n\n**2. Theoretical analysis**\n\n*2.1. Logistic model*\n\nThe Bohart–Adams model is initially applied to the description of\n\nchlorine uptake on charcoal in a fixed-bed system and assumes that\n\nthe rate of reaction is proportional to the residual capacity of the\n\n[Journal of Molecular Liquids 323 (2021) 115061](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.molliq.2020.115061)\n\n⁎ Corresponding author at: College of Architecture and Environment, Sichuan\n\nUniversity, Chengdu 610065, Sichuan, China\n\n*E-mail addresses:* [huqili@cdut.edu.cn ](mailto:huqili@cdut.edu.cn)(Q. Hu), [ydanielle@163.com ](mailto:ydanielle@163.com)(D. Yang).\n\n\u003chttps://doi.org/10.1016/j.molliq.2020.115061\u003e\n\n0167-7322/© 2020 Elsevier B.V. All rights reserved.\n\nContents lists available at [ScienceDirect](http://www.sciencedirect.com/science/journal/)\n\nJournal of Molecular Liquids\n\njournal homepage: [www.elsevier.com/locate/molliq](http://www.elsevier.com/locate/molliq)\n\n-----\n\n|  |\n| :-: |\n| [**Page 2**]() |\n\nadsorbent in the stationary phase and to the concentration of the adsor-\n\nbate in the mobile phase \\[[3](#6)\\].\n\n*c*\n\n*c*0\n\n\\=\n\nexp *k*BA*c*0*t*\n\n(\n\n)\n\nexp *k*BA*a*0*x*\n\n*u*\n\n\\+ exp *kBAc*0*t*\n\n(\n\n)−1\n\n(1)\n\nThe Thomas model is widely used for the modeling of the break-\n\nthrough curves in a single-solute fixed-bed system, which assumes\n\nthat \\[[4](#6)\\]: (i) The removal of the solute follows the second-order revers-\n\nible reaction kinetics and the Langmuir isotherm; (ii) the rate of adsorp-\n\ntion is determined by chemical effects (not diffusion); and (iii) the pore\n\nvolume of the adsorbent and axial dispersion are neglected.\n\n*c*\n\n*c*0\n\n\\=\n\n1\n\n1 + exp *k*T*q*0*m*\n\nν\n\n−*k*T*c*0*t*\n\n(2)\n\nThe Yoon–Nelson model assumes that the rate of decrease in\n\nthe probability of adsorption for each molecule is proportional to\n\nthe product of the probability of adsorption and the probability\n\nof breakthrough \\[[5](#6)\\].\n\n*c*\n\n*c*0\n\n\\=\n\n1\n\n1 + exp *k*YN τ−*t*\n\n(\n\n)\n\n\\[\n\n\\]\n\n(3)\n\nThe recent research work proves that the Bohart–Adams, Thomas\n\nand Yoon–Nelson models are mathematically equivalent, representing\n\na logistic function *y* =\n\n1\n\n1+ exp −*x*\n\n(\n\n)\n\n\\[[13](#6),[14](#6)\\]. The three models provide\n\nthe same fitting curve when correlated with the same set of the exper-\n\nimental data. Their parameters are interchangeable according to the fol-\n\nlowing relations.\n\n*k*YN = *k*T*c*0 = *k*BA*c*0\n\n(4)\n\nτ =\n\n*q*0*m*\n\nν*c*0\n\n\\=\n\n1\n\n*k*BA*c*0\n\nln exp\n\n*k*BA*a*0*x*\n\n*u*\n\n−1\n\n(5)\n\nOne can readily see that the Yoon–Nelson model does not contain any\n\nspecific operating conditions (*v*, *u*, *c*0 and *m*), and thus the model param-\n\neters *k*YN and τ are easily determined by the nonlinear regression\n\nmethod. Eq. [(4) ](#2)and Eq. [(5) ](#2)can be used to directly calculate the parame-\n\nters *k*T, *k*BA, *q*0 and *a*0 under given operating conditions, greatly reducing\n\nthe complexity of the curve fitting. To concisely report the fitting results,\n\nthe Bohart–Adams, Thomas and Yoon–Nelson models are collectively\n\ncalled the logistic model that contains the two parameters *k* and τ.\n\n*2.2. Normalized Gudermannian and error breakthrough models*\n\nThe Gudermannian function relates the circular and hyperbolic func-\n\ntions without the use of functions of imaginary argument, and the error\n\nfunction is a non-elementary function that often appears in probability\n\ntheory, heat conduction and vibrating beams \\[[15](#6)\\], which are defined as\n\n*y* = gd *x*( )=\n\nZ *x*\n\n0\n\n1\n\ncosh *t*( )\n\nd*t* = arctan sinh *x*( )\n\n\\[\n\n\\]\n\n(6)\n\n*y* = erf *x*( )=\n\n2\n\nffiffiffi\n\nπ\n\n/\n\nZ *x*\n\n0\n\ne−*t*2 d*t*\n\n(7)\n\nIt can be clearly seen from [Fig. 1](#2)a that the Gudermannian and error\n\nfunctions are odd functions, i.e. symmetry with respect to the origin.\n\nThe model curves are monotone and bounded S-shaped curves, which\n\nare formally regarded as a mirror of the breakthrough curves. To facilely\n\nidentify the differences between Gudermannian (or error) and logistic\n\n**Nomenclature**\n\n*A*\n\nClark constant\n\n*a*\n\nmodified dose-response constant\n\n*a*0\n\nadsorption capacity (mg L−1)\n\nAdj. *R*2\n\nadjusted coefficient of determination\n\n*b*\n\nmodified dose-response constant (min−1)\n\n*c*\n\neffluent solute concentration (mg L−1)\n\n*c*0\n\ninfluent solute concentration (mg L−1)\n\n*h*\n\nfractal-like exponent\n\n*k*0\n\nfractal-like rate constant (min−(1−*h*))\n\n*k*BA\n\nBohart–Adams rate constant (L mg−1 min−1)\n\n*k*T\n\nThomas rate constant (L mg−1 min−1)\n\n*k*YN\n\nYoon–Nelson rate constant (min−1)\n\n*m*\n\nabsorbent mass filled in the column (g)\n\n*n*\n\nFreundlich constant or number of data points\n\n*q*0\n\nsaturation capacity (mg g−1)\n\n*r*\n\nClark constant (min−1)\n\n*t*\n\noperating time (min)\n\n*u*\n\nlinear flow velocity (cm min−1)\n\n*v*\n\nflow rate (mL min−1)\n\n*x*\n\nbed height (cm)\n\nτ\n\noperating time required to reach 50%\n\nbreakthrough (min)\n\nτ0\n\noperating time required to reach the inflection point of\n\nthe breakthrough curve along *t*-axis (min)\n\nχ2\n\nreduced chi-square value\n\n**Fig. 1.** Schematic diagrams of the logistic, Gudermannian and error functions: (a) before and (b) after normalization.\n\n*Q. Hu, Q. Huang, D. Yang et al.*\n\n*Journal of Molecular Liquids 323 (2021) 115061*\n\n2\n\n-----\n\n|  |\n| :-: |\n| [**Page 3**]() |\n\nfunctions, the range of the Gudermannian and error functions is also\n\nconfined to \\[0,1\\]. The normalized Gudermannian and normalized error\n\nfunctions are expressed as\n\n*y* =\n\n1\n\n2\n\n1 +\n\n2\n\nπ\n\narctan sinh *x*( )\n\n\\[\n\n\\]\n\n(8)\n\n*y* =\n\n1\n\n2\n\n1 + erf *x*( )\n\n\\[\n\n\\]\n\n(9)\n\nAs shown in [Fig. 1](#2)b, the logistic, Gudermannian and error functions\n\nshare similar curve characteristics. The main differences are the degree\n\nof curvature and the rate of convergence. Under this circumstance, these\n\ncurves may correspond to different fixed-bed adsorption systems. It is\n\nexpected that the breakthrough models established based on the\n\nGudermannian and error functions may provide an alternative method\n\nfor the modeling of some breakthrough curves in a fixed-bed column.\n\nIn any regression analysis, the number of the data points must be\n\nmuch greater than that of the parameters to be estimated, and the ex-\n\nperimental effort required for the parameters estimation increases\n\nwith the increase in the number of the parameters. More parameters\n\ndo not necessarily lead to higher fitting quality because the goodness\n\nof fit is limited by the data scatter resulted from the unavoidable exper-\n\nimental errors \\[[1](#6)\\]. A breakthrough model containing too many parame-\n\nters may be inconvenient to the practical application due to the\n\nrequirement of the complicated calculation. As a result, the new break-\n\nthrough models are established by introducing two parameters *k* and τ\n\ninto the Gudermannian (Eq. [(8)](#3)) and error (Eq. [(9)](#3)) functions. Let *y* =\n\n*c*/*c*0, *x* = *t*, then\n\n*c*\n\n*c*0\n\n\\=\n\n1\n\n2\n\n1 +\n\n2\n\nπ\n\narctan sinh *k t*−τ\n\n(\n\n)\n\n\\[\n\n\\]\n\n(\n\n)\n\n(10)\n\n*c*\n\n*c*0\n\n\\=\n\n1\n\n2\n\n1 + erf *k t*−τ\n\n(\n\n)\n\n\\[\n\n\\]\n\n(\n\n)\n\n(11)\n\nSimilar to the Yoon–Nelson model, the parameters *k* and τ de-\n\ntermine the degree of curvature and the location of the curve\n\nalone, respectively \\[[13](#6)\\]. The Gudermannian and error break-\n\nthrough models do not contain any detailed information about\n\nthe operating conditions, the characteristics of the adsorbent and\n\nadsorbates and the physical properties of an adsorption bed. Thus,\n\n*k* and τ may be regarded as the lumped parameters that are related\n\nto the physical processes and operating conditions.\n\n*2.3. Fractal-like Gudermannian and error breakthrough models*\n\nAs mentioned above, the Gudermannian and error functions\n\nrepresent a symmetric sigmoidal curve. However, in the adsorp-\n\ntion of water pollutants, the breakthrough curves are usually\n\nasymmetric even for the adsorption of single solute \\[[16](#6)\\]. Distinct\n\ndeviations may occur when the normalized Gudermannian and\n\nnormalized error breakthrough models are correlated with the ex-\n\nperimental data with the asymmetric distribution. Currently, the\n\nClark and modified dose-response models can be used to predict\n\n**Fig. 2.** Effects of fractal-like exponent *h* on (a) breakthrough curve and (b) rate profile (0 = *h*1 \\\u003c *h*2 \\\u003c *h*3 \\\u003c *h*4).\n\n*Q. Hu, Q. Huang, D. Yang et al.*\n\n*Journal of Molecular Liquids 323 (2021) 115061*\n\n3\n\n-----\n\n|  |\n| :-: |\n| [**Page 4**]() |\n\nthe asymmetric breakthrough curves. The Clark model is based on\n\nthe use of a mass-transfer concept in combination with the\n\nFreundlich isotherm \\[[6](#6)\\].\n\n*c*\n\n*c*0\n\n\\=\n\n1\n\n1 + *A*∙ exp −*rt*\n\n(\n\n)\n\n\\[\n\n\\] 1\n\n*n*−1\n\n(12)\n\nThe modified dose-response model, as an empirical model, mini-\n\nmizes the error caused by the Thomas model, especially at lower or\n\nhigher time periods of the breakthrough curve \\[[7](#6)\\].\n\n*c*\n\n*c*0\n\n\\= 1−\n\n1\n\n1 + *bt*\n\n( )*a*\n\n(13)\n\nThe previous work shows that the asymmetric breakthrough curve\n\nis ascribed to the facts that: The intraparticle diffusion is the rate-\n\ndetermining step \\[[1](#6)\\] and the adsorbent consists of two or more constit-\n\nuents of unequal reactivity \\[[3](#6)\\]. All of the above breakthrough models are\n\nbased on a time-independent rate constant. However, Kopelman finds\n\nthat this property is not applicable for the diffusion-limited process on\n\nthe heterogeneous surfaces, further resulting in the occurrence of the\n\nfractal-like kinetics \\[[17](#6),[18](#6)\\]. A time-dependent rate coefficient is\n\nexpressed as\n\n*k* = *k*0*t*−*h* 0 ≤ *h* ≤ 1\n\n(\n\n)\n\n(14)\n\nSubstitution of Eq. [(14) ](#4)into Eq. [(10) ](#3)and Eq. [(11) ](#3)leads to\n\n*c*\n\n*c*0\n\n\\=\n\n1\n\n2\n\n1 +\n\n2\n\nπ\n\narctan sinh *k*0*t*−*h t*−τ0\n\n(\n\n)\n\nh\n\ni\n\n(15)\n\n*c*\n\n*c*0\n\n\\=\n\n1\n\n2\n\n1 + erf *k*0*t*−*h t*−τ0\n\n(\n\n)\n\nh\n\ni\n\nn\n\no\n\n(16)\n\n**3. Results and discussion**\n\n*3.1. Effects of the parameter h on the breakthrough curves*\n\nThis work investigated the effects of the fractal-like exponent *h*\n\non the breakthrough curves by equidistantly adjusting *h* values and\n\nkeeping other parameters unchanged. As shown in [Fig. 2](#3), the de-\n\ngree of curvature decreases with the increase in the *h* values for\n\nthe fractal-like Gudermannian model. The breakthrough curve is\n\nasymmetric when 0 \\\u003c *h* ≤ 1 and it becomes more asymmetric\n\nwhen *h* deviates from 0. Similar conclusions are also applied to\n\nthe fractal-like error model. To better observe the asymmetric\n\nproperties, the first-order derivatives of Eq. [(17) ](#4)and Eq. [(18) ](#4)i.e.\n\nrate equations can be expressed as\n\nd *c*/*c*0\n\n(\n\n)\n\nd*t*\n\n\\=\n\n1\n\nπ ∙ cosh *k*0*t*−*h t*−τ0\n\n(\n\n)\n\n∙*k*0*t*−*h*∙ 1−*h* 1− τ0\n\n*t*\n\n1 + sinh 2 *k*0*t*−*h t*−τ0\n\n(\n\n)\n\n(17)\n\nd *c*/*c*0\n\n(\n\n)\n\nd*t*\n\n\\= exp − *k*0*t*−*h t*−τ0\n\n(\n\n)\n\nh\n\ni2\n\n∙\n\n*k*0*t*−*h*\n\nffiffiffi\n\nπ\n\n/\n\n∙ 1−*h* 1−\n\nτ0\n\n*t*\n\nh\n\ni\n\n(18)\n\nIt is observed that the rate profiles are the asymmetric Gaussian-like\n\ndistribution with widened right-hand sides (0 \\\u003c *h* ≤ 1). The peaks of the\n\nrate profiles are shifted to the left side by a certain distance with the in-\n\ncrease in the *h* values. The introduction of the fractal-like kinetics makes\n\n**Fig. 3.** Norfloxacin adsorption on granular activated carbon (data from Darweesh and Ahmed, 2017): (a) symmetric and (c) asymmetric, methylene blue adsorption on porous silica\n\nmicrosphere (data from Ataei-Germi and Nematollahzadeh, 2016): (b) symmetric and (d) asymmetric.\n\n*Q. Goodness of* fi*t*\n\nIn this work, norfloxacin adsorption on granular activated carbon\n\n\\[[19](#6)\\] and methylene blue adsorption on porous silica microsphere\n\n\\[[10](#6)\\] were selected to evaluate the fitting performance of the\n\nGudermannian, error, fractal-like Gudermannian and fractal-like\n\nerror models. The required experimental data were extracted by\n\nEngauge Digitizer 11.1 software and the model parameters were es-\n\ntimated by Origin 9 software using the Levenberg–Marquardt\n\nmethod. Considering that different models contained different num-\n\nbers of the parameters, the adjusted coefficient of determination\n\n(Adj. *R*2) and reduced chi-square value (χ2) were used as one\n\n**Table 1**\n\nFitting results for adsorption of norfloxacin and methylene blue in a fixed-bed column.\n\nNo.\n\nModels\n\nParameters\n\nNorfloxacin\n\nAdj. *R*2\n\nχ2\n\nMethylene blue\n\nAdj. *R*2\n\nχ2\n\n1\n\nLogistic\n\n*k* (min−1)\n\n8.86 × 10−2\n\n0.9869\n\n1.78 × 10−3\n\n0.12\n\n0.9980\n\n3.78 × 10−4\n\nτ (min)\n\n37.6\n\n54.3\n\n2\n\nGudermannian\n\n*k* (min−1)\n\n7.30 × 10−2\n\n0.9872\n\n1.72 × 10−3\n\n9.91 × 10−2\n\n0.9982\n\n3.40 × 10−4\n\nτ (min)\n\n37.6\n\n54.3\n\n3\n\nError\n\n*k* (min−1)\n\n3.72 × 10−2\n\n0.9854\n\n1.98 × 10−3\n\n5.00 × 10−2\n\n0.9972\n\n5.09 × 10−4\n\nτ (min)\n\n37.8\n\n54.3\n\n4\n\nClark\n\n*A*\n\n4.40 × 10−2\n\n0.9972\n\n3.79 × 10−4\n\n32.5\n\n0.9992\n\n1.44 × 10−4\n\n*r* (min−1)\n\n6.17 × 10−2\n\n9.27 × 10−2\n\n*n*\n\n1.007\n\n1.29\n\n5\n\nModified dose-response\n\n*a*\n\n3.21\n\n0.9990\n\n1.37 × 10−4\n\n6.45\n\n0.9995\n\n8.67 × 10−5\n\n*b* (min−1)\n\n2.82 × 10−2\n\n1.87 × 10−2\n\n6\n\nFractal-like Gudermannian\n\n*k*0 (min−(1−*h*))\n\n0.38\n\n0.9991\n\n1.26 × 10−4\n\n0.62\n\n0.9997\n\n6.35 × 10−5\n\nτ0 (min)\n\n35.6\n\n53.6\n\n*h*\n\n0.46\n\n0.46\n\n7\n\nFractal-like error\n\n*k*0 (min−(1−*h*))\n\n0.23\n\n0.9979\n\n2.80 × 10−4\n\n0.70\n\n0.9988\n\n2.15 × 10−4\n\nτ0 (min)\n\n35.4\n\n53.3\n\n*h*\n\n0.51\n\n0.66\n\nAdsorption of norfloxacin on granular activated carbon (initial concentration = 225 mg L−1, flow rate = 1.5 mL min−1, bed height = 25 cm).\n\nAdsorption of methylene blue on porous silica microsphere (initial concentration = 100 mg L−1, flow rate = 5 mL min−1, bed height = 3 cm).\n\n**Fig. 4.** Residual plots for norfloxacin adsorption on granular activated carbon: (a) symmetric and (c) asymmetric, methylene blue adsorption on porous silica microsphere: (b) symmetric\n\nand (d) asymmetric.\n\n*Q. Hu, Q. Huang, D. Yang et al.*\n\n*Journal of Molecular Liquids 323 (2021) 115061*\n\n5\n\n-----\n\n|  |\n| :-: |\n| [**Page 6**]() |\n\nmeasure of the fitting quality because they eliminated an adverse ef-\n\nfect of the degrees of freedom \\[[20](#6)\\]. The residual analysis was also\n\nconducted to intuitively observe the fitting quality.\n\nAs shown in [Fig. 3](#4), the logistic, Gudermannian and error models pro-\n\nvide a poor fit for norfloxacin adsorption, especially at the initial and final\n\nstages of adsorption. In contrast, the three models provide a relatively\n\ngood fit for methylene blue adsorption. This behavior was mainly attrib-\n\nuted to their limitedfitting ability for the experimental data with the asym-\n\nmetric distribution. It is worth noting that the experimental data for\n\nmethylene blue adsorption approach the symmetric distribution and\n\nthus relatively high Adj. *R*2 and low χ2 values are observed (see [Table 1](#5)).\n\nThe Clark, modified dose-response, fractal-like Gudermannian and\n\nfractal-like error models provide an excellent fit for norfloxacin and meth-\n\nylene blue adsorption with higher Adj.*R*2 and lower χ2 values, which is as-\n\ncribed to the fact that the model curves are in good agreement with the\n\ndistribution of the experimental data. The fractal-like Gudermannian\n\nmodel provided the best fit for norfloxacin adsorption (Adj. *R*2 = 0.9991\n\nand χ2 = 1.26 × 10−4) and methylene blue adsorption (Adj. *R*2 = 0.9997\n\nandχ2 = 6.35 × 10−5). These deviations between the predicted and exper-\n\nimental values can be intuitively reflected by the residual plot. As shown in\n\n[Fig. 4](#5), compared with the symmetric models (logistic, Gudermannian and\n\nerror models), the residuals of the asymmetric models (Clark, modified\n\ndose-response, fractal-like Gudermannian and fractal-like error models)\n\nfluctuate randomly in the vicinity of zero and fall in narrower horizontal\n\nbands. The residuals range from −0.04 to 0.04 and the distribution does\n\nnot show any trend. The order of fitting quality for the symmetric models:\n\nGudermannian \\\u003e logistic \\\u003e error models and that of fitting quality for the\n\nasymmetric models: fractal-like Gudermannian \\\u003e modified dose-\n\nresponse \\\u003e fractal-like error ≈ Clark models.\n\n*3.3. Model assessment*\n\nSince proposed, the Bohart–Adams, Thomas, Yoon–Nelson, Clark\n\nand modified dose-response models has widely used for the modeling\n\nof the breakthrough curves in a fixed-bed column because of their sim-\n\nple mathematical forms, limited number of the model parameters and\n\ngood fitting quality. The recent study expounded the curve characteris-\n\ntics of these models and physical meanings of the model parameters\n\n\\[[13](#6)\\], which was expected to promote the further understanding and de-\n\nvelopment of the breakthrough models. The Bohart–Adams, Thomas\n\nand Yoon–Nelson models were the logistic function in mathematical\n\nnature and often provided a poor fit when correlated with the experi-\n\nmental data with the asymmetric distribution. Similarly, the fitting\n\ncurves provided by the Gudermannian and error models deviated\n\nfrom the experimental data because they also represented the symmet-\n\nric curves. The logistic, Gudermannian and error models may corre-\n\nspond to different fixed-bed adsorption systems. The fractal-like\n\nkinetics could describe the diffusion-limited process on the heteroge-\n\nneous surfaces. Thus, the fractal-like Gudermannian and fractal-like\n\nerror models were able to describe the asymmetric breakthrough\n\ncurves. The significance of this work was to provide an alternative\n\nmethod for the modeling of the breakthrough curves.\n\n**4. Conclusions**\n\nThe logistic (Bohart–Adams, Thomas, Yoon–Nelson), Gudermannian\n\nand error functions represented different types of the sigmoidal curves,\n\nwhich was likely to correspond to different fixed-bed adsorption sys-\n\ntems. The fractal-like Gudermannian and fractal-like error models\n\ncould describe the diffusion-limited process on the heterogeneous sur-\n\nfaces and have the ability to describe the asymmetric breakthrough\n\ncurves. The breakthrough curve became more asymmetric when the\n\nfractal-like exponent *h* deviated from zero. The fractal-like\n\nGudermannian model provided the best fit with the highest Adj. *R*2\n\nvalues among these breakthrough models (Adj. *R*2 = 0.9991 for\n\nnorfloxacin and 0.9997 for methylene blue). The corresponding resid-\n\nuals fluctuated randomly in the vicinity of zero and fell in a narrower\n\nhorizontal band that ranged from −0.04 to 0.04.\n\n**Declaration of Competing Interest**\n\nThe authors declare that they have no known competing financial\n\ninterests or personal relationships that could have appeared to influ-\n\nence the work reported in this paper.\n\n**Acknowledgements**\n\nThe authors gratefully acknowledge the financial support from the\n\nScientific Research Foundation (10912-KYQD2019-08165), the Natural\n\nScience Foundation of Inner Mongolia (No. 2019BS02006), and the Re-\n\nsearch Start-up Funds for the Chifeng University (No. QDJRCYJ022).\n\n**References**\n\n\\[1\\] [E. Hu, Y. Xie, Z. Zhang, Modification of breakthrough models in a continuous-flow fixed-bed column: Mathematical characteristics of breakthrough curves and rate profiles, Separation and Purification Technology (2019), doi: https://doi.org/10.1016/j.seppur.2019.116399\n\nThis is a PDF file of an article that has undergone enhancements after acceptance, such as the addition of a cover page and metadata, and formatting for readability, but it is not yet the definitive version of record. This version will undergo additional copyediting, typesetting and review before it is published in its final form, but we are providing this version to give early visibility of the article. Please note that, during the production process, errors may be discovered which could affect the content, and all legal disclaimers that apply to the journal pertain.\n\n© 2019 Published by Elsevier B.V.\n\nModification of breakthrough models in a continuous-flow fixed-bed column:\n\nMathematical characteristics of breakthrough curves and rate profiles\n\nQili Hua,b, Yanhua Xiea,\\*, Zhenya Zhangb,\\*\n\na College of Environment and Ecology, Chengdu University of Technology, Chengdu 610059, China\n\nb Graduate School of Life and Environmental Sciences, University of Tsukuba, 1-1-1 Tennodai,\n\nTsukuba, Ibaraki 305-8572, Japan\n\n\\* Corresponding author.\n\nE-mail: xieyanhua10@cdut.cn (Y. Xie).\n\nE-mail: zhang.zhenya.fu@u.tsukuba.ac.jp (Z. Zhang).\n\n1\n\nAbstract\n\nIn order to more completely describe mathematical characteristics of the breakthrough curve,\n\nthis work defines the four parameters: Maximum specific breakthrough rate μmax, lag time λ,\n\ninflection point ti and half-operating time t50. The breakthrough models include the Bohart–\n\nAdams, Thomas, Yoon–Nelson, Clark, Wolborska and dose-response models. Attempts are\n\nmade to address mathematical relationships between the breakthrough models, propose modified\n\nbreakthrough models, investigate effects of model parameters on the breakthrough curve and\n\nrate profile and reveal their physical meanings. The fitting performance of the breakthrough\n\nmodels is verified by the adsorption of nitrate on the chitosan-Fe(III) composite. The results\n\nindicate that the model terms q0m/vc0 and a0x/uc0 are the operating time required to reach 50%\n\nbreakthrough. The Clark model has the best fitting performance with high adjusted determination\n\nfactor (Adj. R2 = 0.9976) and low reduced chi-squared value (χ2 = 2.70 × 10−4). In addition, an\n\ninconsistency concerning application of the Wolborska model is proposed to avoid this situation\n\nwhere it is repeated in subsequent publications. This work is expected to help readers better\n\nunderstand the breakthrough models and select the appropriate model to analyze the dynamic\n\nbehaviors in a continuous-flow fixed-bed column.\n\nKeywords: Adsorption; Modification; Breakthrough model; Rate profile; Parameters\n\n2\n\nNomenclature\n\nA dimensionless Clark constant\n\na dimensionless dose-response constant\n\na0 adsorption capacity (mg L−1)\n\nb dose-response constant (min−1)\n\nc effluent solute concentration (mg L−1)\n\nc0 influent solute concentration (mg L−1)\n\ncb concentration of solute at the breakthrough time (mg L−1)\n\nkBA Bohart–Adams rate constant (mL min−1 mg−1)\n\nkT Thomas rate constant (mL min−1 mg−1)\n\nkYN Yoon–Nelson rate constant (min−1)\n\nm absorbent mass filled in the column (g)\n\nn Freundlich constant or number of data points\n\nq0 saturation capacity (mg g−1)\n\nr Clark constant (min−1)\n\nt operating time (min)\n\nt50 half-operating time (min)\n\ntb breakthrough time (min)\n\nti inflection point (min)\n\nts saturation time (min)\n\nu linear flow velocity (cm min−1)\n\nv flow rate (mL min−1)\n\n3\n\nx bed height (cm)\n\nβa kinetic coefficient of the external mass transfer (min−1)\n\nλ lag time (min)\n\nμmax maximum specific breakthrough rate (min−1)\n\nτ operating time required to reach 50% breakthrough (min)\n\nf number of degrees of freedom\n\np number of model parameters\n\ny c/c0\n\nýi predicted value\n\nyi observed value\n\nωi weighting coefficient (ωi = 1/yi)\n\nR2 multiple coefficient of determination (R2 = 1 − RSS/TSS)\n\nAdj. R2 adjusted determination factor\n\nχ2 reduced chi-squared value\n\nTSS total sum of squares\n\nRSS residual sum of squares\n\n4\n\n1\\. Introduction\n\nAdsorption is one of the most widely used technologies for water reuse and wastewater treatment,\n\ninvolving the selective interaction of one or more compounds from aqueous solution on the solid surfaces\n\n\\[1\\]. The mass transfer processes in porous adsorbents include the four steps: Bulk transport, film diffusion,\n\nintraparticle diffusion and adsorptive attachment \\[2\\]. Adsorption has currently been an indispensable unit\n\noperation in industrial application including separation and purification, industrial catalysis and pollution\n\ncontrol \\[3\\]. The importance of adsorption is growing due to the occurrence of emerging contaminants such\n\nas pesticides, industrial compounds, pharmaceuticals and personal care products \\[4,5\\]. The prevalent use\n\nof adsorption stems from its low cost, fast kinetics, low energy for regeneration, insensitivity to toxic\n\nsubstances and complete removal of pollutants even from dilute solution \\[6,7\\].\n\nIn the practical operation of full-scale adsorption process, a continuous-flow fixed-bed column is\n\npreferred \\[8\\]. In such a system, the adsorption of various solutes is a time- and distance-dependent process\n\n\\[9\\]. The mass transfer is favorable due to the existence of the concentration gradient, which is the driving\n\nforce of the adsorption process \\[10\\]. A good understanding of the dynamic behavior of adsorption systems\n\nis required for process design and optimization \\[11\\]. The dynamic adsorption behaviors of the solutes in a\n\nfixed-bed column are represented by the effluent concentration-time profile, i.e. the breakthrough curve.\n\nThe shape of this curve is determined by the shape of the equilibrium isotherm and is influenced by the\n\nindividual transport processes in the column and adsorbents \\[8\\]. The most effective adsorption performance\n\ncan be obtained when the shape of the breakthrough curve is as sharp as possible \\[12\\]. The breakthrough\n\ntime and the saturation time can’t be exactly predicted by the breakthrough models due to the asymptotic\n\nform of the breakthrough curve \\[9\\]. Therefore, the operating times at c/c0 = 0.05 and c/c0 = 0.95 are often\n\ndefined as the breakthrough time tb and the saturation time ts, respectively \\[13\\].\n\n5\n\nThe appropriate design of an adsorption process requires the development of a mathematical model that\n\ncan describe the dynamic adsorption behaviors and predict the breakthrough curve in a fixed-bed column\n\n\\[11\\]. One approach has been to solve simultaneously the partial differential equations (PDEs) describing\n\nthe mass and the heat balance in a fixed-bed column \\[14\\]. However, the simultaneous solution of PDEs for\n\na given adsorption system, although more general and mathematically rigid, requires complicated numerical\n\nsolutions. Thus, it is desirable to use the simplified models to satisfactorily predict the fixed-bed adsorption\n\nbehaviors. Many attempts have been made to develop simplified breakthrough models to reduce\n\ncomputational time and facilitate optimization studies. Several fixed-bed adsorption models with explicit\n\nequations have been widely used to describe the breakthrough behaviors, including the Bohart–Adams,\n\nThomas, Yoon–Nelson, Clark, Wolborska and dose-response models (see Table 1). These simplified\n\nmodels are primarily established according to the description of mass transfer within adsorption systems\n\nand they can describe the experimental data satisfactorily for most practical design purposes.\n\nHowever, in many publications with respect to the adsorption of various contaminants from aqueous\n\nsolution, the authors mainly focus on the fitting performance of these simplified models and the\n\ndetermination of the parameters and errors. Some crucial problems are still to be addressed: (i)\n\nMathematical relationships between breakthrough models; (ii) Physical meanings of some specific terms;\n\n(iii) Effects of model parameters on the breakthrough curve and rate profile; (iv) Complete description of\n\nthe breakthrough curve; and (v) Establishment of the modified breakthrough models. The objectives of this\n\nwork are to help readers further understand mathematical characteristics of the breakthrough curve and\n\nselect the appropriate model to analyze the dynamic behaviors in a continuous-flow fixed-bed column.\n\n2\\. Mathematical relationships between breakthrough models\n\nIn order to select an appropriate breakthrough model to describe the dynamic behaviors in a fixed-bed\n\n6\n\ncolumn, it is extremely necessary to reveal mathematical relationships between breakthrough models. In\n\nthe previous studies \\[15-18\\], some researchers believe that the Bohart–Adams, Thomas and Yoon–Nelson\n\nmodels are separate and independent when the three models are used to correlate with the same set of the\n\nexperimental data. In other words, the breakthrough curves are not coincident and error values are not equal.\n\nHowever, it can be clearly seen from Table 1 that the three models share the identical mathematical forms\n\nand their parameters are interchangeable: kYN = kBAc0 = kTc0 and τ = a0x/uc0 = q0m/vc0 (where c0, v, x, m\n\nand u are dependent of initial operating conditions). It follows that the Bohart–Adams, Thomas and Yoon–\n\nNelson models are equivalent in mathematical nature. Consequently, the fitting curves provided by the\n\nthree models are coincident and all error values are equal when the curve fitting is carried out. Conclusions\n\ncan be drawn that the physical meanings of the terms q0m/vc0 and a0x/uc0 are the operating time required\n\nto reach 50% breakthrough and that kYN is numerically equal to a product of kBA (kT) and c0. Moreover, the\n\nchange in certain parameter of q0m/vc0 or a0x/uc0 without adjusting q0m/vc0 or a0x/uc0 does not alter\n\nadsorption performance. The most prominent advantage of using q0m/vc0 or a0x/uc0 consists in the fact that\n\none can readily see which group of the parameters affects adsorption performance rather than examining\n\nthe effect of each parameter. The revelation of mathematical relationships between the Bohart–Adams,\n\nThomas and Yoon–Nelson models can also reduce workload significantly instead of the complex curve\n\nfitting.\n\nIt is worth noting that the Yoon–Nelson model does not require detailed data with respect to the\n\ncharacteristics of adsorbates, the type of an adsorbent and the physical properties of a fixed-bed column,\n\nindicating that the parameters kYN and τ may be regarded as lumped parameters that embed some physical\n\nprocesses and operating features. As shown in Fig. S1, the Yoon–Nelson model can be derived from a\n\nlogistic function through stretching and translation transformations successively. Thus, the y = 1\n\n1 + exp( ― t)\n\n7\n\nfitting curve provided by the Yoon–Nelson model is centrosymmetric with respect to the point (τ, 0.5).\n\nFrom a mathematical perspective, the stretching transformation affects its degree of curvature, while the\n\ntranslation transformation simply influences the location of the curve. As a result, kT and τ can be regarded\n\nas the shape and location parameters, respectively. In addition, it can be clearly seen from Table 1 that the\n\nClark model can reduce to the Yoon–Nelson model when n = 2 according to the following relationship:\n\nA = exp(kYNτ) (1)\n\nr = kYN (2)\n\nIt follows that the Clark model can be seen as a generalized form of the Yoon–Nelson model. The use of\n\nthe Clark model requires that an adsorption process should follow the Freundlich isotherm in a batch\n\nadsorption system \\[19\\]. If not, the parameter n is not appropriate to calculate A and r, probably resulting in\n\ndistinct deviations. Thus, it is more reasonable to directly calculate the parameter n instead of the n value\n\nobtained from the batch reactor when the adsorption process does not follow the Freundlich isotherm. It is\n\npredicted that the Clark model may provide better fitting performance than the Yoon–Nelson model because\n\nthe extra adjustable parameter n makes the curve fitting more flexible.\n\nFig. 1 shows the transformation processes of the Wolborska model. It can be derived from an exponential\n\nfunction y = exp(t) by translation and stretching transformations successively. As a result, the Wolborska\n\nmodel does not represent a S-shaped curve and thereby fails to describe the breakthrough curve completely.\n\nIt may be only applied to the region of low breakthrough concentration, in which the process kinetics is\n\ncontrolled by mass transfer and axial diffusion in the liquid phase \\[20\\]. The characteristic parameters βa\n\nand a0 does not precisely reflect an actual fixed-bed adsorption system, either. In our opinion, the use of\n\nthe Wolborska model should be avoided for the modeling of the dynamic behaviors in a fixed-bed column.\n\n8\n\n3\\. Modification of breakthrough models\n\nSimilar to the bacterial growth curve \\[21\\], the breakthrough curve can be also divided into three phases\n\n(Fig. 2): Lag, logarithmic and stationary phases. In the lag phase, the fresh adsorbents packed in a fixed-\n\nbed column contain large numbers of available adsorption sites and thereby hold the adsorbate molecules\n\nconsecutively, leading to the very low effluent concentration; In the logarithmic phase, as the adsorption\n\nprogresses, the decrease in adsorption sites results in the rapid increase in the effluent concentration; In the\n\nstationary phase, the adsorbent particles approach the saturation state gradually and thus the effluent\n\nconcentration tends to a stationary value. The three phases of the breakthrough curve can be described by\n\nthree parameters: Lag time λ, maximum specific breakthrough rate μmax and influent concentration c0.\n\nHerein, μmax and λ are defined as the slope of the tangent line at the inflection point of the breakthrough\n\ncurve and the t-axis intercept of this tangent line, respectively.\n\nThe parameter μmax can reflect whether the adsorption is favorable and the parameter λ represents the\n\nrequired operating time before the breakthrough curve begins to rise. In general, it is difficult to accurately\n\nobtain μmax and λ if they are not estimated directly in the equation. Therefore, in order to effectively predict\n\nμmax and λ, all breakthrough models are rewritten by substituting model parameters and operating conditions\n\nwith μmax and λ. To concisely report this work, the detail derivation processes refer to Supplemental material\n\nand the modified Bohart–Adams, Thomas, Yoon–Nelson, Clark and dose-response models are listed in\n\nTable 1. As mentioned above, the Wolborska model does not represent a sigmoidal curve. Thus, it has no\n\ncorresponding modified model. It should be noted that the modified breakthrough models are derived by\n\nvariable substitution. Therefore, the fitting curves are coincident between the Bohart–Adams, Thomas,\n\nYoon–Nelson, Clark, dose-response models and the corresponding modified breakthrough models.\n\n9\n\n4\\. Symmetry of breakthrough models\n\nIn this work, the inflection point ti and half-operating time t50 are proposed to identify the symmetry of\n\nthe breakthrough curve and more adequately describe the breakthrough curve. Herein, ti and t50 are defined\n\nas the operating time required to reach one point where the shape of the breakthrough curve is converted\n\nfrom the concave to the convex and 50% breakthrough (c/c0 = 0.5), respectively. The mathematical\n\nrelationships between the defined four parameters and model parameters and operating conditions are listed\n\nin Table 2.\n\nThe relative magnitude of ti and t50 can reflect the symmetry of the breakthrough curve. As mentioned\n\nabove, the Bohart–Adams, Thomas and Yoon–Nelson models provide a symmetric breakthrough curve.\n\nThus, ti and t50 are equal. For the Clark and dose-response models, the magnitude of t50 − ti can be given as:\n\nt50 ― ti = 1rln( n ― 1\n\n2n ― 1 ― 1) (3) t50 ― ti = 1b\\[1 ―(a ― 1\n\na + 1)1a\\] (4)\n\nIt is obvious that the positive or negative value of t50 − ti simply depends on the parameters n and a\n\nrespectively. In the Clark model, the parameter n represents a Freundlich constant and the value of n lying\n\nin the range of 1–10 confirms the favorable condition for adsorption \\[22\\]. A plot of t50 − ti as a function of\n\nn is described in Fig. S2. It is found that 1 \\\u003c n \\\u003c 2, t50 \\\u003e ti; n = 2, t50 = ti; and 2 \\\u003c n \\\u003c 10, t50 \\\u003c ti. Thus, the\n\nClark model can describe an asymmetric breakthrough curve when n ≠ 2, and the breakthrough curve\n\nbecomes more asymmetric when the parameter n deviates from 2.\n\nIn the dose-response model, the influence of the parameter a on the breakthrough curve is shown in Fig.\n\n3\\. All breakthrough curves for different a values pass through one point (1/b, 0.5) and the shape of the\n\ncurves only depends on the magnitude of a. It is observed that the dose-response model does not represent\n\n10\n\na sigmoidal curve but exhibits a parabola-like curve when a ≤ 1. The sigmoidal curve occurs only at a \\\u003e 1\n\nand the degree of curvature becomes larger with the increase in a. It is observed from Fig. S3 that a \\\u003e 1, t50\n\n\\\u003e ti. Therefore, the dose-response model provides an asymmetric breakthrough curve. The breakthrough\n\ncurve becomes more symmetric with the increase in a since ti is closer to t50.\n\n5\\. Breakthrough curve and rate profile\n\nThe breakthrough curve and rate profile of the Yoon–Nelson model for different kYN are illustrated in\n\nFig. 4. All of the breakthrough curves pass through one point (τ, 0.5) and the degree of curvature increases\n\nwith the increase in kYN (become steeper). Each rate profile is a symmetric Gaussian distribution shape\n\nconcerning the vertical line t = τ and its shape is only dependent of kYN. The height decreases and the width\n\nincreases with the decrease in kYN. As mentioned above, τ is a location parameter and thereby simply affects\n\nthe location of the breakthrough curve and rate profile. The effects of kT (kBA) and q0 (a0) on the\n\nbreakthrough curve and rate profile are consistent with that of kYN and τ respectively because the Bohart–\n\nAdams, Thomas and Yoon–Nelson models are equivalent in mathematical nature.\n\nIn the Clark model, one can readily see from Fig. 5 that the parameter n can affect the curvature and\n\nlocation of the breakthrough curve. It becomes steeper with the decrease in n. The rate profiles for different\n\nn exhibit three types: (i) A symmetric Gaussian distribution shape (n = 2); (ii) An asymmetric quasi-\n\nGaussian distribution shape with a widened right-hand side (1 \\\u003c n \\\u003c 2); and (iii) An asymmetric quasi-\n\nGaussian distribution shape with a widened left-hand side (2 \\\u003c n \\\u003c 10). As mentioned above, the Clark\n\nmodel can be converted to the Yoon–Nelson model. Thus, the effects of the parameters A and r on the\n\nbreakthrough curve and rate profile are equivalent to that of kYN and τ, respectively.\n\nIn the dose-response model, the effects of the parameters a and b on the breakthrough curve and rete\n\nprofile are represented in Fig. 6. The breakthrough curve become steeper with the increase in a (a \\\u003e 1). The\n\n11\n\noperating time required to reach 50% breakthrough is exactly equal to 1/b. Thus, the half-operating time t50\n\ndepends on the parameter b. The corresponding rate profile is an asymmetric quasi-Gaussian distribution\n\nshape with a widened right-hand side. The increase in a leads to the increased height and reduced width,\n\nwhile the location of the peak is shifted to the left side. It can be clearly seen from Fig. 6b and Fig. 6d that\n\nthe breakthrough curves and rate profiles are shifted to the right side by a certain distance with the decrease\n\nin b. The degree of curvature for the breakthrough curves and maximum specific breakthrough rate μmax\n\nalso decrease. The Bohart–Adams, Thomas, Yoon–Nelson, Clark and dose-response models represent\n\ndifferent types of the breakthrough curves and rate profiles, which provide more flexible selection for the\n\nmodeling of the fixed-bed adsorption data.\n\n6\\. Application of breakthrough models\n\n6.1. Fixed-bed adsorption\n\nIn this work, the adsorption of nitrate on the chitosan-Fe(III) composite is used to evaluate the fitting\n\nperformance of the breakthrough models. In order to concisely report this work, the preparation of the\n\nchitosan-Fe(III) composite refers to Supplementary material. The fixed-bed column was made of Pyrex\n\nglass tube (Inner diameter = 0.7 cm, length = 60 cm). The chitosan-Fe(III) beads (3 g) were immersed in\n\ndeionized water for full swelling prior to use, which avoided adverse effects on the effective bed height,\n\nfollowed by addition of them to the fixed-bed column. The gauze was kept at the top and bottom of the\n\ncolumn to stabilize the adsorbent and provide a uniform flow. The nitrate solution was fed into the column\n\nfrom the bottom by a peristaltic pump at a flow rate of 6.1 mL min−1. The nitrate solution was collected at\n\nthe outlet at preset time intervals and measured by a UV/vis spectrophotometer (UV–1800, Shimadzu,\n\nJapan).\n\n12\n\n6.2. Evaluation of fitting performance\n\nTo be adequately modeled by the breakthrough models, the distribution of the experimental data must\n\napproach the shape of the breakthrough curve. In this work, the adjusted determination factor (Adj. R2) \\[23\\]\n\nand reduced chi-squared value (χ2) \\[24\\] are employed to evaluate the validity of the breakthrough models.\n\nThe data fitting is carried out by an iterative nonlinear regression using Origin 9 software (OriginLab, USA).\n\nAdj. R2 = 1 ―(1 ― R2)(n n ― ― p) 1\n\n(5)\n\nχ2 = 1f\n\n∑nωi(yi ― ýi)2 (6) ð\u009d\u0091\u0096 = 1One can readily see from Fig. 7 that the fitting curves provided by the Bohart–Adams, Thomas and\n\nYoon–Nelson models are coincident and the plot of the predicted versus observed values are also coincident\n\naccordingly, which prove that the three models represent the same breakthrough curve from experimental\n\nperspective. Therefore, as shown in Table 3, the Bohart–Adams, Thomas and Yoon–Nelson models provide\n\nthe same parameters (umax = 3.86 × 10−3 min−1, λ = 33.9 min, t50 = 163.4 min, and ti = 163.4 min) and error\n\nvalues (Adj. R2 = 0.9878 and χ2 = 1.37 × 10−3). The model parameters and operating conditions conform to\n\nkYN = kBAc0 = kTc0 and τ = a0x/uc0 = q0m/vc0, which is perfectly consistent with the theoretical analysis. It\n\nis worth noting that the Clark model has best fitting performance among these models because the predicted\n\nvalues are also closer to the observed values, especially in the region of 0–6 h. As shown in Table 3, the\n\nClark model has higher adjusted determination factor (Adj. R2 = 0.9976) and lower reduced chi-squared\n\nvalue (χ2 = 2.70 × 10−4). The order of the optimal fitting is: Clark \\\u003e dose-response \\\u003e Bohart–Adams =\n\nThomas = Yoon–Nelson models and thus the adsorption of nitrate on the chitosan-Fe(III) composite follows\n\nthe Clark model. As mentioned above, the breakthrough curve will be asymmetric when the parameter n ≠\n\n2 for the Clark model and the parameter a \\\u003e 1 for the dose-response model. In this study, n = 1.0073 and a\n\n13\n\n\\= 2.47 indicate that the breakthrough curve is asymmetric and the rate profile will be a quasi-Gaussian\n\ndistribution shape with a widened right-hand side. This asymmetric breakthrough curve may be ascribed to\n\nthe fact that the intraparticle diffusion is the rate-controlling step and the adsorbent consists of two or more\n\nconstituents of unequal reactivity \\[9,25\\]. Moreover, two different adsorption systems from literature are\n\nselected to further validate the theoretical results: Adsorption of methylene blue on porous silica\n\nmicrosphere \\[26\\] and adsorption of aniline on modified jute fiber \\[27\\]. Given that similar conclusions can\n\nbe drawn for the two adsorption systems, in order to concisely report this work, the fitting results are put in\n\nSupplementary material. This work may help readers better select the optimum model to analyze the\n\ndynamic behaviors in a fixed-bed column.\n\n7\\. Conclusions\n\nThe Bohart–Adams, Thomas and Yoon–Nelson models represent the logistic function from\n\nmathematical perspective, which provide the same symmetric breakthrough curve. The Clark model is the\n\ngeneralized form of the above three breakthrough models and thus provides better fitting performance due\n\nto an extra adjustable parameter n. The dose-response model represents a sigmoidal curve only when the\n\nparameter a is more than unity (a \\\u003e 1). The Wolborska model represents the exponential function in\n\nmathematical nature and does not provide a complete sigmoidal curve. The maximum specific breakthrough\n\nrate μmax, lag time λ, inflection point ti and half-operating time t50 can better reflect mathematical\n\ncharacteristics of the breakthrough curve and thereby contribute to selecting the optimum breakthrough\n\nmodel and gaining insights into the dynamic adsorption processes. The modified breakthrough models with\n\nμmax and λ may be important alternative methods for analysis of continuous-flow adsorption systems. The\n\nphysical meanings of the terms q0m/vc0 and a0x/uc0 are the operating time required to reach 50%\n\nbreakthrough. The model parameters are probably lumped constants that are related to the physical\n\n14\n\nprocesses and operating conditions.\n\nAcknowledgements\n\nThe authors sincerely express their appreciation to Prof. Xiangjun Pei (Chengdu University of\n\nTechnology, China).   "fileContent": "Breakthrough curve analysis by simplistic models of fixed bed adsorption\\_ In defense of the century-old Bohart-Adams model \n\nChemical Engineering Journal 380 (2020) 122513\n\nAvailable online 16 August 2019 1385-8947/ © 2019 Elsevier B.V. All rights reserved.\n\nContents lists available at ScienceDirect\n\nChemical Engineering Journal\n\njournal homepage: www.elsevier.com/locate/cej\n\nBreakthrough curve analysis by simplistic models of fixed bed adsorption: In defense of the century-old Bohart-Adams model\n\nKhim Hoong Chu\n\nHoneychem, Nanjing Chemical Industry Park, Nanjing 210047, China\n\nHIGHLIGHTS\n\n• • • • • Two different forms of the Bohart-Adams model exist in literature. Oversimplified version predicts that exit concentration increases without bound with time. Oversimplified version gives poor fits to sigmoidal breakthrough curves. Proper version predicts that exit concentration approaches an asymptotic value with time. Bohart-Adams, Thomas, and Yoon-Nelson equations are mathematically equivalent.\n\nARTICLE INFO\n\nKeywords: Bohart-Adams Breakthrough curve Thomas Wolborska Yoon-Nelson\n\nABSTRACT\n\nIn the water and wastewater treatment field several simplistic models of packed bed dynamics such as the Bohart-Adams, Thomas, and Yoon-Nelson models are frequently used by investigators to fit adsorption break- through data. The century-old Bohart-Adams model is arguably the best known one which also serves as the foundation of the bed depth-service time equation. In recent years a substantial body of literature on the subject of fixed bed modeling has however claimed that it is inferior to other models. The present paper shows that such claims are incorrect and misleading because of biased comparisons in which the fitting ability of an over- simplified version of the Bohart-Adams model was compared with those of the Thomas and Yoon-Nelson models. The oversimplified Bohart-Adams equation is in effect an exponential function which predicts an exponentially increasing breakthrough value with time. As such, it is unable to fit typical breakthrough curves which are S- shaped or sigmoidal. It can be shown that a proper version of the Bohart-Adams model gives fit quality similar to those of the Thomas and Yoon-Nelson models. This is not unexpected since the three simplistic fixed bed models can be expressed in terms of the logistic equation of population growth; that is, mathematically they are equivalent.\n\n1\\. Introduction\n\nResearch articles on the subject of contaminant removal in fixed bed adsorption columns appear regularly in the Chemical Engineering Journal. A common thread running through much of this research concerns the comparison of simplistic models of fixed bed dynamics. Such models include, among others, the Bohart-Adams, Thomas, Yoon- Nelson, Wolborska, and bed depth-service time (BDST) equations. As is well known, the BDST equation is just a rearranged form of the Bohart- Adams model (henceforth abbreviated as B-A). These fixed bed models are popular because their model equations can be linearized, allowing their unknown parameters to be estimated by linear regression analysis. In May 2019, a search conducted at the website of the Chemical Engineering Journal revealed that the journal has published more than\n\n50 such papers over the period 2008–2019. Typically in these studies the fitting abilities of two or more randomly selected models of fixed bed processes are compared on the basis of the coefficient of determi- nation R2. Somewhat unexpectedly, a sizeable number of these in- vestigations have claimed that the B-A model performed poorly in data fitting when compared with other models \\[1–10\\], or it could only fit the initial portions of breakthrough curves \\[11–19\\]. Similar claims have also been reported in a growing body of research published elsewhere. The deficiency of the B-A model identified by so many investigators is troubling because it has been and remains a cornerstone of fixed bed adsorber design and analysis. The B-A model, along with its variant, the BDST equation, can be found in just about every water and wastewater treatment textbook \\[20–23\\]. A key feature of the 100-year-old model of Bohart and Adams \\[24\\] is that it predicts a linear relationship between\n\nE-mail address: khimchu@gmail.com.\n\nhttps://doi.org/10.1016/j.cej.2019.122513 Received 9 June 2019; Received in revised form 28 July 2019; Accepted 14 August 2019\n\nT\n\nK.H. Chu Chemical Engineering Journal 380 (2020) 122513\n\nbed depth and the time taken for breakthrough to occur. This linear\n\nof the graph and No from the y-intercept. Such rearrangements of relationship simplifies the tasks of adsorber design and analysis and\n\nnonlinear equations were routinely carried out to facilitate parameter provides a simple approach to running pilot tests. As evidence of its\n\nestimation by straight-line fitting at a time when personal computers defect continues to mount, an important question arises: Is something\n\nwere not yet available. Eq. (4) will yield a straight line provided the really wrong with the century-old B-A model?\n\ndata follow the B-A model, i.e., the breakthrough curve is symmetrical In the papers that have reported unsatisfactory performance of the\n\naround its midpoint (C/Co = 0.5). B-A model, the model equation without exception takes the following form:\n\n2.2. Derivation of Eq. (1)\n\nln Co C\n\n\\= k BA N o L\n\nu k BA C o t (1)\n\nDropping the unity term in the left-hand member of Eq. (4) yields\n\nwhere Co is the initial adsorbate concentration, C is the column exit concentration at time t, kBA is the B-A rate coefficient, No is the ad-\n\n2\n\nCC = sorption capacity of the adsorbent per unit volume of the bed, L is the bed depth, and u is the superficial velocity. Oddly, the preceding equation is often called “Adams-Bohart”. The use of Eq. (1) in fixed bed modeling has been recently criticized by Hu et al. \\[25\\], but they did not explain what is wrong with this equation. It seems that the validity of the so-called “Adams-Bohart” equation is suspect. Accordingly, the purpose of the current effort is to perform a dissection on Eq. (1), highlighting its derivation, mathematical attributes, and historical origin. A further intent of this effort is to point out the fallacy of comparing the fitting abilities of the B-A (represented by an appro- priately simplified version), Thomas, and Yoon-Nelson models which are isomorphic to the logistic growth function. While this subject matter has been discussed at some length by several investigators \\[26–29\\], papers reporting model comparisons of this kind grow unabated and continue to show up on a regular basis in the environmental adsorption literature.\n\n2\\. Theory\n\n2.1. Linearization of the B-A model\n\nThe earliest analysis of fixed bed dynamics was made by Bohart and Adams \\[24\\] in 1920 who investigated chlorine gas adsorption in fixed bed columns packed with charcoal. Their model assumes that the ad- sorbate is adsorbed irreversibly and at a local rate of removal that is proportional to both the residual capacity of the adsorbent and the gas- phase concentration of the adsorbate. The analytical solution for the adsorbate concentration at the column outlet as a function of time is\n\n\\= \\\u003e\n\n\\= \\\u003c \\\u003c\n\nln o k BA N o L\n\nu k BA C o t (5)\n\nIt is evident that Eq. (5) is formally identical to Eq. (1), the so-called “Adams-Bohart” model. The two unknown parameters kBA and No may be estimated from Eq. (1) or Eq. (5) by plotting ln(Co/C) against t. It may be noted that there are now two linear forms of the original B-A model: Eqs. (1) and (4).\n\n3\\. Results and discussion\n\n3.1. Mathematical forms of simplified B-A equations\n\nTo get an adequate feel for the mathematical forms of Eqs. (1) and (4), they may be rewritten as\n\nFrom Eq. (1) CC o = exp( bt a )\n\n(6)\n\nFrom Eq. (4) CC o = 1 +\n\nexp( 1\n\na bt ) (7)\n\nwhere a = kBANoL/u and b = kBACo. It is now easy to see the mathe- matical forms of Eqs. (1) and (4). Clearly, Eq. (6) is an exponential function while Eq. (7) is the well-known logistic equation. The simi- larity between the B-A model given by Eq. (4) and the logistic equation given by Eq. (7) was first pointed out by Oulman \\[31\\] almost 40 years ago. Eqs. (6) and (7) are plotted in Fig. 1 by assigning arbitrary values\n\nCexp\n\nk BA C o\n\n( t\n\nu L)C\n\no\n\nexp ( k BA u\n\nN o L\n\n)\n\n\\+ exp k BA C o\n\n( t\n\nu L) 1\n\nfor\n\nt\n\nLu\n\nCC\n\no 0 for 0\n\nt\n\nLu\n\n(2)\n\nwhere ε is the bed voidage. Note that Eq. (2), which was first given by Amundson \\[30\\], is a more rigorous version of the original solution derived by Bohart and Adams \\[24\\]. It is interesting to note that Bohart and Adams \\[24\\] showed that their analytical solution would predict the correct breakthrough shape, but they did not use any experimental data to test their equation.\n\nThe top expression of Eq. (2) may be rewritten as\n\nln Co C\n\n1 = ln exp k BA N o L\n\nu 1 k BA C o t Lu (3)\n\nSince the exponential term exp(kBANoL/u) is expected to be much larger than unity and t is usually much greater than εL/u, the unity term and the εL/u term in the right-hand member of Eq. (3) can be neglected. The final result is\n\nln Co C\n\n1 = k BA N o L\n\nu k BA C o t (4)\n\nThe preceding equation is a linear form of the original B-A model. By plotting ln(Co/C − 1) against t, kBA can be evaluated from the slope\n\nFig. 1. Mathematical forms of Eq. (6) (top) and Eq. (7) (bottom).\n\nK.H. Chu Chemical Engineering Journal 380 (2020) 122513\n\nFig. 2. Comparison of breakthrough data of Foo and Hameed \\[1\\] and theore- tical curves computed from Eqs. (6) and (7).\n\nto a and b. From Fig. 1 it can be seen that Eq. (6) yields a J-shaped curve while Eq. (7) gives an S-shaped or sigmoidal curve. As an exponential function, Eq. (6) predicts that breakthrough percentage increases without bound with time. In contrast, Eq. (7) displays the following characteristic feature of the logistic equation: exit concentration ap- proaches an upper asymptotic value of unity with time. Given that most experimental breakthrough profiles assume the form of a more or less symmetric sigmoidal curve when column saturation is reached, fitting Eq. (6) to such breakthrough profiles is surely doomed to failure.\n\nHad previous investigators in this field used Eq. (4) instead of Eq. (1) to fit their breakthrough data, they could have obtained fitting re- sults comparable to those of the Thomas and Yoon-Nelson models or would have discovered that the B-A model could easily track the full profiles of sigmoidal curves. Let us illustrate this by way of example. Foo and Hameed \\[1\\] analyzed their breakthrough data using the three fixed bed models mentioned above with the B-A model being re- presented by Eq. (1). The fitting results given in terms of the R2 in- dicator for one of the experimental runs (open circles in Fig. 2 of Ref. \\[1\\]) are listed in Table 1. As may be seen in this table, the Thomas and Yoon-Nelson models outperformed the B-A model based on Eq. (1).\n\nHere, the same set of breakthrough data was fitted with Eqs. (1) and (4). Table 1 shows that the Eq. (1) fit yields an R2 score of 0.754, which is somewhat larger than the reported R2 value of 0.661. The best fit curve calculated with the best estimates of No and kAB is shown in Fig. 2. Except for the incipient stage of the breakthrough data, the curve predicted by the exponential B-A model given by Eq. (6) has the wrong shape which deviates substantially from the experimental data. In contrast, Eq. (4) provides an excellent fit of the Fig. 2 data, yielding an R2 score of 0.995 which is comparable to the R2 values obtained for the Thomas and Yoon-Nelson model fits, as may be seen in Table 1. As seen in Fig. 2, the calculated curve of the logistic B-A model given by Eq. (7) is capable of tracking the entire data range. If Foo and Hameed \\[1\\] had\n\nTable 1 Values of R2 for fixed bed model fits reported by Foo and Hameed \\[1\\] and this work.\n\nModel R2Foo and Hameed \\[1\\] This work\n\nThomas 0.997 – Yoon-Nelson 0.996 – Bohart-Adams (Eq. (1)) 0.661 0.754 Bohart-Adams (Eq. (4)) – 0.995\n\n3\n\nused Eq. (4) in lieu of Eq. (1) to fit their breakthrough data, they would not have concluded that the B-A model was inferior to the Thomas and Yoon-Nelson models. It seems that many past investigators \\[1–19\\] were oblivious of the existence of Eq. (4). In sum, a sensitive feeling for mathematical form is needed in treating linearized models of fixed bed adsorption, which tend to obscure their original functional form.\n\nIt is clear that the presence of the unity term in the left-hand member of Eq. (4) allows the B-A model to fit sigmoidal curves. It is important to note that there are two unity terms in Eq. (3), which is a linear form of the B-A model before any simplification. Removing the unity term from the right-hand member of Eq. (3) yields Eq. (4). This simplification is acceptable because it has a negligible effect on the fitting ability of the resultant equation. In contrast, dropping the unity term in the left-hand member of Eq. (4) to obtain Eq. (1) has a dire consequence. This time it is a case of severe simplification because it damages the ability of the resultant equation to fit S-shaped curves.\n\n3.2. Historical origin of Eq. (1)\n\nClearly, Eq. (1) is an oversimplified version of the B-A model. The question then arises, what was the reason for devising Eq. (1)? The original sources of Eq. (1) in the environmental adsorption literature could perhaps shed light on this question. As noted previously, almost all the investigators who used Eq. (1) in their modeling work labeled the B-A model as “Adams-Bohart”. This peculiarity allowed us to track down a paper by Guibal et al. \\[32\\] published in 1995, which appears to be the first one to use the unique “Adams-Bohart” label for Eq. (1). Eq. (1) along with the “Adams-Bohart” name can be found in a review article by Aksu \\[33\\] published in 2005, in which the Guibal et al. paper was cited. According to Google Scholar, this review has received almost 2000 citations up to May 2019. This highly cited review is probably responsible for the proliferation of Eq. (1) in the environmental ad- sorption realm.\n\nGuibal et al. \\[32\\] did not cite any reference for Eq. (1). Neither did they mention Eq. (4), from which Eq. (1) is derived. These early pro- ponents of Eq. (1) probably believed that it was the only linear form of the B-A model. Nonetheless, they were aware of its limited fitting ability, stating that its use should be restricted to breakthrough va- lues \\\u003c 15%. This limited range of utility can also be found in the review article by Aksu \\[33\\]. It seems that Eq. (1) has been devised for the purpose of fitting small breakthrough values. Unfortunately, this con- dition has been largely ignored by many journal articles that have ap- peared over the intervening years. Today, we are in a situation where Eq. (1) is used indiscriminately in data fitting. Inglezakis et al. \\[34\\] have recently called attention to the issue of indiscriminate applications of simplified adsorption kinetics equations in some studies which often disregard the equations’ underlying assumptions and limitations. It should be mentioned that Eqs. (1) and (4) can be found in a recent review \\[28\\] which states that the former equation is valid for C/Co ≪ 1.\n\n3.3. An aside on the Wolborska model\n\nAs a side note, the Wolborska model \\[35\\] is worthy of examination here since it bears many similarities to Eq. (1). The following expression is the common form of the Wolborska model given in most publications:\n\nCC = ln\n\no\n\nC t N\n\noLo u (8)\n\nwhere β denotes the external mass transfer coefficient when the phe- nomenon of axial fluid phase dispersion is of negligible importance. A little known fact about the Wolborska model is that it has been derived from a slightly faulty mass conservation equation written for a fixed bed adsorption column. As a result, the first term on the right-hand side of Eq. (8) is not a dimensionless number because it still carries a ratio of bed volume to solution volume, as may be seen in the following ex- pression:\n\nK.H. Chu Chemical Engineering Journal 380 (2020) 122513\n\nC ot N\n\no\n\n\\= ( time\n\n1\n\n)( solution adsorbate volume mass\n\n) (time) ( adsorbate bed volume mass\n\n)\n\n\\= solution bed volume\n\nvolume\n\n(9)\n\nThe volume of solution within a fixed bed is simply equal to the void volume of the bed. Accordingly, by introducing the bed voidage ε into Eq. (9), the first term becomes dimensionless:\n\nC ot N\n\no\n\n\\= ( void bed volume volume\n\n)( time\n\n1 ( )( adsorbate void volume mass\n\n) (time) adsorbate bed volume mass )\n\n\\= dimensionless (10)\n\nA related mistake is that the velocity term appearing in the de- nominator of the second term on the right-hand side of Eq. (8) should be the interstitial fluid velocity through the bed (u/ε) instead of the superficial velocity u.\n\nBased on the remarks above, an alternate and correct expression of the Wolborska model is found to be\n\nln\n\nC Co\n\n\\= C ot\n\nN\n\no Lu (11)\n\nThe preceding equation can be found in a later paper by Wolborska and Pustelnik \\[36\\], in which the corrected expression was given without any clarification. Unfortunately, this corrected form of the Wolborska model has attracted little attention. Consequently, Eq. (8) is still being used by many present investigators to analyze their break- through data, reporting β values which are values for the product εβ. The incorrect version of the Wolborska model given by Eq. (8) can be found in the highly cited review by Aksu \\[33\\] published about 15 years ago as well as the recent review by Tan and Hameed \\[28\\] published in 2017.\n\nIt is simple to show that the Wolborska model given by Eq. (11) is mathematically equivalent to the B-A model given by Eq. (1). If one defines kBA = εβ/No, Eq. (1) becomes\n\nCC = 4\n\nFig. 3. Comparison of small breakthrough values (C/Co \\\u003c 0.5) and theoretical curves computed from Eqs. (6) and (7).\n\nbreakthrough percentages, Eq. (7) is quantitatively superior. The comparison is not exhaustive but does illustrate the superiority of Eq. (7). From a curve-fitting perspective, it is evident that Eq. (7) can fit either a partial J-shaped or a complete S-shaped breakthrough curve with good accuracy. As a result, no advantages exist in using Eq. (1) over Eq. (4) in data fitting. In a strictly practical sense, fitting Eq. (1) instead of Eq. (4) to breakthrough data does not make the linear re- gression procedure any simpler. Therefore, it is totally not necessary to devise Eq. (1) in order to meet the need for a model that can fit small exit concentrations. For these reasons, it does seem that Eq. (1) or (6) is unlikely to be of great practical value as an approximate, simplified\n\nln o LC ot\n\nu\n\nN o model of fixed bed adsorption, and will rarely prove to be superior.\n\n(12)\n\nWhen the experimental breakthrough profile is not perfectly sym- metrical, Eq. (4) may not give a linear plot over the complete break- or\n\nthrough range because the logistic function is symmetrical around its\n\nln\n\nCC o\n\nmidpoint (C/Co = 0.5). It is then necessary to fit Eq. (4) to the initial portion of the breakthrough curve in order to get a linear plot from which the two B-A parameters kBA and No can be estimated. In such cases, one should still use Eq. (4) because it can give an adequate or better fit. There is no need to use Eq. (1) to fit small breakthrough values. Here, a recommendation is made that the use of the over- simplified Eq. (1) in data fitting be avoided. All in all, Eq. (1) does not appear to have any unique or preferential role for interpretation of breakthrough data.\n\nAt this juncture, a few remarks about the use of linearized equations in data fitting may be in order. Since most of the commonly used ad- sorption models including equilibrium isotherms, kinetic rate expres- sions, and the simplistic fixed bed models analyzed in this study are described most readily in terms of equations that are nonlinear in the parameters, there are strong statistical arguments in favor of using nonlinear regression to estimate their parameters. Accordingly, in the case of the B-A model, one should use Eq. (2) or (7) in lieu of the lin- earized form given by Eq. (4) in data fitting. However, it is not within the scope and intent of the present paper to discuss the topic of linear regression versus nonlinear regression. There are a number of articles on this subject in the literature \\[39–41\\], and the reader is referred to these for further detail.\n\n3.5. Model comparisons\n\nA secondary objective of this paper is to provide a critique of the widespread practice of comparing the fitting abilities of the B-A (Eq. (4)), Thomas, and Yoon-Nelson models (see, for example, Refs. \\[42,43\\]). Comparisons of this kind are ubiquitous in the environmental = C ot\n\nN\n\no Lu (13)\n\nwhich is the same as Eq. (1), is an exponential function, as shown in the following ex- pression:\n\nCC o = exp( bt a )\n\n(14)\n\nwhere a = εβL/u and b = εβCo/No. Obviously, being an exponential function, the Wolborska model is valid for small exit concentrations; a condition which is explicitly stated in Wolborska’s article \\[35\\]. As discussed previously, the underlying assumptions and limitations of simplistic models are often ignored by researchers. In this respect the Wolborska model is no exception. Recent examples of its misuse in breakthrough curve modeling abound in the environmental adsorption literature \\[37,38\\].\n\n3.4. Fitting small breakthrough values\n\nThe fundamental problem with Eq. (1) is that whatever it can do; Eq. (4) can do just as well or better. To demonstrate this, the two equations were fitted to small breakthrough values taken from Fig. 2 (C/Co \\\u003c 50%). Values of R2 obtained for the Eqs. (1) and (4) fits are 0.953 and 0.973, respectively, indicating that the latter equation gives a better overall fit. As is evident in Fig. 3, both the exponential and lo- gistic versions of the B-A model, Eqs. (6) and (7), are able to track the J- shaped breakthrough data up to about 30% breakthrough. (7) and (24), will exhibit the same functional form because mathematically they are equivalent. Both are capable of tracking S-shaped or sigmoidal breakthrough curves, and when fitted to the same breakthrough data set, should give identical fit quality. As such, there is little to be gained by making comparisons between the two models.
