Consequently, the Yoon-Nelson model is mathematically equivalent to the simplified B-A and Thomas models.\n\n3.5.3. Parameter estimation\n\nTable 2 summarizes the relations between the general parameters of the logistic function and the respective parameters of the simplified B- A, Thomas, and Yoon-Nelson models. To estimate the parameters of the three fixed bed models, it is only necessary to fit the logistic function to a given set of breakthrough data once to extract a and b from the re- sultant plot. With known values of a and b and other relevant opera- tional and system variables, the three models’ parameters can be computed from the simple relations given in Table 2. To illustrate this point, a linear form of the logistic function (ln(Co/C − 1) = a − bt) was fitted to the Fig. 2 data to obtain a = 6.1 and b = 0.062 1/min. Values of the operational and system variables required for using the relations in Table 2 are as follows: Co = 0.15 mg/cm3, L = 6 cm, u = 17.7 cm/ min, M = 3.99 g, and Q = 20 cm3/min \\[1\\]. The three fixed bed models’ parameters computed in this manner are listed in Table 3. Also included in this table are the corresponding literature values \\[1\\]. Evidently, the computed parameter values agree with the values reported in the study\n\nFig. 4. Parameter estimation via the logistic, Bohart-Adams, Thomas, and Yoon-Nelson equations.\n\nof Foo and Hameed \\[1\\], the parameter estimation method of which is based on fitting the three models one by one to the breakthrough data. Note that the B-A model was excluded from this analysis because Foo and Hameed \\[1\\] used the oversimplified Eq. (1) in the data fitting process. The parameter estimation method of this study and the con- ventional method favored by many investigators in this field \\[1–19,42,43\\] are illustrated in Fig. 4.\n\nThis work has shown that the parameters of the three fixed bed models can be estimated via the fitting of a single equation: the logistic function. There is thus only one R2 value in the data fitting process. This data fitting example demonstrates the fallacy of treating the three fixed bed models as independent models and comparing their data fitting\n\nTable 2 Parameters of the Bohart-Adams (Eq. (7)), Thomas (Eq. (24)), and Yoon-Nelson\n\nabilities based on the R2 indicator. Many researchers seem oblivious to the fact that the three fixed bed models are one and the same, often (Eq. (30)) models expressed in terms of the logistic equation’s general para-\n\nclaiming that one model is better than the others based solely on mar- meters a and b.\n\nginal differences in the values of R2. As a result, the significance of such\n\nModel Logistic equation parameter\n\nmodel comparisons becomes open to question. Despite past efforts calling attention to this issue \\[26–29\\], model comparisons of this kind remain\n\na b\n\nfrequent. Although this modeling practice has become pervasive and\n\nBohart-Adams kBANoL/u Thomas kTqoM/Q Yoon-Nelson kYNτ kBACo\n\nkTCo kYN\n\nentrenched in this field of research, it is hoped that the present paper will help promote awareness of the problems associated with meaningless model comparisons of this sort, particularly among novice modelers.\n\nK.H. Chu Chemical Engineering Journal 380 (2020) 122513\n\n4\\. Conclusions\n\nThe B-A (Eq. (4)), Thomas, and Yoon-Nelson models can all be ex- pressed in terms of the logistic growth function with two general parameters. In principle, one only needs to fit the logistic equation to breakthrough data to extract the two general parameters, from which the respective parameters of the three fixed bed models may be com- puted, as has been demonstrated in the present paper for a set of lit- erature data. No useful insight can be gained from the widespread practice of fitting the three fixed bed models one by one to experi- mental data. Mathematically they are one and the same, and will therefore give similar fit quality.\n\nMoreover, the proliferation of different forms of the same model creates a confusing modeling environment. A case in point is the B-A model, which has two commonly used versions, designated by Eqs. (1) and (4) in this work. Previous investigators who have used Eq. (1) in their modeling work assert that the B-A model is inferior to the Thomas and Yoon-Nelson models. This research has shown that such claims are incorrect and misleading and as such the B-A model is often unjustly criticized. Eq. (1) is in effect an exponential function which predicts an exponentially increasing breakthrough percentage with time. As such, it is unable to fit typical breakthrough curves which are S-shaped or sigmoidal. To fit such curves, one should use Eq. (4) which will give fit quality comparable to those of the Thomas and Yoon-Nelson models.\n\nBecause of oversimplification in its derivation, the B-A model as defined by Eq. (1) is valid only for small exit concentrations. The year 2020 will celebrate the centennial of the B-A model, yet confusion and misunderstanding over its application still exists. It is recommended that the use of the oversimplified Eq. (1) in data fitting be avoided. A particularly apt aphorism attributed to Einstein \\[55\\] fits well here: “Make everything as simple as it can be, but not one bit simpler.”\n\nReferences\n\n\\[1\\] K.Y. Foo, B.H. Myers[a](#1)[,](#1)∗, Alba Cabrera-Codony[b](#1), Abel Valverde[a](#1)\n\na *Centre de Recerca Matemàtica, Campus de Bellaterra, Edifici C, Bellaterra 08193, Barcelona, Spain*\n\nb *LEQUIA, Institute of the Environment, Universitat de Girona, Girona 17003, Catalonia, Spain*\n\na r t i c l e\n\ni n f o\n\n*Article history:*\n\nReceived 20 April 2022\n\nRevised 8 November 2022\n\nAccepted 12 November 2022\n\nAvailable online 28 November 2022\n\n*2010 MSC:*\n\n35Q35\n\n*Keywords:*\n\nContaminant removal\n\nPollutant removal\n\nAdsorption\n\nMathematical model\n\nTravelling wave\n\nBohart–Adams\n\nAdvection-diffusion\n\na b s t r a c t\n\nIn this paper we investigate the standard one-dimensional model for adsorption of a contaminant in a\n\npacked column. Initially we focus on two mass sink models: a full nonlinear Langmuir equation, including\n\nadsorption and desorption and the linearised version which neglects the local concentration. Travelling\n\nwave solutions are developed for both cases, providing exact analytical formulae for the concentration\n\nand adsorbed fraction throughout the column. Comparison is made with experimental data for the ad-\n\nsorption of toluene on activated carbon (dominated by physical adsorption) and Cr(III) on zeolite NaX\n\n(dominated by ion exchange). For toluene the nonlinear model provides good agreement but it is less ac-\n\ncurate for Cr(III). The linear model apparently works well for Cr(III) but only at the expense of violating\n\nthe model assumptions. This motivates extending the travelling wave solution to a Sips sink model, which\n\nhas a power law dependence on local concentration. Comparison with data provides excellent agreement\n\nin all cases and also indicates why the previous models fail. Following from the investigation of the linear\n\nsink model, we prove that previously accepted results frequently fail due to a violation of assumptions\n\nmade in their derivation. Specifically we challenge the belief that widely used models such as Bohart–\n\nAdams, Yoon–Nelson, Bed Depth Service Time, etc can accurately predict breakthrough data. Their success\n\ninstead may be attributed to: at times, having a similar shape to the breakthrough curve rather than be-\n\ning the correct solution; treating measurable quantities as fitting parameters and a willingness to accept\n\nthat constant parameters vary. In contrast to these previous models, the travelling wave solution of the\n\npresent paper, based on a Sips sink, provides analytical solutions consistent with the model assumptions\n\nfor a wide range of experimental data.\n\n© 2022 The Author(s). Published by Elsevier Ltd.\n\nThis is an open access article under the CC BY license (\u003chttp://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/\u003e)\n\n**1. Introduction**\n\nAdsorption columns are employed in a vast range of con-\n\ntaminant removal processes. Important applications include wa-\n\nter treatment involving emerging contaminants (such as phar-\n\nmaceuticals and pesticides, bacteria, viruses, salts, minerals and\n\ndyes), chromatography and the removal of greenhouse gases\n\n[\\[1,7,14,19,25\\]](#15). Mathematically the problem is equivalent to solvent\n\nextraction in a packed bed which is employed, for example, in the\n\nproduction of essential oils or lanolin, see [\\[17,23,29\\]](#15). Given the\n\nprevalence of adsorption processes and the many practical applica-\n\ntions it is imperative to improve and optimise equipment. To this\n\nend there exists a vast literature concerning experimental, compu-\n\ntational and mathematical studies. Experimental studies have pro-\n\nvided a wealth of valuable data for a multitude of contaminants\n\n∗ Corresponding author.\n\nand adsorbents. The computational studies tend to involve a high\n\nnumber of coupled differential equations and, whilst able to pro-\n\nvide practical results, it can be difficult to infer the dependence on\n\noperating conditions, see [\\[19\\] ](#15)for example. Theoretical studies deal\n\nwith a much reduced system leading to approximate results but\n\nthe analytical solutions do clarify the system dependencies.\n\nA particular focus of the mathematical models is the break-\n\nthrough curve, the concentration measured at the column out-\n\nlet, where the majority of studies indicate an exponential form.\n\nExamples include the Bohart–Adams (BA), Yoon–Nelson, Wol-\n\nborska, Bed Depth Service Time (BDST) and Myers models, see\n\n[\\[13,21,22,30,31\\] ](#15)and also the model summary of [Patel \\[25](#15), Table 3\\].\n\nOften these are mathematically equivalent, for example BA, Yoon–\n\nNelson, BDST and the Myers model, see [\\[21\\] ](#15)all have the same\n\nmathematical form, with the main differences coming through the\n\ndefinition of coefficients. The equivalence of these models, together\n\nwith their verification against experimental data, suggests that the\n\n[https://doi.org/10.1016/j.ijheatmasstransfer.2022.12366](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.ijheatmasstransfer.2022.123660)0\n\n0017-9310/© 2022 The Author(s). Published by Elsevier Ltd. This is an open access article under the CC BY license [( ](http://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/)\u003chttp://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/\u003e )\n\n-----\n\n|  |\n| :-: |\n| [**Page 2**]() |\n\n*T.G. Myers, A. Cabrera-Codony and A. Valverde*\n\n*International Journal of Heat and Mass Transfer 202 (2023) 123660*\n\nsolution form is capable of reproducing the physical behaviour, at\n\nleast for certain types of experiment. Unfortunately not all models\n\nare as successful as they may appear.\n\nOne reason that previous models appear so successful is that\n\nonce derived the physical meaning is often forgotten and the form\n\nis used as the basis for a curve fit. Consider a common form of BA\n\nbreakthrough curve\n\n*c(L,t)* =\n\n*cin*\n\n1 + exp*(kadcin(ρbqmL/(uincin )* − *t))*\n\n*,*\n\n(1)\n\nwhere *cin* is the inlet concentration, *kad* the (constant) adsorption\n\ncoefficient, *ρb* the bulk density, *qm* the adsorption capacity, *L* the\n\ncolumn length,  the void fraction and *uin* the initial interstitial\n\nvelocity. This equation is derived in [Appendix A](#14). If all the constants\n\nare lumped together it may be written\n\n*c(L,t)* =\n\n*cin*\n\n1 + exp*(A* − *Bt))*\n\n*,*\n\n(2)\n\nand the BA, Yoon–Nelson, BDST and constant velocity Myers forms\n\nare all represented. Assuming *A, B* are unknown then permits [(2) ](#2)to\n\nbe applied to a wide range of data. This amounts to a curve fit with\n\ntwo fitting parameters and, if agreement is shown with data, any\n\nof the models may be claimed accurate. Good agreement may be\n\njudged through the *R*2 value (which on its own is not a good mea-\n\nsure, see [Section 5.1](#9)) or visually, but if the results are plotted for\n\nthe whole experimental time, rather than focussing around break-\n\nthrough, then the area of interest is compressed and discrepancies\n\nare harder to spot (this is highlighted in [Myers and Font \\[22](#15), Figs 2,\n\n3\\]). However, *A, B* do have physical meanings and generally there\n\nis only one unknown which is the adsorption coefficient *kad*.\n\nDespite taking the same mathematical form, the models are not\n\nthe same. For example, they require some statement about the up-\n\ntake of contaminant through the adsorption equation. The Yoon–\n\nNelson model focuses on the outlet where the mass loss rate is\n\nproportional to the probability of adsorption and of breakthrough.\n\nRelating the probability of breakthrough to the concentration re-\n\nsults in *qt (L,t)* ∝ *c(L,t)(cin* − *c(L,t))*. For the BA model the uptake\n\nis proportional to the available sites and concentration of contam-\n\ninant throughout the column, *qt (x,t)* ∝ *c(x,t)(qm* − *q(x,t))*, where\n\n*qm* is related to the maximum available sites (and therefore in-\n\ndependent of concentration). The **constant of proportionality** in\n\nboth cases is the adsorption coefficient. However, when compared\n\nwith experimental data in many cases the values of *kad* or *qm* de-\n\npend on the inlet concentration. This violates the basic assumption\n\nand invalidates the result. The issue may not be noticed if only a\n\nsingle data set is used, leading to a single value each for *A, B*. This\n\nhighlights the necessity to test the model against multiple data\n\nsets.\n\nDjelloul and Hamdaoui [\\[13\\] ](#15)illustrate the testing point well,\n\napplying three similar model forms to experimental data for the\n\nremoval of dye. All show good agreement between the theoret-\n\nical result and data. For the BA model the adsorption constant\n\ndoes remain relatively constant with *cin*. The sorption capacity,\n\n*qm*, varies with *cin* and velocity. The Clark model has a parame-\n\nter *r* equivalent to *kad*, this is shown to vary with both *cin, uin*.\n\nA second constant, related to available sites appears to vary lin-\n\nearly with *cin*. Note both these models involve two fitting param-\n\neters. The Yoon–Nelson model has a single parameter. The results\n\nshow *kad* almost doubling as the inlet concentration doubles. De-\n\nspite the failure to satisfy the model’s constraints they conclude\n\nthat all models are suitable for describing the “dynamic behaviour\n\nof the column”. Darweesh and Ahmed [\\[12, Tables 2, 3\\] ](#15)present re-\n\nsults for a “Thomas” model (which, as pointed out by Chu [\\[10\\] ](#15)is\n\na form of Bohart–Adams model rather than the equation derived\n\nby Thomas), an “Adams–Bohart” model (which is a much reduced\n\nand less accurate version of Bohart–Adams) and Yoon–Nelson. In\n\nthis case changing *cin* leads to a relatively small variation in *kad*\n\nfor Yoon–Nelson but an almost linear relation between *kad* and *cin*\n\n[for the Thomas/Bohart–Adams model. The review of Patel ](#15)[\\[25, Ta-](#15)\n\n[ble 3\\] ](#15)[summarises how model “constants” vary with inlet concen-](#15)\n\ntration, flow rate, temperature and bed length. At least for bed\n\nlength and inlet concentration they should remain constant. For\n\nexample with the BA model the value of the adsorption coefficient\n\nincreases with an increase in initial concentration and flow rate\n\nand decreases with column length. The number of available sites\n\n(per unit length) decreases with inlet concentration and flow rate\n\nand increases with column length. Similar results are provided for\n\nanother six models, including Yoon–Nelson and BDST. From these\n\nand a multitude of other studies, it is clear that the models do\n\nnot work. Further, if the “constants” vary with system parameters\n\nthey cannot be used to infer the performance of a different set-up\n\nwhich may be one cause for the well-known problems of scale-up.\n\nThe inability of [(2) ](#2)to effectively capture many data sets is\n\nillustrated in the work of Chu, see [\\[3,10,11\\] ](#15)for example. Chu\n\n[\\[10\\] ](#15)provides a careful discussion of the BA model, clarifying var-\n\nious misunderstandings and incorrect versions and tracing errors\n\nback through the literature. Possibly motivated by inconsistencies\n\nwith data, after championing the BA model and showing its good\n\nagreement with data, the discussion is continued in a series of\n\nsubsequent publications where alternative models are proposed. In\n\n[\\[11\\] ](#15)a modification due to Amundson [\\[2\\] ](#15)is introduced. For column\n\nadsorption this involves shifting the time axis so that *t* → *t* − *L/u*.\n\nHowever in Chu [\\[11\\] ](#15)the shift is almost immediately neglected, by\n\nassuming *t*  *L/u*, with the end result that a standard BA [Eq. (1) ](#2)is\n\nretrieved. The fact that the time shift is irrelevant has previously\n\nbeen pointed out by Hu and Zhang [\\[16\\]](#15). It will also be verified\n\nlater, although it is apparent even from the data of [Chu \\[10](#15), Table\n\n1\\] which gives *L/u* = 2 s (*L* = 0*.*1 m, *u* = *(*0*.*02*/*0*.*4*)* m/s) whereas\n\nbreakthrough occurs at around 8000 s and 2000 s in the two ex-\n\namples shown in that paper. To match different data sets in subse-\n\nquent papers Chu proposes alternative forms based on the obser-\n\nvation that they have the correct shapes, these include a Gompertz\n\ntype equation, a Weibull function and the “logarithmic Bohart–\n\nAdam”. The latter is developed by taking the logarithm of two\n\nof the terms in the BA equation and is justified as an “empir-\n\nical” modification, see [\\[3\\]](#15). Comparison with limited data shows\n\nthat in each case the new model performs better than the BA\n\nmodel. The distance between the models and reality is highlighted\n\nthrough the statement that “the parameter *kad* lacks physical sig-\n\nnificance and can thus be viewed as a purely empirical parameter\n\nwhich is adjusted to fit the shape of a given breakthrough curve”\n\n[Apiratikul and Chu \\[3](#15), P. 5\\].\n\nIn reality the parameter *kad* has a clear physical meaning: it\n\nis the rate constant in the kinetic relation and quantifies the at-\n\ntraction between the adsorbent and adsorbate, see Patel [\\[25\\]](#15), Tran\n\net al. [\\[28\\]](#15). The fact that such a variety of models have been pro-\n\nposed, “constant” parameters vary and extra unknowns are intro-\n\nduced all suggest that the models are based on erroneous assump-\n\ntions or that they do not hold in certain situations, not that pa-\n\nrameters have no physical meaning. This statement provides the\n\nmotivation behind the present study. Current models clearly have\n\nvarying degrees of success, suggesting they need refining, repair-\n\ning or limits placing on their range of applicability. To do this we\n\nwill first develop the mathematical model underpinning the break-\n\nthrough curves, which clarifies the physical meaning of problem\n\nparameters. Subsequently we derive two solutions, taken from the\n\nwork of Myers et al. [\\[21,22\\] ](#15)and compare them, as well as the\n\nBA solution with data from two sets of experiments. The first is\n\nfor toluene on activated carbon and is a physcial adsorption pro-\n\ncess. The second is for Cr(III) on NaX zeolite which is driven by\n\nion exchange. The results demonstrate that none of the models\n\nwork well with all data sets. Subsequently we extend the Myers\n\nmethodology to account for a Sips adsorption equation, where the\n\n2\n\n-----\n\n|  |\n| :-: |\n| [**Page 3**]() |\n\n*T.G. Myers, A. Cabrera-Codony and A. Valverde*\n\n*International Journal of Heat and Mass Transfer 202 (2023) 123660*\n\n**Fig. 1.** (a) Column configuration. (b) Typical concentration profiles through the column.\n\nadsorption rate varies non-linearly with concentration, *qt (x,t)* ∝\n\n*c(x,t)m(qm* − *q(x,t))n*, see [\\[26\\]](#15). This is discussed in more detail\n\nin [Section 2.2](#4). An analytical solution is presented which, in the\n\ncases *m* = 0*,* 1 reduces to previous results. Comparison with data\n\nshows that this form may do well while other models fail. Impor-\n\ntantly, parameters which are assumed to be constant during the\n\nmodel development are shown to remain approximately constant\n\nwhen verified against experimental data. To demonstrate the is-\n\nsues with previous models we also discuss the Bohart–Adams and\n\nYoon–Nelson models, since these are generally used with two and\n\none fitting parameters respectively and therefore also represent re-\n\nsults provided by other models with a similar mathematical form.\n\n**2. Mathematical model development**\n\n*2.1. Mass transfer model*\n\nConsider the set-up depicted in [Fig. 1](#3)(a), a fluid enters the col-\n\numn at the inlet, *x* = 0, as it travels through the pipe contami-\n\nnant is removed by sorption (and may return via desorption). If\n\nthe sorption process is sufficiently strong the concentration of con-\n\ntaminant, *c*, reaches zero before the outlet is reached. With a suf-\n\nficiently weak sink, particularly if the sorption rate depends on\n\nthe concentration, the contaminant may theoretically extend over\n\na large domain and in practice continue to the outlet. These two\n\ncases are shown in [Fig. 1](#3)(b).\n\nThe basic mathematical model for this process is straightfor-\n\nward, assuming that a known flux of contaminant is introduced\n\nto the column and then taken up by the sorbent. This leads to a\n\nsimple mass balance, however the form of the uptake can cause\n\ndifficulties. We assume that:\n\n1\\. The fluid is well-mixed and wall effects are negligible, so that\n\nquantities such as concentration and velocity vary only along\n\nthe length of the column (equivalently they may be treated as\n\nbeing averaged over the cross-section).\n\n2\\. The process is isothermal (thermal effects may easily be added\n\nbut here we focus on the base model).\n\n3\\. That model constants take a constant value\\!\n\nDefining the mass per unit length as *m(x,t)* a mass balance\n\nalong the column determines\n\n*∂mc*\n\n*∂t*\n\n\\+\n\n*∂*\n\n*∂x*\n\n*umc* − *D*\n\n*∂mc*\n\n*∂x*\n\n\\= −\n\n*∂mad*\n\n*∂t*\n\n*,*\n\n(3)\n\nwhere *c* denotes contaminant and *ad* adsorbate, *u* is the interstitial\n\nfluid velocity and *D* the axial diffusion coefficient. We then define\n\nthe variable *q* = *mad/mat* to be the ratio of adsorbed mass to (con-\n\nstant) adsorbent mass, denoted *mat* .\n\nThe contaminant moves with the fluid phase, which occupies\n\nthe void space in the column, and so the mass of contaminant per\n\nunit length may be written *mc* = *Ac* where *c* \\[kg/m3\\] is the con-\n\ncentration density of contaminant in the carrier fluid, *A* the cross-\n\nsectional area and  the void fraction. This leads to\n\n*∂*\n\n*∂t*\n\n*(Ac)* +\n\n*∂*\n\n*∂x*\n\n*(uAc)* =\n\n*∂*\n\n*∂x*\n\n*D*\n\n*∂*\n\n*∂x*\n\n*(Ac)*\n\n− *mat*\n\n*∂q*\n\n*∂t*\n\n*.*\n\n(4)\n\nNoting that *A* is constant and *mat* = *Mat /L*, where *Mat* is the total\n\nmass of adsorbent in the column and *L* the column length we may\n\nwrite\n\n*∂*\n\n*∂t*\n\n*(c)* +\n\n*∂*\n\n*∂x*\n\n*(uc)* =\n\n*∂*\n\n*∂x*\n\n*D*\n\n*∂*\n\n*∂x*\n\n*(c)*\n\n− *ρb*\n\n*∂q*\n\n*∂t*\n\n*,*\n\n(5)\n\nwhere *ρb* = *Mat /(AL)* is termed the bulk density.\n\nFrequently the process is such that the void space does not vary\n\nin size, for example if only a monolayer attaches to the adsorbent\n\nor most of the adsorption occurs within pores inside the adsor-\n\nbent. In which case *, D* may be taken as constant. Myers and Font\n\n[\\[22\\] ](#15)study the constant  case in an investigation of CO2 removal,\n\nwhere approximately 20% of the volume is adsorbed along the col-\n\numn. Often, if the adsorption is a relatively slow process or the car-\n\nrier fluid contains only small amounts of the contaminant then the\n\nvelocity *u* = *uin* = *Jin/(A)* may also be assumed constant, where *Jin*\n\nis the inlet volume flux. Then\n\n*∂c*\n\n*∂t*\n\n\\+ *uin*\n\n*∂c*\n\n*∂x*\n\n\\= *D*\n\n*∂*2*c*\n\n*∂x*2 −\n\n*ρb*\n\n*∂q*\n\n*∂t*\n\n*.*\n\n(6)\n\nThis is a standard form of advection-diffusion equation for contam-\n\ninant flow and removal in a packed column. The key now is to de-\n\nfine the sink term.\n\nThe strength of the sink is important for the definition of ap-\n\npropriate boundary conditions. If there is no sink, *qt* = 0, then we\n\nhave a classical advection-diffusion equation. Mathematically the\n\ninclusion of the diffusion term means that the concentration has\n\ninfinite speed of propagation: the “front” of the contaminant wave\n\nwill stretch to infinity. In reality this cannot occur, the infinite\n\nspeed derives from a continuum assumption and when the number\n\nof molecules becomes small the continuum model no longer holds.\n\nHowever, it does demonstrate that for very small sinks the bound-\n\nary at the front of the wave may be far ahead of the main body\n\nof contaminant. On the other hand, with a sufficiently strong sink,\n\n*q(t)* 1, the concentration will soon reach zero. The two cases are\n\ndepicted in [Fig. 1](#3)(b).\n\nThus we propose two sets of boundary conditions for the con-\n\ntaminant front. For a sufficiently strong sink the contaminant will\n\nbe completely removed at position *x* = *s(t)*, where *s(t)* is *a priori*\n\nunknown and moves forward in time as adsorbent is exhausted on\n\nthe inlet side. Then imposing zero concentration and zero flux at\n\nthe front\n\n*c(s(t),t)* = *cx(s(t),t)* = 0 *.*\n\n(7)\n\n3\n\n-----\n\n|  |\n| :-: |\n| [**Page 4**]() |\n\n*T.G. Myers, A. Cabrera-Codony and A. Valverde*\n\n*International Journal of Heat and Mass Transfer 202 (2023) 123660*\n\nThe second condition follows due to the definition of the flux, *J* =\n\n*uc* − *Dcx*, since *c(s,t)* = 0 zero flux then requires *cx(s,t)* = 0. With\n\na sufficiently weak sink\n\n*c(x,t)* = *cx(x,t)* → 0 a*s x* → ∞ *.*\n\n(8)\n\nWe will clarify the definition of “sufficiently weak” later. At the\n\ninlet we impose continuity of flux\n\n*uincin* =\n\n*uc* − *D*\n\n*∂c*\n\n*∂x*\n\n*x*=0+\n\n*,*\n\n(9)\n\nwhere *cin* is the inlet concentration and *uin* = *Jin/(A)*.\n\n*2.2. Mass sink model*\n\nOne of the most common ways to deal with the mass loss is\n\nthrough the Langmuir equation. This is based on assumptions that\n\nthe adsorbent is an ideal solid surface with distinct sites that hold\n\nthe adsorbate molecules, the adsorbed layer is a single molecule\n\nthick, the adsorption rate depends on the partial pressure of the\n\nadsorbate and also the number of available sites while desorp-\n\ntion depends only on the number of occupied sites. Denoting the\n\nfraction of occupied sites as *θ* the two rates are proportional to\n\n*p(*1 − *θ), θ*. The Langmuir equation holds for low pressures where\n\nthe adsorbate behaves as an ideal gas at isothermal conditions and\n\nso the pressure is proportional to the concentration. To be consis-\n\ntent with the advection-diffusion equation we work in terms of the\n\nadsorbed mass and so define *θ* = *q/qm* where *qm* is the maximum\n\npossible value of *q* (i.e. when all sites are occupied). From these\n\nthe rate at which the adsorbent fills with adsorbate molecules is\n\n*∂q*\n\n*∂t*\n\n\\= *kadc(qm* − *q)* − *kdeq ,*\n\n(10)\n\nsee [\\[20\\] ](#15)for example. Due to the *cq* term we will refer to this as the\n\nnonlinear model. The adsorption and desorption coefficients may\n\nvary with certain system parameters, such as temperature and flow\n\nrate but, according to the assumption of proportionality, must re-\n\nmain constant with respect to *c, q*. The sorption equation may be\n\nrearranged to\n\n*∂q*\n\n*∂t*\n\n\\= *kadc*\n\n1 +\n\n1\n\n*KLc*\n\n*qmKLc*\n\n1 + *KLc*\n\n− *q*\n\n*.*\n\n(11)\n\nThe coefficient *KL* = *kad/kde* is the Langmuir constant, \\[*KL*\\] =m3/kg.\n\nIn equilibrium, when desorption matches adsorption it is clear\n\nfrom [Eq. (11) ](#4)that\n\n*qe* =\n\n*qmKLce*\n\n1 + *KLce*\n\n*,*\n\n(12)\n\nwhere *qe* represents the equilibrium adsorbed mass and *ce* the\n\nconcentration in the surrounding fluid. This is the Langmuir\n\nIsotherm. In a column experiment, where contaminant is con-\n\nstantly introduced into the system, equilibrium is only reached\n\nwhen the outlet concentration matches the inlet value and so\n\n*ce* = *cin*. Experimentally we may determine *qe* through the initial\n\nand final weight of material in the column (provided the experi-\n\nment has run for sufficient time to exhaust the material)\n\n*qe* =\n\n*Mf* − *Mi*\n\n*Mi*\n\n*.*\n\n(13)\n\nThe value of *qe* can also be determined experimentally by the nu-\n\nmerical integration of the concentrations obtained, if enough data\n\nis available,\n\n*qe* =\n\n1\n\n*Mi*\n\n ∞\n\n0\n\n*Jin(ce* − *c)dt* ≈\n\n*Jin*\n\n2*Mi*\n\n*N*\n\n*i*=1\n\n*(*2*ce* − *ci* − *ci*−1 *)(ti* − *ti*−1 *),*(14)\n\nwhere *ce* = *cin* for column experiments and *N* is the total number\n\nof experimental data points available (the initial pair *(c*0*,t*0 *)* may\n\nboth be set to zero). Note that *Jin* is assumed to be constant and\n\nthe trapezoidal rule of integration has been used. Similar results\n\nshould be retrieved from both methods.\n\nIn [\\[4\\] ](#15)it is stated that “adsorption equilibrium information is\n\nthe most important piece of information needed for a proper un-\n\nderstanding of an adsorption process”. Given the difficulties in\n\nmatching certain data sets to model predictions and the wide\n\nrange of materials and fluids employed it is no surprise that a\n\nvariety of isotherms other than [(12) ](#4)are applied in the literature.\n\nMany isotherms may be found by simple modifications to the\n\nLangmuir isotherm, for example, [(12) ](#4)reduces to Henry’s isotherm\n\n*qe* = *qmKLce* in the limit *KLce* 1. The Freundlich isotherm is an\n\nempirical relationship obtained through observation of experimen-\n\ntal isotherm data, *qe* = *qmKF cm*\n\n*e* . In an attempt to correct the un-\n\nphysical behaviour of the Freundlich isotherm near saturation Sips\n\n[\\[26\\] ](#15)proposed a more general form, *qe* = *qmKF cm*\n\n*e /(*1 + *KF cm*\n\n*e )* (note,\n\nit is also common to use *c*1*/n*\n\n*e*\n\n, see [\\[28, Eqs (56, 59)\\]](#15)). This isotherm\n\ncorresponds to an adsorption term proportional to *cm(qm* − *q)*, it\n\ncould also be expressed in the form *cm(qm* − *q)n* where *m, n* rep-\n\nresent the partial order of the chemical reaction. However, it is the\n\nratio *m/n* which determines the isotherm shape so here we keep\n\nthe single parameter model. In the limit *KF cm*\n\n*e* 1 the Freundlich\n\nrelation is retrieved, while for *m* = 1 the Sips model reproduces the\n\nLangmuir isotherm. Adsorption isotherms are discussed in detail in\n\nAyawei et al. [\\[4\\]](#15), de Franco et al. [\\[14\\]](#15), Tran et al. [\\[28\\]](#15)[.](#15)\n\nDue to the difficulty of analysing nonlinear equations it is com-\n\nmon to study a linear approximation of [(10)](#4). A linear adsorption\n\nequation is obtained by placing the Langmuir isotherm into a lin-\n\near kinetic relation\n\n*∂q*\n\n*∂t*\n\n\\= *kL(qe* − *q).*\n\n(15)\n\nIf we conform to the Langmuir equation then *qe* = *qmKLcin/*\n\n*(*1 + *KLcin )* but at times it is left as an unknown fitting parame-\n\nter. It is commonly termed the Linear Driving Force (LDF) model. It\n\nhas the advantage of being simple to deal with. In [\\[27\\] ](#15)it is argued\n\nthat as a result of the averaging process the “LDF model works in\n\npractice” (later we will show examples which disagree with this\n\nconclusion). The form of the isotherm *qe* in this equation is ob-\n\nviously a constant version of the corresponding term in [(11)](#4). The\n\nlinear adsorption equation cannot capture certain key physical as-\n\npects of the full model. [Eq. (15) ](#4)indicates that the adsorption rate\n\nis highest when *q* → 0, i.e. there are lots of sites available, and\n\nlowest as *q* → *qe*, i.e. when it approaches the equilibrium cover-\n\nage. The amount of adsorbed material then varies between these\n\nlimits hence *qt* ≥ 0. Since the concentration *c* is absent from the\n\nequation, it can predict adsorption even when there is no contam-\n\ninant. In the worst case, for example near the front of the contami-\n\nnant wave *x* = *s(t)* where *c, q* ≈ 0, [Eq. (15) ](#4)indicates *qt* ≈ *kLqe* takes\n\nits highest value, i.e. adsorption is greatest even though there is\n\nnothing to adsorb. In the same scenario [Eq. (11) ](#4)correctly predicts\n\n*qt* = 0 and in general recognises the need for contaminant in the\n\nadsorption process: if *c* = 0 then there is no adsorption. If there is\n\nnegligible contaminant but the sites are occupied then [Eq. (10) ](#4)in-\n\ndicates *qt \\\u003c* 0 and there is desorption, [Eq. (15) ](#4)cannot predict this.\n\nSince adsorption dominates over desorption in general *KLc* 1\n\nand [Eq. (11) ](#4)may be approximated by\n\n*∂q*\n\n*∂t*\n\n≈ *kadc(qm* − *q),*\n\n(16)\n\nwhich is of course the pure adsorption version of [(10)](#4). The\n\nisotherm is then the maximum possible amount *qm* which is of-\n\nten referred to as the rectangular isotherm. Note, this reduction\n\ncannot hold over the whole domain, the removal of contaminant\n\nmeans that for much of the process there will be a region where\n\n*c* 1, for example close to *x* = *s(t)*, such that *KLc* is order unity, in\n\n4\n\n-----\n\n|  |\n| :-: |\n| [**Page 5**]() |\n\n*T.G. Myers, A. Cabrera-Codony and A. Valverde*\n\n*International Journal of Heat and Mass Transfer 202 (2023) 123660*\n\nwhich case all terms must be retained. In the limit *KLc* 1 we ob-\n\ntain *qt* = *kde(qmKLc* − *q)*. From this we may conclude that the lin-\n\near model only provides an approximate description of the com-\n\nbined sorption/desorption process in certain regions of the flow. It\n\ndoes not follow as any distinguished limit of the nonlinear model\n\n[(10)](#4). Importantly, the linear adsorption equation fails in crucial ar-\n\neas, at times predicting almost the opposite behaviour to the non-\n\nlinear model. For this reason, in the following work a main focus\n\nwill be on the effect of the mass sink model.\n\nFinally, the sink equation requires a single initial condition\n\n*q(s(t),* 0*)* = 0 *.*\n\n(17)\n\nNote, the usual condition *q(x,* 0*)* = 0 applied throughout the litera-\n\nture is only correct if there is a supply of contaminant throughout\n\nthe column at *t* = 0+. In numerous papers this boundary condition\n\nis imposed after integrating the linear adsorption equation to ob-\n\ntain\n\n*q(x,t)* = *qe(*1 − exp*(*−*kLt)).*\n\n(18)\n\nThis indicates that *q \\\u003e* 0 for any *t \\\u003e* 0. There is no *x*-dependence,\n\nmeaning that every part of the column shows the same behaviour,\n\neven if no contaminant has arrived. The correct solution to the lin-\n\near adsorption equation is\n\n*q(x,t)* = *qe(*1 − exp*(*−*kL(t* − *ts(x)))* for *x* ≤ *s(t),*\n\n*q(x,t)* = 0 for *x* ≥ *s(t)*\n\n(19)\n\nwhere *ts(x)* is the time taken for the contaminant to reach a given\n\npoint *x*. Only in static experiments is [(18) ](#5)correct. This issue is dis-\n\ncussed in Myers et al. [\\[21\\]](#15)[.](#15)\n\n**3. Mathematical solutions**\n\nWe begin by writing the governing equations in non-\n\ndimensional form. To do this we let\n\n*x* = *L*ˆ*x , c* = *cin* ˆ*c , q* = *Q* ˆ*q , t* = *τ* ˆ*t , u* = *uin* ˆ*u ,*\n\n(20)\n\nwhere *uin* = *Jin/(πR*2 *)* is the fluid velocity just inside the column.\n\nProvided *u* = *uin* is constant throughout the column then ˆ*u* = 1 ev-\n\nerywhere. In this way the advection-diffusion [Eq. (6) ](#3)may be writ-\n\nten\n\n*δ*1\n\n*∂* ˆ*c*\n\n*∂*ˆ*t*\n\n\\+\n\n*∂* ˆ*c*\n\n*∂* ˆ*x*\n\n\\= *δ*2\n\n*∂*2 ˆ*c*\n\n*∂* ˆ*x*2 −\n\n*∂* ˆ*q*\n\n*∂*ˆ*t*\n\n(21)\n\nwhere *δ*1 = *Da* = *L/(uinτ)* is the Dahmkohler number, *δ*2 = *Pe*−1 =\n\n*D/(uinL)* the inverse Peclet number and the length-scale *L* =\n\n*uincinτ/(ρbQ)*. Note, the length-scale reflects the width of the\n\nmain reaction zone rather than the column length. The time and\n\n*q* scales depend on the form of the sink term. If the velocity varies\n\nthen ˆ*u* appears in the derivative term *(* ˆ*u*ˆ*c)*ˆ*x*. This case is dealt with\n\nin Myers and Font [\\[22\\]](#15).\n\nIn non-dimensional form the nonlinear sink, [Eq. (10)](#4), may be\n\nwritten\n\n*d* ˆ*q*\n\n*d*ˆ*t*\n\n\\= ˆ*c(*1 − ˆ*q)* − *δ*3 ˆ*q ,*\n\n(22)\n\nwhere *Q* = *qm*, *τ* = 1*/(kadcin )* and *δ*3 = *kde/(kadcin )*. For the linear\n\nadsorption equation\n\n*d* ˆ*q*\n\n*d*ˆ*t*\n\n\\= *(*1 − ˆ*q),*\n\n(23)\n\nwith *Q* = *qe*, *τ* = 1*/kL*. The pair of differential equations, [(21) ](#5)and\n\n[(22) ](#5)or [(23)](#5), are subject to\n\nˆ*c(*ˆ*s(*ˆ*t),* ˆ*t)* = ˆ*c*ˆ*x(*ˆ*s(*ˆ*t),* ˆ*t)* = ˆ*q(*ˆ*s(*ˆ*t),* ˆ*t)* = 0\n\n(24)\n\nor\n\nˆ*c(*ˆ*x,* ˆ*t),* ˆ*c*ˆ*x(*ˆ*x,* ˆ*t),* ˆ*q(*ˆ*x,* ˆ*t)* → 0 a*s* ˆ*x* → ∞ *.*\n\n(25)\n\nAt the inlet\n\n1 =\n\nˆ*c* − *δ*2\n\n*∂* ˆ*c*\n\n*∂* ˆ*x*\n\nˆ*x*=0+\n\n*.*\n\n(26)\n\n*3.1. Travelling wave solutions*\n\nWhen the contaminant first enters the column the adsorbent is\n\nempty and so contaminant is rapidly removed in the inlet region.\n\nAs the adsorbent fills up the contaminant front starts to move for-\n\nward, from this stage the process takes on a self-similar form. Con-\n\nsequently we may seek a travelling wave solution. The accuracy\n\nof the travelling wave form for the linear adsorption model has\n\nbeen verified by comparison with numerics and experimental data\n\nin Myers et al. [\\[21\\]](#15), Myers and Font [\\[22\\]](#15). This form will hold for\n\ntimes significantly greater than those of the initial transient, which\n\nis related to the relative size of the ˆ*c*ˆ*t* term in [Eq. (21)](#5), thus requir-\n\ning *t*  *L/uin*. Since we obtain information from the breakthrough\n\nand equilibrium solutions this condition is always satisfied. How-\n\never, this restriction prevents the application of an initial condition\n\non the concentration, instead late time data such as that provided\n\nby the breakthrough curve is required.\n\nIn this section we will follow the methodology of Myers et al.\n\n[\\[21\\]](#15), Myers and Font [\\[22\\] ](#15)for the linear model and extend it to\n\ndeal with the nonlinear sink. First we define the travelling wave\n\nco-ordinate and variables\n\n*η* = ˆ*x* − ˆ*s(*ˆ*t),*\n\n*F(η)* = ˆ*c(*ˆ*x,* ˆ*t),*\n\n*G(η)* = ˆ*q(*ˆ*x,* ˆ*t).*\n\n(27)\n\nIn the presence of a moving front, where *F(*0*)* = *G(*0*)* = 0, the po-\n\nsition ˆ*x* = ˆ*s(*ˆ*t)* represents the leading edge of the front. If the sink\n\nis too weak to remove all contaminant and so the concentration\n\nnever reaches zero then ˆ*s(*ˆ*t)* could represent the middle position\n\nof the wave, where ˆ*c* = *F* = 1*/*2. The velocity of the moving front\n\nis defined by ˆ*v* = ˆ*s*ˆ*t* , where ˆ*v* is constant, and hence\n\nˆ*s* = ˆ*v*ˆ*t* + ˆ*s*0 *.*\n\n(28)\n\nSince the travelling wave does not hold close to ˆ*t* = 0 we cannot\n\nimpose ˆ*s(*0*)* = 0 and so ˆ*s*0 must be determined through the exper-\n\nimental breakthrough data.\n\nIn the new co-ordinate system [Eq. (21) ](#5)may be written\n\n*(*1 − ˆ*vδ*1 *)F*\n\n = *δ*2*F*\n\n + ˆ*vG*\n\n *.*\n\n(29)\n\nWhile the two forms of sink equation become\n\n−ˆ*vG*\n\n = 1 − *G ,*\n\n−ˆ*vG*\n\n = *F(*1 − *G)* − *δ*3 *G .*\n\n(30)\n\nEquations [(29) ](#5)and [(30) ](#5)are solved subject to\n\n*F* = *F*\n\n = *G* = 0\n\n(31)\n\neither at *η* = 0 for a strong sink or as *η* → ∞ for a weak sink.\n\nThe travelling wave requires the solution to have the same form\n\nfor all time, it merely translates through space which results in\n\na fixed form in the *η* domain. This means that the conditions at\n\nˆ*x* = 0 must be replaced by conditions as *η* → −∞. Below we will\n\nsee that solutions take on an exponential form so that this modi-\n\nfication requires terms of the order exp*(η(*ˆ*x* = 0*))* = exp*(*−ˆ*s(*ˆ*t))* to\n\nbe negligible. For example if we take ˆ*s* = 5, which in dimensional\n\nterms corresponds to five times the length of the reaction zone\n\nthen exp*(*−ˆ*s)* ≈ 7 × 10−3 and we expect errors of the order 0.7%.\n\nAs ˆ*s* increases the error in applying the condition at −∞ will de-\n\ncrease further. The concentration boundary condition may then be\n\nwritten\n\n*F* → 1 as *η* → −∞ *.*\n\n(32)\n\nConsequently *F*  = *F*  → 0 and according to [(29) ](#5)then *G*  → 0 as\n\n*η* → 0. Setting *G*  = 0 in [Eq. (30) ](#5)gives the equilibrium values *Ge* =\n\n1 for the linear model and *Ge* = 1*/(*1 + *δ*3 *)* for the nonlinear.\n\n5\n\n-----\n\n|  |\n| :-: |\n| [**Page 6**]() |\n\n*T.G. Myers, A. Cabrera-Codony and A. Valverde*\n\n*International Journal of Heat and Mass Transfer 202 (2023) 123660*\n\nHenceforward we will neglect diffusion which is generally\n\nsmall, *δ*2 1 (this is confirmed in [Section 4.1](#7). The coefficient *δ*1\n\nis also small however we will retain it since it does not affect the\n\norder of the equation nor the solution form. Neglecting the *δ*2 term\n\nand integrating [(29) ](#5)leads to\n\n*(*1 − ˆ*vδ*1 *)F* = ˆ*vG* + *A*0\n\n(33)\n\nImposing the boundary conditions at *η* = 0 or *η* → ∞ shows *A*0 =\n\n0\\. Imposing the conditions as *η* → −∞ then determines the wave\n\nspeed\n\nˆ*v* =\n\n1\n\n*Ge* + *δ*1\n\n*.*\n\n(34)\n\nSo now we have a simple relation between *F* and *G*, namely\n\n*F* =\n\n*G*\n\n*Ge*\n\n(35)\n\nwhere *Ge* = 1 or *Ge* = 1*/(*1 + *δ*3 *)*.\n\n*3.1.1. Solution with linear sink*\n\nIn the travelling wave form the linear adsorption equation inte-\n\ngrates immediately to\n\n*G* = 1 − exp*(η/*ˆ*v)* = *F*\n\nfor *η* ≤ 0 *.*\n\n(36)\n\nThis satisfies *G(*0*)* = 0, i.e. the adsorbed mass and hence concen-\n\ntration (via [(35)](#6)) reach zero over a finite distance. The linear ad-\n\nsorption equation therefore constitutes a strong sink model.\n\nReturning to dimensional form\n\n*c*\n\n*cin*\n\n\\=\n\n*q*\n\n*qe*\n\n\\= 1 − exp\n\n*kL*\n\n*x* − *s*0\n\n*v*\n\n− *t*\n\nfor *x* ≤ *s(t),*\n\n(37)\n\nwhere\n\n*v* =\n\n*L*\n\n*τ(*1 + *δ*1 *)*\n\n\\=\n\n*u*\n\n1 + *ρbqe/(cin )*\n\n*.*\n\n(38)\n\nThis demonstrates that the velocity of the contaminant wave is al-\n\nways slower than that of the fluid. The velocity decreases as the\n\nbulk density or equilibrium adsorption increase and increases as\n\nthe fluid speed, void fraction or inlet concentration increase.\n\nWith the definition of *s(t)* we may write *ts(x)* = *(x* − *s*0 *)/v* and\n\nthen [(37) ](#6)is the same as [Eq. (19)](#5), so verifying the statement\n\nthat [Eq. (18) ](#5)is not the correct solution for column adsorption.\n\n[Eq. (37) ](#6)still involves the unknown *s*0, we may remove this by\n\ndefining the time where the outlet concentration reaches half of\n\nits inlet value, that is *c(L,t*1*/*2*)* = *cin/*2, which leads to\n\n*c*\n\n*cin*\n\n\\= 1 −\n\n1\n\n2\n\nexp\n\n*kL*\n\n*x* − *L*\n\n*v*\n\n\\+ *t*1*/*2 − *t*\n\n\\=\n\n*q*\n\n*qe*\n\nfor *x* ≤ *s(t),* (39)\n\nwhere\n\n*s(t)* = *v*\n\n*t* − *t*1*/*2 +\n\nln*(*2*)*\n\n*kL*\n\n\\+ *L .*\n\n(40)\n\nFor *x* ≥ *s* both *c, q* = 0. This describes the concentration and ad-\n\nsorbed mass fraction throughout the column, for times beyond the\n\ninitial transient. The breakthrough curve then follows by setting\n\n*x* = *L*\n\n*c(L,t)*\n\n*cin*\n\n\\=\n\n*q(L,t)*\n\n*qe*\n\n\\= 1 −\n\n1\n\n2\n\nexp\n\n*kL(t*1*/*2 − *t)*\n\n*.*\n\n(41)\n\nThe breakthrough curve only holds beyond the first breakthrough\n\ntime when the wave front reaches the outlet, that is, when *s(tb)* =\n\n*L*. This is the final time when *c(L,tb)* = 0. Substituting *s* = *L*, *t* =\n\n*tb* into [(40) ](#6)determines the first breakthrough time *tb* = *t*1*/*2 −\n\nln*(*2*)/kL*.\n\n*3.1.2. Solution with nonlinear sink*\n\nNow the sink term is defined by\n\n−ˆ*vG*\n\n = *F(*1 − *G)* − *δ*3 *G ,*\n\n(42)\n\nso as *η* → −∞ we obtain the equilibrium value *Ge* → 1*/(*1 + *δ*3 *)* so\n\n*F* = *G/Ge* = *(*1 + *δ*3 *)G*. With *A*0 = 0 in the concentration equation,\n\n[(33)](#6), we obtain the wave speed\n\nˆ*v* =\n\n1 + *δ*3\n\n1 + *δ*1*(*1 + *δ*3 *)*\n\n*.*\n\n(43)\n\nReplacing *G, Ge* in [(42)](#6)\n\n−ˆ*vF*\n\n = *F(*1 − *F)* ⇒ *F* =\n\n1\n\n1 + *B* exp*(η/*ˆ*v)*\n\n*.*\n\n(44)\n\nThis satisfies *F* → 1 as *η* → −∞ but will only satisfy *F* = 0 in the\n\nlimit *η* → ∞. Consequently we see that with the nonlinear sink,\n\ntheoretically, the concentration never reaches zero and the con-\n\ntaminant occupies a semi-infinite domain. This could be expected\n\nfrom the model: the mass loss is proportional to *c(*1 − *q)* and so\n\nas *c* → 0 so does the mass loss. This is in contrast to the linear\n\nadsorption model where the concentration has no effect on the\n\nstrength of the sink.\n\nApplying *F* = ˆ*c* = 1*/*2 at ˆ*x* = ˆ*L,* ˆ*t* = ˆ*t*1*/*2 we may eliminate *Be*−*s*0*/*ˆ*v*.\n\nThen reverting to dimensional form\n\n*c*\n\n*cin*\n\n\\=\n\n1\n\n1 + exp\n\n*kadcin((x* − *L)/v* + *(t*1*/*2 − *t))*\n\n = *(*1 + *δ*3 *)*\n\n*q*\n\n*qm*\n\n*.* (45)\n\nwhere the velocity\n\n*v* =\n\n*u*\n\n1 + *ρbqm/((cin* + *kde/kad ))*\n\n*.*\n\n(46)\n\n[Eq. (45) ](#6)holds for all *x,t \\\u003e* 0 although physically we must restrict\n\n0 ≤ *x* ≤ *L*, *t* ≥ *L/u*. In the limit 1*/KL* = *kde/kad* → 0 this reproduces\n\nthe velocity from the linear adsorption model, [Eq. (38)](#6). Hence the\n\nwave speed has similar behaviour to that with a linear sink except\n\nthat now *v* increases with *KL*. This is to be expected since increas-\n\ning *KL* means that less contaminant can be adsorbed at any *x* and\n\nso the front must move forward more rapidly.\n\nAt the outlet the breakthrough equations are\n\n*c(L,t)* =\n\n*cin*\n\n1 + exp*(kadcin(t*1*/*2 − *t))*\n\n*,*\n\n(47)\n\n*q(L,t)* =\n\n*qmkadcin*\n\n*(kadcin* + *kde )(*1 + exp*(kadcin(t*1*/*2 − *t))*\n\n*.*\n\n(48)\n\nAn interesting feature of this solution is that the breakthrough\n\ncurve, *c(L,t)*, provides no information on the desorption. This\n\ncomes through knowledge of the isotherm. Fitting [(47) ](#6)to experi-\n\nmental data determines *kad*, the isotherm determines *KL* and hence\n\n*kde* = *kad/KL* (isotherms are discussed in more detail in [Section 5.1](#9)).\n\nThe value of *qm* is also found from the isotherm, so defining the\n\nwave speed, which is an important indicator of the efficacy of the\n\nprocess.\n\n*3.2. Bohart–Adams and Yoon–Nelson models*\n\nThe Bohart–Adams model is obtained by balancing advection\n\nwith the sink, where the sink is pure adsorption, *kde* = 0. The so-\n\nlution in dimensional form is\n\n*c*\n\n*cin*\n\n\\=\n\n1\n\n1 − exp*(*−*kadcint)* + exp *(kadcin (ρbqmx/(uincin )* − *t))*\n\n*,*\n\n(49)\n\n*q*\n\n*qm*\n\n\\= 1 −\n\n1\n\n1 − exp*(*−*ρbkadqmx/(uin ))* + exp *(*−*kadcin (ρbqmx/(uincin )* − *t))*\n\n*.*\n\n(50)\n\n6\n\n-----\n\n|  |\n| :-: |\n| [**Page 7**]() |\n\n*T.G. Myers, A. Cabrera-Codony and A. Valverde*\n\n*International Journal of Heat and Mass Transfer 202 (2023) 123660*\n\nThe full derivation of [(49) ](#6)and [(50) ](#6)is provided in [Appendix A](#14). To\n\nobtain this solution the following initial and boundary conditions\n\nhave been imposed\n\n*c(*0*,t)* = *cin*\n\n*q(x,* 0*)* = 0 *.*\n\n(51)\n\n(We have already explained why this is not the correct boundary\n\ncondition for column adsorption). To be consistent with the form\n\nof solution these conditions require an initial concentration and in-\n\nlet adsorbed quantity\n\n*c(x,* 0*)* = *cin* exp*(*−*kadρbqmx/(uin )),*\n\n(52)\n\n*q(*0*,t)* = *qm(*1 − exp*(*−*kadcint)).*\n\n(53)\n\nCommon simplifications involve assuming *t, L* are sufficiently\n\nlarge such that exp*(*−*kadcint)*, exp*(*−*ρbkadqmL/(uin ))* 1 and\n\nthen the breakthrough concentrations may be expressed as\n\n*c(L,t)*\n\n*cin*\n\n\\=\n\n1\n\n1 + exp *(kadcin(ρbqmL/(uincin )* − *t))*\n\n\\=\n\n*q(L,t)*\n\n*qm*\n\n*.*\n\n(54)\n\n[Eq. (54) ](#7)is perhaps the most well recognised form of the BA model.\n\nIt is identical to the form provided in [Chu \\[10](#15), Eq. (8)\\] (although\n\nthat paper employs the superficial velocity and the adsorbent den-\n\nsity *ρp* = *ρb/(*1 − *)*). It is also clear that [Eq. (54) ](#7)has the same\n\nmathematical form as the Myers model with a nonlinear sink, [(47)](#6),\n\nalthough the constant term in the exponential is different.\n\nThe method employed to obtain [(54) ](#7)is an elegant way to solve\n\nthe nonlinear system, but it is unphysical. It requires the column\n\nto be everywhere occupied with contaminant at *t* = 0, as shown in\n\n[(52)](#7), yet at the same time none of it has attached to the adsor-\n\nbent, *q(x,* 0*)* = 0, see [(51) ](#7)(which in itself is incorrect). In the ab-\n\nsence of the sink the concentration equation is a kinematic wave.\n\nThis means information from the inlet will be translated along the\n\ncolumn, for this reason the breakthrough concentration contains\n\nan identical *x* dependence to that of [Eq. (52)](#7): if incorrect infor-\n\nmation is input then incorrect information is output. When we\n\nmove on to comparison with data it will become clear that the *x*\n\nterm in the breakthrough equation is incorrect. The neglect of the\n\nexp*(*−*kadcint)* term in the concentration equation is inconsistent. If\n\nexp*(*−*kadcint)* 1 then according to [(53) ](#7)the inlet adsorbed quan-\n\ntity is zero for all time, *q(*0*,t)* = 0.\n\nThe modification of Amundson [\\[2\\] ](#15)involves replacing *t* → *t* −\n\n*L/u* and the equations hold for *t \\\u003e L/u*. The above objections all\n\nhold for this model, except they hold at a slightly different time.\n\nNote, the travelling wave solution [(47) ](#6)and [(48) ](#6)also indicates\n\n*c(x,* 0*), q(x,* 0*)* = 0 however in contrast to the above solution the\n\ntravelling wave is not defined near *t* = 0 and only becomes valid\n\nsome time after the initial transient. In fact the breakthrough curve\n\npredicted by the travelling wave bears a closer resemblance to the\n\nYoon–Nelson model than the BA model. The Yoon–Nelson form\n\nis based on assumptions regarding the probability of a molecule\n\nemerging from the column. Denoting the probability of being ad-\n\nsorbed as *Q* and of breakthrough as *P* where *P* = 1 − *Q* then *Qt* =\n\n−*kYNQP*, where *kYN* is constant with respect to *P, Q*. Defining *P* ∝\n\n*c/cin* = *q/qm* leads to\n\n*c(L,t)*\n\n*cin*\n\n\\=\n\n1\n\n1 + exp*(kYN (t*1*/*2 − *t))*\n\n\\=\n\n*q(L,t)*\n\n*qm*\n\n*.*\n\n(55)\n\nSince it is derived based purely on probabilities of escape from\n\nthe column outlet this provides no information about the concen-\n\ntrations within the column and when compared to [Eq. (47) ](#6)it is\n\nclear that their ‘constant’ *kYN* can vary with inlet concentration. In\n\nfact during their derivation *kYN* is stated to depend on concentra-\n\ntion, flow rate and mass of adsorbent but these are then combined\n\ninto a single constant to permit the integration, see [\\[31, Eqs (5,6)\\]](#15).\n\nUnsurprisingly when comparing with experiments their calculated\n\nvalues for *kYN* often show a strong variation with *cin*. They also\n\nprovide values for *kYN/cin* which shows a much weaker variation\n\n(confirming the suspicion of a concentration dependent *kYN*), see\n\n[\\[31, Table 1\\] ](#15)(where their *k*  = *kYN* and *k* ∝ *k*  */cin*).\n\n**4. Comparison with experimental data**\n\n*4.1. Experimental data collection*\n\nThe true test of any model comes through comparison with ex-\n\nperimental data. In this section we employ data for the adsorp-\n\ntion of gaseous toluene on activated carbon as well as Cr(III) re-\n\nmoval from distilled water using zeolite NaX as the adsorbent, the\n\nlatter is taken from the literature [\\[5\\]](#15). These data sets were cho-\n\nsen because of their very different flow regimes and adsorption\n\nmechanisms which thus provides a good challenge for the mod-\n\nels. While toluene is a physical process (without strong chemical\n\ninteractions), Cr(III) is adsorbed in the NaX by ion exchange. The\n\nReynolds numbers in the two sets of experiments are order 1 and\n\n0.5 respectively. Based on the experimental findings on dispersion\n\nof fluids in packed beds [\\[18\\]](#15), these values suggest a dispersion\n\ncoefficients *D* = *O(*10−5 *)* and *O(*10−6 *)* respectively. These lead to\n\n*δ*2 ≈ 0*.*1 for both cases. This low value validate the neglect of the\n\ndiffusion term in [Eq. (33)](#6). Data and operating conditions for the\n\nCr(III) experiments are all taken from Barros et al. [\\[5\\]](#15), data sets for\n\nthe toluene results are available on the CORA Repository (the open\n\naccess repository for Catalan universities and research institutes),\n\nsee [\\[24\\]](#15).\n\nFor comparison we will use the two Myers’ models given by\n\n[Eqs. (41) ](#6)and [(47)](#6), the first based on the linear sink the second on\n\nthe nonlinear sink. These involve only a single fitting parameter.\n\nThe Bohart–Adams model could be manipulated to match [(47) ](#6)by\n\nusing two fitting parameters, instead we will demonstrate that the\n\ncorrect BA model shifts the curve far from the experimental posi-\n\ntion. For this reason we do not plot it. In both sets of data studied\n\nwe found that the Yoon–Nelson model had a variable *kYN* so also\n\nomit these results.\n\nTo obtain the toluene adsorption data dynamic adsorption tests\n\nwere carried out following the set up described in Cabrera-Codony\n\net al. [\\[9\\]](#15). The chemical and physical features of the activated car-\n\nbon, Norit RB3, were fully characterized in that paper. Briefly,\n\nglass adsorption columns of 10 mm diameter were filled with\n\n160 mg of activated carbon with a particle diameter range of\n\n0.212–0.425 mm, leading to a column length of *L* = 5*.*4 mm, bulk\n\ndensity *ρb* = 377*.*25 kg/m3, porosity  = 0*.*336. Adsorption tests\n\nwere performed using streams with gaseous toluene concentra-\n\ntions of 0.41, 1.32 and 2.84 g/m3. The test gas was generated using\n\na syringe pump (Pump 11 Elite, Harvard apparatus) to infuse liquid\n\ntoluene (99.8% purity, Sigma Aldrich) at rates of 7–40 μL/h into a\n\ndry nitrogen flow of 205 STPmL/min, set by a mass flow controller\n\n(Alicat Scientific). In all cases this resulted in an interstitial veloc-\n\nity *uin* = 0*.*13 m/s. Toluene concentration was measured using a gas\n\nchromatograph equipped with a flame ionisation detector (GC-FID\n\nCP3800, Varian) equipped with an automatic injection valve sys-\n\ntem to sample and analyse the gas every ca. 7 min. The system was\n\nregularly calibrated by determining the GC-FID response to differ-\n\nent toluene infusion rates.\n\nFor each test the adsorption column was operated until the\n\ntoluene’s outlet concentration matched the inlet concentration. The\n\nexperiments were conducted at constant pressure (1.117 × 105 Pa)\n\nand room temperature (22 ◦C).\n\nThe experimental set up of Barros et al. [\\[5\\] ](#15)has an adsorption\n\ncolumn of 16 mm length and 9 mm of internal diameter, with a\n\nbed porosity of 0.5 and a bulk density of 440 kg/m3. The flow rate\n\nwas fixed at 9 mL/min, i.e. 4.72 × 10−3 m/s approximately.\n\n7\n\n-----\n\n|  |\n| :-: |\n| [**Page 8**]() |\n\n*T.G. Myers, A. Cabrera-Codony and A. Valverde*\n\n*International Journal of Heat and Mass Transfer 202 (2023) 123660*\n\n**Fig. 2.** Comparison of models for toluene adsorption on steam activated carbon. Solid line nonlinear adsorption [Eq. (47)](#6) , dashed line linear adsorption [Eq. (41)](#6) , circles\n\nrepresent the data points.\n\n*4.2. Comparison of models with data*\n\n[Fig. 2 ](#8)shows predictions of the two Myers’ models [(41) ](#6)and\n\n[(47) ](#6)and also the experimental data for toluene for three different\n\ninlet concentrations. The solid line represents the nonlinear sink\n\nmodel, [Eq. (47)](#6), the dashed line the linear adsorption model, [(41)](#6),\n\nand the circles the experimental data. The value of *t*1*/*2 was ob-\n\ntained by specifying a straight line between the data points on ei-\n\nther side to obtain the time when *c(L,t)* = *cin/*2. In all cases the\n\nMyers model with the nonlinear sink provides the best agreement\n\nwith data, although as *cin* increases both models miss the shoulder\n\nbefore *c(L,t)* → *cin*. For *cin* = 0*.*00041 kg/m3 it is difficult to distin-\n\nguish the nonlinear result from the data. The linear sink is worse\n\neverywhere except at *t*1*/*2 where the two must coincide. Notably\n\nthe linear adsorption model misses all early data points. This is en-\n\ntirely predictable following the discussion in [Section 2.2](#4). The linear\n\nmodel fails when *c* ≈ 0 and so is likely to be worst near first break-\n\nthough, which corresponds to *c(L,t)*  *cin*. Of course this would\n\nsuggest that the linear sink always fails at first breakthrough yet\n\nthis is clearly not the case. The Myers model with a linear sink was\n\napplied to the adsorption of CO2, congo red dye and amoxicillin in\n\nMyers and Font [\\[22\\]](#15). First breakthrough data was not available for\n\nthe dye but for CO2 the model did not capture the first data points\n\nwhile for amoxicillin the agreement with experimental data was\n\nexcellent for all time. In the next example we will also see the lin-\n\near sink apparently performs well.\n\nThe adsorption constants obtained from fitting [Eqs. (41) ](#6)and\n\n[(47) ](#6)to the data are given in [Table 1](#8). Note the constants have\n\ndifferent units due to the model forms, for the linear adsorp-\n\ntion equation *qt* = *kL(qe* − *q)* and hence \\[*kL*\\] =1/s, for the nonlin-\n\near model *qt* ∼ *kadc(qm* − *q)* and \\[*kad*\\] =m3/(kg s). From the table\n\nwe see that with the linear sink *kL* varies almost linearly with\n\n*cin*, increasing by a factor of around 6.3 while the inlet concen-\n\ntration increases by 6.9. So, not only is the linear model a worse\n\nfit to the data but the adsorption coefficient varies with con-\n\ncentration, indicating the model assumptions are incorrect. The\n\nnonlinear sink model shows a much smaller variation in adsorp-\n\ntion coefficient with a maximum of approximately 30% (although\n\nthe close agreement between the lowest and highest *cin* values\n\nalso suggests a possible inaccuracy with the data of the mid-\n\n**Table 1**\n\nAdsorption parameters obtained by fitting[ ](#6)[Eqs. (41)](#6) and [(47)](#6) to the data for three\n\ninlet concentrations of toluene.\n\n*cin* × 10 3 \\[kg/m 3\\]\n\n0.41\n\n1.32\n\n2.84\n\n*kL* × 10 3 \\[s −1 \\]\n\n0.34\n\n1.32\n\n2.14\n\n*kad* \\[m 3 kg −1 s −1 \\]\n\n1.12\n\n1.53\n\n1.13\n\n**Table 2**\n\nComparison of half adsorption times between the experimental value of *t*1*/*2 and\n\nthe reduced Bohart–Adams model,[ ](#7)[Eq. (54)](#7) where *tBA*\n\n1*/*2 = *ρbqmL/(uincin )*.\n\n*cin* × 10 3 \\[kg/m 3\\]\n\n0.41\n\n1.32\n\n2.84\n\n*t*1*/*2 \\[min\\]\n\n459.4\n\n183.7\n\n98.7\n\n*tBA*\n\n1*/*2 \\[min\\]\n\n681.5\n\n211.81\n\n98.3\n\ndle case). This small variation in *kad* suggests that the nonlin-\n\near sink model captures the physics well without violating model\n\nassumptions.\n\nAn important claim made earlier was that the Bohart–Adams\n\nmethod does not work yet we have not proved this by plotting\n\nthe result on [Fig. 2](#8). To explain why we have omitted the BA\n\ngraph consider the time *t*1*/*2 which may be calculated by set-\n\nting *c(L,t)* = *cin/*2 in the BA model, [Eq. (54)](#7). This demonstrates\n\nthat *tBA*\n\n1*/*2 = *ρbqmL/(uincin )*. In the experiments *ρb* = 377*.*25 kg/m3,\n\n*qm* = 0*.*36 kg/kg, *L* = 0*.*0054 m,  = 0*.*336, *u* = 0*.*13 m/s (the *qm*\n\nvalue is calculated from the isotherm in [Section 5](#9)). This leads to\n\n*tBA*\n\n1*/*2 ≈ 16*.*8*/cin*. The results of this calculation are shown in [Table 2](#8)\n\nfor the three values of *cin*. It is clear that only for one case is the BA\n\ntime close to the experimental value. For the lowest concentration\n\nBohart–Adams predicts *tBA*\n\n1*/*2 ≈ 680 min, almost 1.5 times the exper-\n\nimental value. A plot of the BA curve could have the same shape\n\nas the nonlinear result, after choosing the *kad* value provided in\n\n[Table 1](#8), but shifted by approximately 220 min to the right. That is,\n\nthe BA result is out by nearly four hours. The correspondence im-\n\nproves with increasing *cin*: for *cin* = 1*.*32 × 10−3 kg/m3 the curve is\n\nout by 30 min while for *cin* = 2*.*84 × 10−3 kg/m3 the result is al-\n\nmost in line with the experimental data. Given the vast difference\n\nin times for the low concentration results it is clear that in gen-\n\neral the Bohart–Addams solution should not be applied. There re-\n\nmains the possibility that the error lays with the neglected terms.\n\nFirst, the full BA model contains the term exp *(*−*kadcint)* but this\n\nvaries between 10−6 and 10−14 for the three cases and so is clearly\n\nnegligible while the Amundson modification shifts time by *t* =\n\n*L/uin* = 0*.*04 s which is also clearly irrelevant. The coefficient of the\n\nYoon–Nelson model is related to the Myers model via *kYN* = *kadcin*.\n\nSince *kad* is approximately constant Yoon–Nelson must have an\n\napproximately linear variation with *cin*, contradicting the assump-\n\ntion of a constant adsorption coefficient. Hence Yoon–Nelson also\n\nfails.\n\nIn [Fig. 3 ](#9)we compare the two Myers’ models with data taken\n\nfrom [Fig. 4](#9)(c), (e) and (f) of Barros et al. [\\[5\\] ](#15)which concern the ad-\n\nsorption of Cr(III) on zeolite NaX. Somewhat surprisingly it is now\n\nthe linear sink (dashed line) that captures the data well, the non-\n\nlinear sink (solid line) provides rather poor agreement. The cor-\n\nresponding adsorption coefficients are presented in [Table 3](#9). Here\n\n8\n\n-----\n\n|  |\n| :-: |\n| [**Page 9**]() |\n\n*T.G. Myers, A. Cabrera-Codony and A. Valverde*\n\n*International Journal of Heat and Mass Transfer 202 (2023) 123660*\n\n**Fig. 3.** Comparison of models for Cr(III) adsorption on zeolite NaX using data taken from figures 4c,4e and 4f of Barros et al[ ](#15)[\\[5\\]](#15) . Solid line [(47)](#6) , dashed line [(41)](#6) , circles\n\nrepresent data points.\n\n**Table 3**\n\nAdsorption parameters obtained by fitting[ ](#6)[Eqs. (41)](#6) and [(47)](#6) to the data for three\n\ninlet concentrations of Barros et al.[ ](#15)[\\[5\\]](#15) .\n\n*cin* × 10 3 \\[kg/m 3\\]\n\n0.9\n\n1.9\n\n2.9\n\n*kL* ×10 4 \\[s −1 \\]\n\n1.93\n\n2.28\n\n3.95\n\n*kad* \\[m 3 kg −1 s −1 \\]\n\n0.63\n\n0.31\n\n0.32\n\n**Table 4**\n\nComparison of half adsorption times between the experimental value for Cr(III) and\n\nreduced Bohart–Adams,[ ](#7)[Eq. (54)](#7) .\n\n*cin* × 10 3 \\[kg/m 3\\]\n\n0.9\n\n1.9\n\n2.9\n\n*t*1*/*2 \\[min\\]\n\n83.3\n\n73.2\n\n51.7\n\n*tBA*\n\n1*/*2 \\[min\\]\n\n189.1\n\n95.1\n\n62.2\n\nwe see that the good fit is at the expense of breaking the model\n\nassumptions: *kL* varies strongly with *cin* so, although the linear sink\n\ncan provide the correct shape it still cannot be claimed to work.\n\nSince the BA, Yoon–Nelson, BDST and other formulations take the\n\nform of the nonlinear model they will also fail.\n\nAgain to demonstrate the inaccuracy of the BA model we\n\npresent the times *t*1*/*2, *tBA*\n\n1*/*2 = *ρbqmL/(uincin )* in [Table 4](#9). The full BA\n\nmodel only leads to changes of at most one minute, so we again\n\nneglect this. The Amundson modification changes times by ap-\n\nproximately three seconds. As before the correspondence increases\n\nwith *cin*. For the lowest inlet concentration *cin* = 0*.*9 × 10−3 kg/m3\n\nthe BA value is approximately 2.3 times greater than the cor-\n\nrect value. When *cin* = 2*.*9 × 10−3 kg/m3 the difference is down to\n\n11 min, an approximately 20% error.\n\n**5. Sips sorption model**\n\n*5.1. Sips mass sink*\n\nIn the previous section we have observed that for one data set\n\nthe Myers model, with a nonlinear sink provides excellent agree-\n\nment whilst satisfying the model assumptions. With the second\n\ndata set the nonlinear sink failed while the linear could match\n\nthe data but at the expense of using a concentration dependent\n\nadsorption coefficient and so violating the model assumptions. Al-\n\nthough we have shown that the BA model fails in all cases it is\n\nalso true that any model with the form of [Eq. (2) ](#2)would break the\n\nmodel assumptions for the second data set. This inability to accu-\n\nrately match all data suggests different mechanisms are occurring\n\nin different cases and that the models have ranges of applicabil-\n\nity. In an attempt to better understand the problem we now use a\n\nfurther piece of information from the experiments.\n\nIn [Fig. 4 ](#9)we present the equilibrium isotherms using data\n\nfor toluene adsorption and Cr(III). Circles represent data and the\n\ndashed line the best fit by least-squares using an interior-point al-\n\ngorithm to the linear form of the Langmuir isotherm\n\n1\n\n*qe*\n\n\\=\n\n1\n\n*qm*\n\n\\+\n\n1\n\n*qmKL*\n\n1\n\n*cin*\n\n*,*\n\n(56)\n\nwhich follows by rearranging [(12)](#4). The *qe* values are reached when\n\nthe process ends and the adsorbent is saturated, so the equilibrium\n\nconcentration is the inlet concentration.\n\nThe best fit by least-squares of the Langmuir isotherm de-\n\ntermines *qm* and *KL* = *kad/kde*. In the left figure the linear form\n\n(obscured by the solid line) gives a reasonable approximation\n\n**Fig. 4.** Equilibrium isotherms for the Langmuir[ ](#9)[(56)](#9) (dashed line) and Sips[ ](#10)[(58)](#10) (solid line) expressions. On the left, experimental data for toluene, and on the right for the\n\nCr(III) data of Barros et al.[ ](#15)[\\[5\\]](#15) .\n\n9\n\n-----\n\n|  |\n| :-: |\n| [**Page 10**]() |\n\n*T.G. Myers, A. Cabrera-Codony and A. Valverde*\n\n*International Journal of Heat and Mass Transfer 202 (2023) 123660*\n\nhowever in the right figure the trend is clearly nonlinear and the\n\nLangmuir isotherm does not work. The isotherm for the BA model\n\nis not shown since it involves a constant value *qe* = *qm* which\n\nwould show up as a (unrealistic) horizontal line on the graphs. In\n\nthis case the value of *qm* could be obtained, for example, by mea-\n\nsuring the column’s initial and final weight.\n\nMotivated by the failure of Langmuir for Cr(III) sorption we now\n\ninvestigate the Sips model, as discussed in [Section 2.2](#4), which has\n\na nonlinear form\n\n*∂q*\n\n*∂t*\n\n\\= *kmcm(qm* − *q)* − *kdeq .*\n\n(57)\n\nThis is based on the assumption that adsorption does not depend\n\nlinearly on the partial pressure and so introduces a power law\n\nform. Recall the linear relation stems from an ideal gas assumption,\n\nit is therefore unsurprising that it fails on a data set for adsorption\n\nfrom water. The corresponding Sips isotherm is\n\n*qe* =\n\n*qmKScm*\n\n*e*\n\n1 + *KScm*\n\n*e*\n\n*,*\n\n(58)\n\nwhere *ce* = *cin* in a column experiment and *KS* = *km/kde*. We re-\n\nstrict the range of *m* such that 0 ≤ *m* ≤ 1 (this choice will be justi-\n\nfied in the next section): *m* = 0 recovers the rectangular isotherm\n\nwhile *m* = 1 is the Langmuir expression. A more general form of\n\n[(57) ](#10)involves *(qm* − *q)n*. We do not use this here since the shape of\n\nthe isotherm is determined by the ratio *m/n* and so invoking just\n\nthe single parameter *m* is sufficient to predict the behaviour. Cor-\n\nresponding to the linear Langmuir form the solid line in [Fig. 4 ](#9)rep-\n\nresents\n\n1\n\n*qe*\n\n\\=\n\n1\n\n*qm*\n\n\\+\n\n1\n\n*qmKs*\n\n1\n\n*cm*\n\n*in*\n\n*.*\n\n(59)\n\nUsing this on the Cr(III) data provides a much better fit than Lang-\n\nmuir.\n\nThe parameters obtained from fitting the Langmuir and Sips\n\nisotherms are presented in [Table 5](#10). For toluene, where the non-\n\nlinear sink captured the breakthrough data well, the Sips isotherm\n\nhas *m* = 0*.*994 and so is very close to the Langmuir isotherm. The\n\nsmall difference could easily be attributed to experimental error.\n\nSimilarly the values of *KL, KS* and *qm* are very close. In order to\n\nquantify the goodness of the fit, the Sum of Squares Errors (SSE)\n\nare given in the final row of [Table 5](#10). Many authors present only\n\nthe coefficient of determination *R*2, the proportion of the variation\n\nin the dependent variable, as an indicator of the goodness of fit. In-\n\nterpreting a good fitting only based on this coefficient is incorrect,\n\nsince even a poor fitting can result in a high *R*2. It is also neces-\n\nsary to examine the SSE. A high *R*2 combined with a relatively low\n\nSSE and a good assessment of the residuals distribution is a better\n\napproach [\\[15\\]](#15). This criteria has also been used in Darweesh and\n\nAhmed [\\[12\\]](#15). The problem with the SSE is that its size depends on\n\nthe size of the quantity being measured as well as number of data\n\npoints so, for example in the left hand of [Fig. 4 ](#9)since 1*/qe* varies\n\nbetween 3 and 4.2 we expect a low SSE. With the right hand 1*/qe*\n\nvaries between 300 and 700 and we expect a high SSE. The SSE\n\nshould then be used for a comparison between models, rather than\n\nas an absolute value. In all the examples shown in [Table 5 ](#10)the *R*2\n\n**Table 5**\n\nParameters obtained from fitting Langmuir [(12)](#4) and Sips isotherms[ ](#10)[(58)](#10) to experi-\n\nmental data for toluene and Cr(III) sorption. SSE: *Sum of Squares Error*.\n\nToluene\n\nCr(III)\n\nLangmuir\n\nSips\n\nLangmuir\n\nSips\n\n*m*\n\n1\n\n0.994\n\n1\n\n0.53\n\n*KL* or *KS* \\[(m 3 /kg) *m* \\]\n\n5200.5\n\n4322.8\n\n2443.1\n\n19.3\n\n*qm* \\[kg/kg\\]\n\n0.358\n\n0.363\n\n0.0036\n\n0.007\n\nSSE\n\n0.0443\n\n0.0443\n\n1550\n\n464.7\n\nvalue is very close to unity, so we do not show this criteria. For the\n\ntoluene data the SSE takes almost the same value for both models\n\nand consequently we conclude that either one is a good fit. With\n\nCr(III), where both previous breakthrough models failed, *R*2 is again\n\nhigh in both cases, but Sips has an SSE almost a third the value of\n\nLangmuir. In this case it is clear that Sips is preferable. The Sips\n\nisotherm has *m* ≈ 0*.*53, which is far from both the Langmuir value\n\nor the rectangular isotherm. This explains why both models failed\n\nfor this case (despite showing a high *R*2).\n\nThis analysis demonstrates the importance of examining both\n\nthe isotherm and breakthrough data. The isotherm determines the\n\nform of the sink model, which then determines the form of break-\n\nthrough. Since it is only the Sips isotherm that applies to both\n\ncases we now proceed to extend the travelling wave solutions to\n\nthe Sips model.\n\n*5.2. Travelling wave approximation*\n\nIn non-dimensional form [Eq. (57) ](#10)becomes\n\n*∂* ˆ*q*\n\n*∂*ˆ*t*\n\n\\= ˆ*cm(*1 − ˆ*q)* − *δ*3 ˆ*q ,*\n\n(60)\n\nwhere the length-scale is now *L* = *uc*1−*m*\n\n*in*\n\n*/(ρbqmkm )* and the\n\nadsorption time-scale is *τ* = 1*/(cm*\n\n*in*\n\n*km )*. These values affect *δ*1 =\n\n*L/(uτ)*, *δ*2 = *D/(uL)* and *δ*3 = *kde/(kmcm*\n\n*in )*.\n\nFollowing the same path as for the Myers’ models we obtain\n\n*F* =\n\n*G*\n\n*Ge*\n\n*,*\n\n−ˆ*v*\n\n*∂G*\n\n*∂η*\n\n\\= *Fm(*1 − *G)* − *δ*3 *G ,*\n\n(61)\n\nwhere\n\n*Ge* =\n\n1\n\n1 + *δ*3\n\n*,*\n\nˆ*v* =\n\n1 + *δ*3\n\n1 + *δ*1*(*1 + *δ*3 *)*\n\n*,*\n\n(62)\n\nare identical to the nonlinear model values.\n\nExcept for when *m* is an integer the analytical solution of the\n\nsystem for *F* or *G* takes the form of an infinite series. To avoid this\n\nwe look for an approximate solution which, if carried on, would\n\nreproduce the infinite series. Writing *F* = 1 − *(*1 − *F )* we may ex-\n\npress *Fm* as\n\n*Fm* = 1 − *m(*1 − *F)* +\n\n*m(m* − 1*)*\n\n2\n\n*(*1 − *F)*2 + *. . .*\n\n(63)\n\nFor integer *m* the result is correct regardless of the value of *F*. For\n\nnon-integer *m*, since *F, m* ∈ \\[0*,* 1\\], we see that neglecting higher or-\n\nder terms produces the greatest error near *F* = 0, *m* = 1*/*2. Retain-\n\ning only the linear term and replacing *G* = *GeF* the sink term be-\n\ncomes\n\n−ˆ*vGe*\n\n*∂F*\n\n*∂η*\n\n≈ *(*1 − *m(*1 − *F))(*1 − *GeF)* − *δ*3*GeF .*\n\n(64)\n\nWriting *δ*3 = *(*1 − *Ge )/Ge* the right hand side may be rearranged to\n\n*(*1 − *m(*1 − *F))(*1 − *GeF)* − *δ*3*GeF* = *(*1 − *F)(*1 − *m* + *mGeF).*(65)\n\nNow consider the different possible cases:\n\n**m** = **0**\n\nHere there is a single root *F* = 1 and the resultant differential\n\nequation is equivalent to the first equation of [(30) ](#5)resulting in the\n\nform given by [Eq. (37) ](#6)(see below).\n\n**0** *\\\u003c* **m** *\\\u003c* **1**\n\nNow there are two roots *F*1 = 1, *F*2 = *(m* − 1*)/mGe* such that\n\n−ˆ*v*\n\n*∂F*\n\n*∂η*\n\n≈ *(*1 − *F)(F* − *F*2 *)m .*\n\n(66)\n\nThese roots define the turning points of the equation, subject to\n\nthe restriction that *F* ∈ \\[0*,* 1\\]. Since 0 *\\\u003c m \\\u003c* 1 we may write *F*2 =\n\n10\n\n-----\n\n|  |\n| :-: |\n| [**Page 11**]() |\n\n*T.G. Myers, A. Cabrera-Codony and A. Valverde*\n\n*International Journal of Heat and Mass Transfer 202 (2023) 123660*\n\n−*ω*2 *\\\u003c* 0, where *ω* is real giving *F*  = 0 when *F* = 1*,* −*ω*2. The first\n\nstationary point coincides with the far-field, *F*  → 0 as *F* → 1. The\n\nsecond stationary point occurs when *F* = *F*2 *\\\u003c* 0. To reach this point\n\nthe solution must pass through zero at a finite point. Physically\n\nthis is impossible, instead what we will see is that *F* reaches zero\n\nwith a finite, negative gradient somewhere within the *η* domain\n\nand our solution stops there. Since the governing equation is au-\n\ntonomous and the origin may be shifted arbitrarily we may define\n\nthe point where *F* = 0 as *η* = 0.\n\n**m** = **1**\n\nIf *m* = 1 the equilibria occur at *F* = 0*,* 1. In this case as *F* → 0\n\nthe gradient *F*  → 0 meaning that zero is only reached as *η* → ∞.\n\nThis is the borderline case, where *F* = 0 is the asymptote.\n\n**m** *\\\u003e* **1**\n\nFinally, for *m \\\u003e* 1 we write *F*2 = *ω*2 *\\\u003e* 0 and the equilibria both\n\nhave *F \\\u003e* 0: the curve moves between *F*1 and *F*2 without ever\n\nreaching zero. Clearly this corresponds to an impractical adsorp-\n\ntion system and is a highly undesirable feature.\n\nTo summarise, when using the Sips model:\n\nIf 0 ≤ *m \\\u003c* 1 the sink is sufficiently strong everywhere that the\n\nconcentration reaches zero in a finite distance;\n\nIf *m* ≥ 1 the sink becomes too weak near the wave front such\n\nthat the concentration never reaches zero.\n\nConsequently we restrict *m* ∈ \\[0*,* 1\\].\n\nTo clarify these statements consider the situation where 0 *\\\u003c*\n\n*m* ≤ 1, such that *F*2 = *(m* − 1*)/mGe* = −*ω*2 ≤ 0\n\n−ˆ*v*\n\n*∂F*\n\n*∂η*\n\n\\= *m(*1 − *F)(F* + *ω*2 *),*\n\n(67)\n\nso theoretically *F* decreases from unity in the far-field to −*ω*2 as\n\n*η* → ∞. The solution is\n\n*F* =\n\n1 − *A*0*ω*2 exp*(m(*1 + *ω*2 *)η/*ˆ*v)*\n\n1 + *A*0 exp*(m(*1 + *ω*2 *)η/*ˆ*v)*\n\n(68)\n\nwhere *A*0 is a constant of integration. In the limit *η* → −∞ the\n\nconcentration *F* → 1. In the limit *η* → ∞, *F* → *(*−*A*0*ω*2 exp*(m(*1 +\n\n*ω*2 *)η/*ˆ*v))/(A*0 exp*(m(*1 + *ω*2 *)η/*ˆ*v))* = −*ω*2. The fact that *F* is a con-\n\ntinuous function which changes from a positive to a negative value\n\nmeans that for some finite *η* it must satisfy *F* = 0. By choosing\n\n*A*0 = 1*/ω*2 we may define this to occur at *η* = 0, i.e. *F(*0*)* = 0.\n\nWhen *m* = 1, *F*2 = −*ω*2 = *(m* − 1*)/mGe* = 0, that is the second\n\nroot is *F*2 = 0 so this is the borderline case. [Eq. (68) ](#11)reduces to\n\n*F* =\n\n1\n\n1 + *A*0 exp*(η/*ˆ*v)*\n\n*.*\n\n(69)\n\nAs required *F* → 1 as *η* → −∞ but *F* → 0 only when *η* → ∞: there\n\nis no finite solution *F(η)* = 0.\n\nWriting *η* = ˆ*x* − ˆ*s*0 − ˆ*v*ˆ*t* and using *F* = *c* = 1*/*2 at *t* = *t*1*/*2 we may\n\nsimultaneously eliminate the *A*0*, s*0 terms. The dimensional con-\n\ncentration then becomes\n\n*c*\n\n*cin*\n\n\\=\n\n*mGe* + 2*(*1 − *m)* − *(*1 − *m)* exp\n\n*(mGe* + 1 − *m)kmcm*\n\n*in (x* − *L* − *v(t* − *t*1*/*2 *))/(vGe )*\n\n*mGe* + 2*(*1 − *m)* + *mGe* exp\n\n*(mGe* + 1 − *m)kmcm*\n\n*in (x* − *L* − *v(t* − *t*1*/*2 *))/(vGe )*\n\n *,*\n\n(70)\n\nwhere *Ge* = 1*/(*1 + *kde/(kmcm*\n\n*in ))* = 1*/(*1 + 1*/(KScm*\n\n*in ))*. In the limit\n\n*m* = 1 this reduces to the nonlinear Myers model, [Eq. (45) ](#6)(pro-\n\nvided we identify *km* = *kad*). In the case *m* = 0 the form of\n\n[Eq. (39) ](#6)is obtained. The wave velocity is\n\n*v* =\n\n*u*\n\n1 + *ρbqm/((cin* + *kde/(kmcm*−1\n\n*in*\n\n*)))*\n\n*.*\n\n(71)\n\nNow the model is defined we return to finding the optimal param-\n\neter values. [Table 5 ](#10)provides values for *m, qm, KS* (or *KL*) obtained\n\nfrom the isotherm data which leaves *km* as the only unknown in\n\n**Table 6**\n\nParameters obtained from fitting[ ](#11)[Eq. (70)](#11) to toluene data, using the Sips isotherm.\n\n*cin* × 10 3 \\[kg/m 3\\]\n\n0.41\n\n1.32\n\n2.84\n\n*m*\n\n0.9992\n\n0.9986\n\n1.0058\n\n*km* \\[(m 3 /kg) *m* s −1 \\]\n\n1.11\n\n1.51\n\n1.19\n\n*KS* \\[(m 3 /kg) *m* \\]\n\n5210\n\n5180\n\n5470\n\n*q*exp\n\n*e*\n\n\\[kg/kg\\]\n\n0.29\n\n0.31\n\n0.32\n\n*qe* \\[kg/kg\\]\n\n0.25\n\n0.31\n\n0.33\n\n**Table 7**\n\nParameters obtained from fitting[ ](#11)[Eq.
