(70)](#11). However, the isotherm involves a relatively low number of\n\ndata points whereas the breakthrough data comprises many points.\n\nFor this reason we compromise and use the breakthrough data to\n\ndetermine *m, km* and the isotherm for *qm, KS*. Specifically the op-\n\ntimisation routine for *km, m* includes the isotherm function, so for\n\neach *m* the appropriate values for *qm, KS* are calculated. Later we\n\nwill see that the *m* values calculated this way agree well with\n\nthose obtained from the isotherm alone, providing a good level of\n\nconfidence in the results. With *km* and *KS* known the desorption\n\ncoefficient *kde* = *km/KS* (or *kde* = *kad/KL*) may be calculated.\n\nResults of fitting the breakthrough isotherm models to the\n\ntoluene data are presented in [Table 6](#11). The values of *m* are all\n\nclose to unity (the final value generated by the optimisation rou-\n\ntine should strictly be rounded down to unity), so agreeing with\n\nthe value obtained from the isotherm alone and confirming that\n\nthis process may be described by a nonlinear sink Myers model.\n\nCrucially the variation of *km* is small. In all cases the value *qm* ≈\n\n0*.*35 kg/kg was obtained, again matching the isotherm predictions.\n\nThe ratio *KS* = *km/kde* shows little variation and is slightly higher\n\nthan that obtained from the Sips isotherm alone (but in good\n\nagreement with the Langmuir isotherm value). The average *kde* =\n\n*km/KS* ≈ 2*.*4 × 10−4 s−1 is reassuringly lower than *km* verifying that\n\nadsorption dominates with this process. The final two rows com-\n\npare the experimental value of *qe* used to plot the isotherm and\n\nthat predicted by *qe* = *qm/(*1 + 1*/(KScm*\n\n*in ))*.\n\nThe breakthrough curves for toluene corresponding to the re-\n\nsults presented in [Table 6 ](#11)are shown in [Fig. 5](#12). The nonlinear and\n\nSips isotherms results are indistinguishable. The linear sink, as be-\n\nfore, misses the early breakthrough data but merges with the other\n\ntwo models from around *t*1*/*2.\n\nIt should come as no surprise that the Sips and nonlinear\n\nisotherm models lead to almost identical results for toluene, given\n\nthe similarity in the isotherm parameters. On the other hand the\n\nCr(III) models showed a significantly different trend. The parameter\n\nvalues obtained in this case using the breakthrough and isotherm\n\ndata are shown in [Table 7](#11). The predictions for *m* are close to those\n\nobtained by the isotherm alone, however the *KS* values are around\n\n50% higher. This could be due to the lack of isotherm data over the\n\ncentral region. In all cases the value *qm* ≈ 0*.*0062 kg/kg, which is\n\nslightly lower than the value predicted from the isotherm. The ad-\n\nsorption coefficient is relatively constant, so conforming with the\n\nmodel assumptions. This leads to an average *kde* ≈ 2*.*4 × 10−4 s−1\n\n(similar to toluene). The predicted and experimental *qe* values\n\nmatch very well.\n\nThe breakthrough curves are presented in [Fig. 6](#12). The values of\n\n*m*, close to 0.55, force the curve away from the nonlinear sink\n\nmodel. In all cases the Sips model provides good agreement with\n\n11\n\n-----\n\n|  |\n| :-: |\n| [**Page 12**]() |\n\n*T.G. Myers, A. Cabrera-Codony and A. Valverde*\n\n*International Journal of Heat and Mass Transfer 202 (2023) 123660*\n\n**Fig. 5.** Comparison of models for toluene adsorption on steam activated carbon. Solid line Sips sink[ ](#11)[(70)](#11) , dotted line nonlinear [(47)](#6) , dashed line linear [(41)](#6) , circles represent\n\ndata points.\n\n**Fig. 6.** Comparison of models for Cr(III) adsorption on zeolite NaX using data reported in Barros et al.[ ](#15)[\\[5\\]](#15) . Solid line [(70)](#11) , dotted line [(47)](#6) , dashed line [(41)](#6) .\n\ndata. However, it is worth pointing out that for the lowest concen-\n\ntration the linear sink provides slightly better agreement, but recall\n\nthat this model involves a concentration dependent *kad* and so is\n\nnot valid.\n\nThe time when the adsorbent is exhausted is directly related\n\nto the wave velocity. The most general form is given by the Sips\n\nmodel\n\n*v* =\n\n*uin*\n\n1 + *ρbqm/((cin* + *kde/(kmcm*−1\n\n*in*\n\n*)))*\n\n*.*\n\n(72)\n\nThe exhaustion time may then be estimated through *te* ≈ *L/v*\n\n*te* ≈\n\n*L*\n\n*v*\n\n\\=\n\n*L*\n\n*uin*\n\n1 +\n\n*ρbqm*\n\n*(cin* + *kde/(kmcm*−1\n\n*in*\n\n*))*\n\n*.*\n\n(73)\n\nNote, since this does not account for the initial transient it merely\n\nprovides an estimate for exhaustion. It will become more accurate\n\nas the column length increases or wave speed decreases. In this\n\nsense it should work well for large scale industrial processes and\n\nstruggle with small laboratory experiments. From [Eq. (73) ](#12)we see\n\nclearly that the exhaustion time increases linearly with the col-\n\numn length and decreases linearly as *uin* increases. Bulk density\n\nand capacity of the adsorbent also provide a linear increase while\n\nincreases in inlet concentration, void fraction and desorption coef-\n\nficient decrease the time. Although most of these appear obvious\n\nthe explicit relation has not been previously presented.\n\nThe parameter values obtained in this and the preceding sec-\n\ntions permit us to calculate the model scales. For the three toluene\n\nexamples the length scale is *L* = 0*.*289, 0.21, 0.285 ×10−3 m and\n\nthe time-scale *τ* = 36*.*45, 8.242, 5.192 min. This indicates that the\n\nreaction zone is typically a fraction of a millimetre thick. The time-\n\nscales may be verified by examining the width of the breakthrough\n\ncurves in [Fig. 5](#12). For Cr(III) we find *L* = 5*.*53*,* 8*.*57*,* 7*.*7 × 10−3 m,\n\nthe length-scale is between 5 and 8 mm, while the column is\n\n16 mm and particle diameter is 0.18 mm. The time-scale *τ* =\n\n112*.*314*,* 87*.*412*,* 51*.*287 min, again this is in line with the experi-\n\nmental results.\n\n**6. Model predictions for a varying flow rate**\n\nIn the previous section, we have followed the conventional ap-\n\nproach of studying different inlet concentrations with all other sys-\n\ntem parameters fixed. However, if our goal is to use the model\n\nto help optimise the adsorption process then it should also be\n\ntested by varying other quantities. In the literature there is less\n\ndata concerned with constant *cin* but Barros et al. [\\[6\\] ](#15)provide good\n\ndata for the adsorption of Cr(III) on zeolite NaX at different in-\n\nlet flow rates. Consequently we also carried out experiments with\n\ntoluene using the same conditions as presented in the previous\n\nsection for an inlet concentration of 2.84 × 10−3 kg/m3, but at 100\n\nand 205 mL/min, leading to an interstitial velocity of 0.064 and\n\n0.13 m/s, respectively.\n\nThe final amount adsorbed should be the same for different\n\nflow velocities, even though the approach to that value may dif-\n\nfer. Consequently changes in interstitial velocity should not affect\n\nthe isotherm, so, from the previous section we expect the non-\n\nlinear model (or Sips with *m* = 1) to hold for toluene with *qm* =\n\n0*.*358 kg/kg, *KL* = 5200*.*5 m3/(kg s), see [Table 5](#10). For Cr(III) the Sips\n\nmodel is best and we take mean values *qm* = 0*.*0062 kg/kg, *KS* = 29\n\n(m3/kg)0*.*55 s−1 and *m* = 0*.*55 from [Table 7](#11). In [Fig. 7](#13)(a) we show re-\n\nsults with the two inlet velocities for toluene. [Fig. 7](#13)(b) shows two\n\nprofiles corresponding to the Cr(III) data with an inlet flux of 7,\n\n9 ml/minute. In both figures only a single parameter, *km* or *kad*,\n\nhas been used to fit the breakthrough data. As expected (hoped\n\nfor) the agreement is good in all cases.\n\n12\n\n-----\n\n|  |\n| :-: |\n| [**Page 13**]() |\n\n*T.G. Myers, A. Cabrera-Codony and A. Valverde*\n\n*International Journal of Heat and Mass Transfer 202 (2023) 123660*\n\n**Fig. 7.** (a) Toluene adsorption experiments at 100 and 205 mL/min (i.e. 0.064 and 0.13 m/s) fitted using the nonlinear model based on Langmuir equation. (b) Cr(III)\n\nadsorption experiments at 7 and 9 mL/min (i.e. 0.0047 and 0.006 m/s) fitted using the Sips model.\n\n**Table 8**\n\nParameters obtained from fitting the nonlinear model, [(47)](#6) , to toluene adsorption data, and Sips model [(70)](#11) to Cr(III) ad-\n\nsorption [\\[6\\]](#15) at diverse flow rates. For toluene sorption: *qm* = 0 *.*358 kg/kg and *KL* = 5200 *.*5 m 3/(kg s). For Cr(III) sorption:\n\n*qm* = 0 *.*0062 kg/kg, *KS* = 29 (m 3/kg) 0*.*55 s −1 and *m* = 0 *.*55 .\n\nExperimental data\n\nToluene\n\nCr(III)\n\nFlow rate \\[mL/min\\]\n\n100\n\n205\n\n7\n\n9\n\nu *in* ×10 2 \\[m/s\\]\n\n6.4\n\n13.0\n\n0.47\n\n0.60\n\nt 1*/*2 \\[min\\]\n\n194.2\n\n98.7\n\n1555.0\n\n1206.4\n\n*kad* \\[m 3 /(kg s)\\] or *km* × 10 3 \\[(m 3 /kg) 0*.*55 s −1 \\]\n\n0.58\n\n1.08\n\n1.37\n\n1.46\n\n*kde* × 10 4 \\[s −1 \\]\n\n1.11\n\n2.08\n\n0.47\n\n0.50\n\n[Fig. 7 ](#13)shows excellent agreement between the models and the\n\nexperimental data at diverse flow rates. This is specially significant,\n\nsince for each set of data only one parameter has been fitted: *kad*\n\nor *km* depending on the model. Note that *kde* is determined by\n\n*kde* = *KL/kad* or *kde* = *KS/km*, where the values of *KL* and *KS* have\n\nbeen determined in previous sections, as well as *m* and *qm*.\n\n[Table 8 ](#13)gives the obtained adsorption and desorption constants\n\nas well as the flow rate, Reynolds number, *t*1*/*2 and *kde*. The re-\n\nsults show a clear pattern of increasing *kad* (or *km*) and *kde* with\n\nincreasing flow rate for both toluene and Cr(III) adsorption data.\n\nThis behaviour is more significant in toluene experiments, proba-\n\nbly because of the greater variation in the flow rate. For toluene\n\nthe flow rate more than doubles while the adsorption coefficient\n\nincreases by a factor of approximately 1.86. For Cr(III) the flow rate\n\nincreases by a factor of approximately 1.29 while *km* has increased\n\nby a factor 1.07.\n\nEquations [(71) ](#11)and [(72) ](#12)indicate that wave velocity is propor-\n\ntional to the interstitial velocity and so the time taken to tra-\n\nverse the column is proportional to 1*/uin*, this is confirmed by the\n\nchange in *t*1*/*2. For the toluene cases as the velocity increases by\n\na factor 0*.*13*/*0*.*064 ≈ 2*.*03 the time taken for the mid-point of the\n\nwave to reach the exit decreases by a factor 194*.*2*/*98*.*7 ≈ 1*.*97. For\n\nCr(III) the interstitial velocity increases by a factor 0*.*6*/*0*.*47 ≈ 1*.*28\n\nwhile *t*1*/*2 decreases by the same amount 1555*/*1206*.*4 ≈ 1*.*28.\n\n**7. Conclusions**\n\nThe motivation for this study was to define and solve a mathe-\n\nmatical model to describe a wide range of breakthrough data and\n\nwhich was consistent with the model assumptions. Analytical solu-\n\ntions are particularly important since they clearly present the de-\n\npendence of the process on the operating parameters in a way not\n\npossible with numerical solutions. Although there already exists a\n\nnumber of accepted analytical solutions we have shown that in\n\ngeneral they do not work unless the physical meaning of param-\n\neters is neglected or constant values are allowed to vary.\n\nImportant conclusions from this work include:\n\n1\\. The Bohart–Adams model requires an unphysical initial condi-\n\ntion which defines the form of the breakthrough curve. Its per-\n\nceived success comes through neglecting the full solution and\n\ninstead lumping system parameters into two fitting parameters,\n\nwhen in practice there should be just a single unknown. Us-\n\ning actual operating conditions we demonstrated that predicted\n\nbreakthrough times could be completely unrealistic.\n\n2\\. Sink models are based on the assumption that adsorption\n\nand desorption rates are proportional to concentrations. Con-\n\nsequently the proportionality constant must be independent of\n\nthese concentrations. However the literature is full of studies\n\nshowing variable ”constants”, to the extent that review papers\n\nshow tables of the variation with inlet concentration. This is a\n\nclear indicator that the incorrect sink model has been applied.\n\nThe Sips model provides an added degree of freedom which, in\n\nthe cases investigated, leads to approximately constant coeffi-\n\ncients.\n\n3\\. A popular method to quantify the goodness of fit to data is\n\nthe use of the *R*2 test but even a poor fit can provide a value\n\n*R*2 ≈ 1. A Sum of Squares Error (SSE), or similar test, must also\n\nbe carried out when comparing different models. Consequently\n\nreported values of the adsorption coefficient may not provide\n\nthe best fit.\n\n4\\. The isotherm data should be plotted before starting any analy-\n\nsis. This demonstrates the type of sink model to be applied.\n\n5\\. The exponent *m* in the Sips model plays an important math-\n\nematical and physical role. As *m* increases we observe a de-\n\ncrease in the sink strength near the front of the concentration\n\n13\n\n-----\n\n|  |\n| :-: |\n| [**Page 14**]() |\n\n*T.G. Myers, A. Cabrera-Codony and A. Valverde*\n\n*International Journal of Heat and Mass Transfer 202 (2023) 123660*\n\nwave where *c* → 0. For 0 ≤ *m \\\u003c* 1 we found that concentration\n\nreaches zero within a finite distance. For the borderline case\n\n*m* = 1 the concentration, theoretically, asymptotes to zero since\n\nthe sink becomes too weak near the front to adsorb all mate-\n\nrial. Values *m \\\u003e* 1 lead to a concentration which never reaches\n\nzero and so suggests that the system is impractical for contam-\n\ninant removal. Consequently the *m* value is crucial to defining\n\nthe boundary condition on the outlet side of the flow.\n\nWhile this work has gone some way to consistently uniting\n\nmathematical predictions to experimental adsorption data there\n\nremain a number of open questions. For example, the current work\n\nis based on an assumption of constant fluid velocity meaning it\n\nis best applied to the removal of trace amounts of contaminant.\n\nA further assumption is that intra-particle diffusion occurs over a\n\nmuch shorter time-scale than the flow or adsorption scales while\n\naxial diffusion is negligible. A more complex sink model may be re-\n\nquired for different systems. Adjusting the analysis to account for\n\nthese effects will produce a significantly more general model. This\n\nwill be the focus of future studies.\n\n**Declaration of Competing Interest**\n\nThe authors have no affiliation with any organization with a di-\n\nrect or indirect financial interest in the subject matter discussed in\n\nthe manuscript\n\n**CRediT authorship contribution statement**\n\n**Timothy G. A. Cabrera-Codony was financed by\n\nIJC2020-045964-I/MCIN/AEI/10.13039/501100011033 and European\n\nUnion “NextGenerationEU/PRTR”.\n\n**Appendix A. Bohart–Adams breakthrough model**\n\nThe Bohart–Adams model involves balancing advection with the\n\nsink term, where the strength of the sink is represented by the\n\npure adsorption [Eq. (16)](#4). Here we work with the mass fraction, but\n\notherwise the derivation is identical to that of the original authors,\n\n[\\[8\\]](#15). Using the notation of [Eqs. Now I'm ready to extract and organize all the equations by their categories.

Compiling the fluid phase mass balance equations... Now I'm looking at several empirical correlations for the axial dispersion coefficient from different sources—Hu et al. provides a formulation combining molecular diffusion and convective dispersion, Lin et al. offers a simpler linear relationship, and Juela et al. gives a dimensionless correlation based on Reynolds number and bed porosity. I'm also starting to set up the Danckwerts boundary conditions section.

Writing mathematical equations... Writing kinetic equations... Now I'm looking at several empirical correlations for the Sherwood number to estimate the film mass transfer coefficient—the Wakao-Funazkri correlation with its Reynolds and Schmidt number dependence, followed by the Wilson-Geankoplis and Ohashi correlations that provide alternative formulations for different flow regimes.

Moving into the macropore diffusion equations, I'm setting up the partial differential equations that govern how the adsorbate concentration changes within the particle pores over time, accounting for both the fluid phase and adsorbed phase accumulation, with the diffusive flux depending on the pore diffusivity and concentration gradients. Now I'm accounting for how temperature affects both the micropore diffusivity and the barrier resistance rate constant through Arrhenius expressions. Then I'm deriving the overall LDF coefficient by combining the series resistances from film diffusion, macropore diffusion, and micropore diffusion, each weighted by the particle and crystal dimensions.

For the macropore-based LDF model, I'm setting up the mass balance equation that couples the pore concentration change with adsorbed phase accumulation, where the transfer coefficient incorporates both the macropore diffusivity and a Biot number correction factor. Finally, I'm integrating the pore concentration profile across the particle radius to get the average adsorbed amount.

Now moving to the gas phase energy balance, which accounts for axial heat conduction, convective heat transport, transient temperature changes, and heat transfer between the gas and solid phases through the interfacial area. Now I'm working through the remaining correlations for heat transfer—the wall heat transfer coefficient follows a similar Nusselt number relationship, and I need to capture how the effective axial dispersion and gas-solid heat transfer coefficients are defined using Chilton-Colburn analogy. Now I'm working through the momentum balance equations using the Ergun model, which breaks down the pressure gradient into viscous and inertial drag components based on bed porosity and particle diameter, then moving into specific pressure drop correlations for different reactor geometries like monoliths and foams. Now I'm working through the diffusivity equations—Knudsen diffusivity for pore transport, then combining it with molecular diffusivity using the Bosanquet equation to get the effective pore diffusivity, and finally the Wilke-Chang correlation for molecular diffusivity in gas mixtures. All 9 papers have been read and every unique equation extracted. The compendium contains 148 numbered equations across 23 sections, plus a full nomenclature table. Here's what's covered:

Governing PDEs (§1–2) — Dispersed plug-flow mass balance, continuity, Myers advection-diffusion form, non-dimensional form, all boundary/initial conditions.

Isotherms (§3) — Langmuir, Freundlich, Sips, Henry, and both linear fitting forms.

Kinetic rate equations (§4) — Full nonlinear Langmuir, pure adsorption, LDF, Sips, barrier micropore, and three non-dimensional forms.

Diffusion models (§5–7) — Macropore PDE, GRM pore-phase PDE, micropore distributed/barrier, Darken correction, Arrhenius, all series/lumped LDF coefficients (§7).

Transport correlations (§8–10) — Wakao–Funazkri, Wilson–Geankoplis, Ohashi (film); Wakao–Smith, Ruthven, Suzuki–Smith, Soriano (axial dispersion); Wilke–Chang, Knudsen, Bosanquet, pore/surface diffusivities.

Energy + momentum (§12–14) — Gas/solid/wall energy balances, five heat-transfer correlations, Ergun, monolith, and foam pressure-drop equations.

Breakthrough models (§17–18) — Logistic (BA/Thomas/YN unified), Clark, modified dose-response, Wolborska (corrected), Klinkenberg, Dima, Chern–Chien (both forms), Gompertz, Log-Gompertz, Weibull, Avrami, Gudermannian, error-function, tanh, log-normal, parallel sigmoidal, all three log-modified forms, and all four fractal-like variants.

Parametric equations (§20) — Complete μmaxμmax​, λλ, titi​, t50t50​ for every model (Hu, Xie & Zhang 2020), and modified μmax/λμmax​/λ breakthrough forms.

Travelling wave solutions (§21) — All five Myers et al. forms (linear, nonlinear, Sips), full BA appendix solution, exhaustion time estimate.

Multicomponent models (§22) — Logistic and Gompertz superposition forms + equilibrium loading integrals.

Error statistics (§23) — R2R2, Adj.R2R2, RMSE, χ2χ2, AAD, SSE, FF-test, AIC, Akaike weight.
